Adiabatic Approximation of Abelian Higgs models
Amirmasoud Geevechi, Robert L. Jerrard

Abstract
. We construct novel solutions in d ≥ 3 d\geq 3 space dimensions of a family of nonlinear evolutions equations that includes the critical hyperbolic Abelian Higgs model (AHM). For the AHM, these solutions exhibit an ensemble of N ≥ 1 N\geq 1 slowly-moving, nearly parallel vortex filaments, whose leading-order dynamics are described by a wave map from ℝ d − 2 {\mathbb{R}}^{d-2} into the Abelian Higgs moduli space, a manifold carrying a natural Riemannian structure that parametrizes stationary 2d solutions of the AHM. We also prove extremely similar results that relate the critical Abelian Higgs heat flow, modeling certain superconductors, to the harmonic map heat flow into the Moduli space, as well as some parallel results for near-critical equations. When d = 3 d=3 , these results allow for the study of the poorly-understood phenomenon of vortex reconnection in this setting.



1.
Introduction

In this paper we construct novel solutions of a family of evolution equations arising in gauge theory in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
for
d\geq 3
. In all the equations we consider, the unknowns are a complex-valued function
\Phi
and a (real valued)
1
-form
A=A_{1}dx_{1}+\ldots+A_{d}dx_{d}+A_{0}dt
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
which is used to form a covariant derivative

D_{\alpha}=\partial_{\alpha}-iA_{\alpha},\qquad\mbox{ where $\partial_{\alpha}$ denotes }\begin{cases}\partial_{t}&\mbox{ if }\alpha=0\\
\partial_{x_{\alpha}}&\mbox{if }\alpha\in\{1,\ldots,d\}.\end{cases}

We will often tacitly identify
A
with a map taking values in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
.

We are chiefly interested in two related equations.
The first of these is the Abelian Higgs model:

\left.\begin{aligned} D_{0}D_{0}\Phi-D_{k}D_{k}\Phi+\frac{\lambda}{2}(|\Phi|^{2}-1)\Phi&=0\\
\partial_{0}F_{0\alpha}-\partial_{k}F_{k\alpha}-(i\Phi,D_{\alpha}\Phi)&=0,\qquad\alpha=0,\ldots,d.\end{aligned}\ \ \right\}

(1.1)

where
\lambda>0
is a real parameter, the repeated index
k
is summed from
1
to
d
, and we use the notation

F_{\alpha\beta}=\partial_{\alpha}A_{\beta}-\partial_{\beta}A_{\alpha},

and

\mbox{ for $v,w\in{\mathbb{C}}$,}\qquad(v,w):=\frac{1}{2}(v\bar{w}+w\bar{v}).

This is a nonlinear hyperbolic equation arising in high energy physics, as perhaps the simplest instance of a Yang-Mills-Higgs theory.
We will also study its parabolic counterpart, the Abelian Higgs heat flow:

\left.\begin{aligned} D_{0}\Phi-D_{k}D_{k}\Phi+\frac{\lambda}{2}(|\Phi|^{2}-1)\Phi&=0\\
F_{0j}-\partial_{k}F_{kj}-(i\Phi,D_{j}\Phi)&=0,\qquad j=1,\ldots,d.\end{aligned}\ \ \right\}

(1.2)

This arises as a description of the evolution of superconductors in Ginzburg-Landau theory.

Both (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) are invariant with respect to gauge transformations

\Phi\mapsto e^{i\chi}\Phi,\qquad A\mapsto A+d\chi

(1.3)

where
\chi
is a sufficiently smooth function with the same domain as
(\Phi,A)
, in the sense that
(\Phi,A)
is a solution of (
1
) if and only if
(e^{i\chi}\Phi,A+d\chi)
is a solution. As with all gauge theories, this has important consequence for the analysis of the equations. For example, it is not possible to prove uniqueness or to estimate any quantities that are not gauge-invariant without fixing the degree of freedom represented by (
1.3
).

For both (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) it is known that for suitable initial data, solutions possess vortex submanifolds — codimension
2
concentration sets of the vorticity and energy -- whose dynamics is governed in a suitable limit by a geometric evolution equation: the timelike Minkowskian minimal surface equation
1
1

1

this is only proved when
d=3
but is expected to be valid in all higher dimensions as well.
for (
1.1
), see
[
4
]
, and the mean curvature flow for (
1.2
), see
[
23
]
. In this paper we study
the dynamics of vortex filaments in a regime that differs from those considered in
[
4
]
,
[
23
]
, in which we are able to resolve the fine structure of multiple interacting filaments.

Our starting point is a classification of solutions
(\Phi,A_{a}):{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
of the critical 2d system

\left.\begin{aligned} -D_{a}D_{a}\Phi+\frac{1}{2}(|\Phi|^{2}-1)\Phi&=0\\
\-\partial_{a}F_{ab}-(i\Phi,D_{b}\Phi)&=0\end{aligned}\ \ \right\}

(1.4)

where indices
a,b,...
run from 1 to 2 and repeated indices are implicity summed. In Section
2
we recall in detail this classification and other relevant background. Briefly, one can associate an integer
N
, called the
vortex number
, to every solution of (
1.4
), indeed to every finite-energy pair
(\Phi,A_{a})
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. The space of finite-energy solutions
2
2

2

More precisely, gauge equivalnce classes of finite-energy solutions, see (
2.10
).
with vortex number
N
is parametrized by a Riemannian manifold called the
N
-vortex moduli space, denoted
(M_{N},g)
, that we describe in Section
2.2
. Given a point
q\in M_{N}
, we will write

\mathfrak{u}(x;q)=\binom{\varphi(x;q)}{\mathcal{A}_{a}(x;q)}
to denote the corresponding solution. The
vortex centers
associated to
\mathfrak{u}(\cdot;q)

are exactly the zeroes of
\varphi(\cdot;q)
. For
q\in M_{N}
, there are exactly
N
vortex centers, counting multiplicity.

The construction and classification of solutions of (
1.4
) was completed by Jaffe and Taubes
[
12
]
in 1980. Shortly thereafter, Manton
[
16
]
proposed that when
\lambda=1
, low-energy solutions of the Abelian Higgs model (
1.1
) in
2
space dimensions could be of the form

\binom{\Phi}{A_{a}}_{a=1,2}(x_{1},x_{2},t)\approx\mathfrak{u}(x_{1},x_{2};q_{\varepsilon}(t))

(1.5)

where
q_{\varepsilon}(t)=q(\varepsilon t)
and
t\mapsto q(t)\in M_{N}
is a geodesic in
(M_{N},g)
, that is, a solution of

\nabla^{g}_{t}\partial_{t}q=0,\qquad\mbox{where $\nabla^{g}$ is the Levi-Civita connection on $(M,g)$}.

Stuart
[
27
]
gave a rigorous proof of this scenario for vortex number
N=2
. In fact his results also cover the near-critical case and
\lambda=1+\iota\varepsilon^{2}
for
\iota\in{\mathbb{R}}
, in which case the geodesic flow is modified by adding a potential reflecting inter-vortex forces, not present when
\lambda=1
, resulting in

\nabla^{g}_{t}\partial_{t}q+\iota\nabla_{g}V_{0}(q)=0,\qquad V_{0}(q):=\frac{1}{8}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}(|\varphi(q)|^{2}-1)^{2},

(1.6)

where
\nabla_{g}
denotes the gradient with respect to the Riemannian structure on
(M,g)
.
Later work of Palvelev
[
21
]
, see also
[
22
,
20
]
, extended Stuart’s work in the critical case to cover arbitrary vortex number
N
.

For the Abelian Higgs model (
1.1
) with
\lambda=1
, in our main theorem we fix a vortex number
N\geq 1
, and we construct solutions in
d\geq 3
space dimensions such that for every
(x_{3},\ldots,x_{d},t)=(x_{\bar{a}},t)

\binom{\Phi}{A_{a}}(\cdot,x_{\bar{a}},t)\approx\mathfrak{u}(\cdot,q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t)),

(1.7)

where
3
3

3

Here and below,
A_{a}
denotes
(A_{a})_{a=1,2}
and
x_{\bar{a}}
denotes
(x_{\bar{a}})_{\bar{a}=3}^{d}
. In addition, we implicitly sum over repeated indices.

q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t)=q(\varepsilon x_{\bar{a}},\varepsilon t)
and
(x_{\bar{a}},t)\mapsto q(x_{\bar{a}},t)\in M_{N}
is a wave map into
(M_{N},g)
, that is, a solution of

\nabla^{g}_{t}\partial_{t}q-\nabla^{g}_{\bar{a}}\partial_{\bar{a}}q=0.

Our results also apply to the parabolic equation (
1.2
), with
q
solving the harmonic map heat flow rather than the wave map equation.

Thus, in both (
1.1
) and (
1.2
), when
d=3
, at every time
t
and height
x_{3}
the
restriction to the horizontal slice
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times\{(x_{3},t)\}
of the
(\Phi,A_{1},A_{2})
components of the solution that we construct is close to a stationary
2d
solution, and the moduli space parameters that characterize these 2d slices are slowly-varying functions of
(x_{3},t)
. Fixing
t
, the
N
(not necessarily distinct) vortex centers on the
2d
slices at height
x_{3}
, determined by the parameters
q(x_{3},t)
, trace out
N
curves as
x_{3}
varies. As
t
varies, this ensemble of
N
curves evolves in a way governed by the appropriate geometric evolution equation into
(M_{N},g)
.

The statement and proof of our main theorem for the hyperbolic (
1.1
) and parabolic (
1.2
) equations are extremely similar. In order to give a unified treatment, and because the added generality may be of interest, we embed both of these equations
4
4

4

The Abelian Higgs heat flow as normally written does not include the equation
-\partial_{k}F_{k0}-(i\Phi,D_{0}\Phi)=0
, see (
1.2
). In fact, when
\kappa_{1}>\kappa_{0}=0
, this equation is a consequence of the other equations, as follows from Lemma
1
below. Thus, in the pure parabolic case it is redundant but not incorrect to include it in the system (
1
).

in a larger family:

\left.\begin{aligned} (\kappa_{0}D_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})D_{0}\Phi-D_{j}D_{j}\Phi+\frac{\lambda_{\varepsilon}}{2}(|\Phi|^{2}-1)\Phi&=0\\
(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})F_{0j}-\partial_{k}F_{kj}-(i\Phi,D_{j}\Phi)&=0,\qquad j=1,\ldots,d\\
-\partial_{k}F_{k0}-(i\Phi,D_{0}\Phi)&=0\end{aligned}\ \ \right\}

where

0\leq\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1}\leq 1,\qquad\kappa_{1}>0\mbox{ if }\kappa_{0}=0,\qquad\lambda_{\varepsilon}=1+\iota\varepsilon^{2}\mbox{ for some }\iota\in{\mathbb{R}}.

The most interesting cases
are
\kappa_{0}=1,\kappa_{1}=0
and
\kappa_{0}=0,\kappa_{1}=1
. The upper bound
\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1}\leq 1
can always be achieved by rescaling in the
t
variable.

The scaling in (
1
), in particular the factor of
\varepsilon
multiplying
\kappa_{1}D_{0}\Phi
, is dictated by the ansatz (
1.7
).
If
\kappa_{0}=0
then one can rescale in the
t
variable to bring the equation into the form (
1.2
), corresponding to a parabolic rescaling
q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t)=q(\varepsilon x_{\bar{a}},\varepsilon^{2}t)
.

In our first main result, assuming
\lambda=1
, we construct a solution of (
1
) satisfying (
1.7
), where
q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t)=q(\varepsilon x_{\bar{a}},\varepsilon t)
for
q:{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times(0,T)\to M_{N}
solving the geometric evolution equation

\kappa_{0}\nabla^{g}_{t}\partial_{t}q+\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}q-\nabla^{g}_{y_{\bar{a}}}\partial_{\bar{a}}q=0.

(1.8)

This is the wave map equation when
0=\kappa_{1}<\kappa_{0}
and the harmonic map heat flow when
0=\kappa_{0}<\kappa_{1}
. We start from a solution of (
1.8
) that we will always assume satisfies

\mbox{ $\exists$ compact }K\subset M_{N}\mbox{ such that }q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})\in K\mbox{ for all }(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times(0,T).

(1.9)

As we discuss in Section
2
,
M_{N}
is canonically isomorphic to
{\mathbb{C}}^{N}\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}
, and in the canonical coordinates, which we always use, the metric is uniformly comparable to the Eucildean metric on every compact
K\subset M_{N}
. For purposes of defining Sobolev spaces of maps into
M_{N}
, once
K
in (
1.9
) is fixed, it is thus consistent with the geometry of
(M_{N},g)
to treat maps into
K\subset M_{N}
as maps into
{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}
, with the Euclidean connection. We will always do this.
We assume that the solution
q
of (
1.8
) satisfies

\partial_{\bar{a}}q\in C(0,T;H^{L}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})),\qquad\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}q\in C(0,T;H^{L}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})).

(1.10)

for some
L
to be specified below. The assumption about
\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}q
is of course
vacuous if
\kappa_{0}=0
.
We will write

\Theta:=\|\partial_{\bar{a}}q\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{L}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{0}q\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{L}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}

(1.11)

where, if
\tau>0
and
\|\cdot\|_{X}
is a norm on a space of functions,

\|v\|_{L^{\infty}_{\tau}X}=\|v\|_{L^{\infty}(0,\tau;X)}:=\operatorname{ess\,sup}_{0<t<\tau}\|v(\cdot,t)\|_{X}.

(1.12)

We will always assume
T
is chosen so that
\Theta<\infty
.

Our main result may be summarized as follows:

Theorem 1
.

Let
q:{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times(0,T)\to M_{N}
be a solution of (
1.8
) satisfying (
1.9
) and (
1.10
) for
L=2d+11
,
and assume that
\Theta<\infty
.
Then there exists
T_{0}\in(0,T)
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and, for
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, a solution
U_{\varepsilon}=(\Phi_{\varepsilon},A_{\varepsilon a},A_{\varepsilon\bar{a}},A_{\varepsilon 0})
of (
1
) for
\lambda=1
such that

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{\varepsilon}-\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}(\Phi_{\varepsilon}-\varphi(q_{\varepsilon}))\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2},

(1.13)

\displaystyle\|A_{\varepsilon a}-\mathcal{A}_{a}(q_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}(A_{\varepsilon a}-\mathcal{A}_{a}(q_{\varepsilon}))\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}

\displaystyle\|A_{\varepsilon\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}A_{\varepsilon\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon

for
a=1,2
and
\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d
, where
q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t)=q(\varepsilon x_{\bar{a}},\varepsilon t)
and

\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})(x,t):=\varphi(x_{a};q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t)),\qquad\mathcal{A}_{a}(q_{\varepsilon})(x,t):=\mathcal{A}_{a}(x_{a};q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t)).

(1.14)

A lower bound for
T_{0}
can be estimated from
K
and
\Theta
, and upper bounds for the other constants depend only on
K,\Theta
and
T_{0}
.

For
\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d
, the
1
-form coefficients
A_{\varepsilon\alpha}
have the form
A_{\varepsilon\alpha}=\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}[q_{\varepsilon}]+O(\varepsilon^{3})
, where the leading-order term
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}[q_{\varepsilon}]
encodes aspects of the geometry of
(M_{N},g)
and the map
q
, see (
3.2
) and (
3.4
).

For
d=3
and
\kappa_{1}=0
, a version of Theorem
1
was first proved in the PhD thesis
[
9
]
of the first author, and our proof follows the ideas developed there very closely.

Our proof also yields, in addition to (
1.13
), estimates in Sobolev norms such as
L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}H^{\frac{d}{2}+2}
, and
L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}H^{\frac{d}{2}+1}
for the time derivatives. These can easily be extracted from (
6.7
), (
4.4
), and (
6.2
).

Remark 1
.

In Section
6
we restate the theorem with more details. Although here we have only asserted the existence of a single solution satisfying (
1.13
), the full version of the theorem shows that the initial data implicit in Theorem
1
as formulated above can be perturbed somewhat while preserving the conclusions of the theorem.
That is, if we write
(U_{0},U_{1}):=(U_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,0),\partial_{t}U_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,o))
for the solution
U_{\varepsilon}
constructed in Theorem
1
, and if we seek a solution
V_{\varepsilon}
of (
1
) with initial data satisfying

\|V_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,0)-U_{1}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}V_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,0)-U_{0}\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq c_{0}\varepsilon^{2}

for a suitably small
c_{0}
, and if an additional orthogonality condition is satisfied (see (
5.3
)), then (
1.13
) still holds with the same
T_{0}
, and the constants
C
all multiplied by
2
.

The orthogonality condition (
5.3
) says, heuristically, that the initial data for
q
gives the initial vortex curve location and velocity that best approximates the vortex structure in the initial data for (
1
).

Remark 2
.

The proof is carried out by first constructing an approximate solution
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times(0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
, then finding the actual solution as a perturbation of
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}
. The approximate solution is constructed iteratively, by a rigorous asymptotic expansion. One step in the expansion requires solving a nonlinear evolution equation. The time
T_{0}\leq T
arises because the solution of this equation, and hence the approximate solution, may lose regularity or cease to exist before time
T/\varepsilon
. If the approximate solution exists and is smooth enough
only
up to time
T_{1}/\epsilon
, then any
T_{0}\in(0,T_{1})
is an acceptable choice for the theorem, with the other constants diverging as
T_{0}\nearrow T_{1}
.

In the physical case
d=3
, global smooth solutions of (
1.8
) from
{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}={\mathbb{R}}
into any smooth manifold exist for smooth data, and hence
T
may be taken arbitrarily large.

Remark 3
.

In our iterative construction of an approximate solution, the leading terms inherit
H^{L}_{loc}
regularity from
q
, and at each stage of the construction, some regularity is lost. At the final stage we use a fixed-point argument to prove existence of a perturbation
\tilde{U}

such that
U^{\varepsilon}+\tilde{U}
solves (
1
). This fixed-point argument needs to be carried out in a rather smooth space – we use the space of maps
\tilde{U}
such that
(\tilde{U},\partial_{t}\tilde{U})\in L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}H^{2\ell+1}\times L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}H^{2\ell}
for
\ell
such that
2\ell>\frac{d}{2}+1
– so we need to begin with a large number
L
of derivatives.

In the statement of the theorem, for simplicity we have chosen a value of
L
that works in all dimensions but is sometimes larger than necessary. We also have not made a great effort to keep
L
as small as possible, and some of our arguments could be sharpened to allow for a smaller
L
.

In near-critical regime
\lambda=1+\iota\varepsilon^{2}
, the equation governing
q
is expected to be modified by the addition of a potential:

\kappa_{0}\nabla^{g}_{t}\partial_{t}q+\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}q-\nabla^{g}_{y_{\bar{a}}}\partial_{\bar{a}}q+\iota\nabla_{g}V_{0}(q)=0

(1.15)

for
V_{0}
as in (
1.6
). Theorem
1
as stated does not extend to this case, as the assumption
\Theta<\infty
, used throughout our proof, is
generally incompatible with equation (
1.15
), at least when
\kappa_{0}>0
. For example, if the initial data for (
1.15
) is independent
of
y_{\bar{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}
, the solution will have the form
q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})=q_{0}(y_{0})
, where
q_{0}
solves
the ODE obtained by setting
\partial_{\bar{a}}q=0
in (
1.15
). In this case
\Theta=+\infty
whenever it is nonzero, if
\kappa_{0}>0
.

It would be interesting for this and other reasons to relax the assumption in Theorem
1
that
\|\partial_{0}q\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{L}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}<\infty
.

However, the following variant of Theorem
1
holds in the near-critical case. In the statement we use the notation

{\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R}:={\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}/R{\mathbb{Z}}^{d-2}
.

Theorem 2
.

Fix
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
,
\iota\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
R>0
, and let
q:{\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R}\times(0,T)\to M_{N}
be a solution of (
1.15
) such that

\mbox{ $\exists$ compact }K\subset M_{N}\mbox{ such that }q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})\in K\mbox{ for all }(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})\in{\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R}\times(0,T)

(1.16)

and

\Theta:=\|\partial_{\bar{a}}q(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{L}({\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{0}q(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{L}({\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R})}<\infty

(1.17)

for
L=2d+11
.
Then there exists
T_{0}\in(0,T)
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and, for
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, a solution
U_{\varepsilon}=(\Phi_{\varepsilon},A_{\varepsilon a},A_{\varepsilon\bar{a}},A_{\varepsilon 0})
of (
1
) for
\lambda=1+\iota\varepsilon^{2}
such that

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{\varepsilon}-\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R/\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2},

\displaystyle\|A_{\varepsilon a}-\mathcal{A}_{a}(q_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R/\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2},\qquad a=1,2

\displaystyle\|A_{\varepsilon\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R/\varepsilon})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon,\qquad\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d.

A lower bound for
T_{0}
can be estimated from
K
and
\Theta
, and upper bounds for the other constants depend only on
K,\Theta
and
T_{0}
.

1.1.
Physical consequences

As noted above, earlier works
[
4
,
23
]
characterize the evolution of vortex filaments in solutions of (
1.1
), (
1.2
). In both cases these evolutions can lead to collisions of vortex filaments in 3 space dimensions, and it is natural to ask what the outcome of such a collision might be.
One possibility is that the filaments might simply pass through each other with minimal interaction. Another is that each filament might split into two halves at the moment of collision, with the connectivity of the two halves of the filaments switching.

Figure 1.
filament collision and reconnection

This second scenario, pictured in Figure
1
, is known as
reconnection
5
5

5

also sometimes called intercommutation, recombination, or vortex cutting.
, and it is believed, based on physical arguments and extensive experimental and numerical evidence, to occur very widely in both quantum
and classical fluids, see for example
[
15
]
,
[
1
]
and
[
19
]
,
[
13
]
,
[
24
]
,
[
14
]
respectively. In particular, empirical evidence for the ubiquity of reconnection in fluids such as liquid helium, air, and water is abundant and convincing, though rigorous proof is completely lacking. Collisions of vortex filaments in the Abelian Higgs model (
1.1
) have been intensively studied in the physics community, motivated by models of cosmic strings and their possible cosmological signatures, and here too the evidence points to the inevitability of reconnection, at least for collisions at subrelativistic velocities, see for example
[
26
]
,
[
17
]
,
[
11
]
. Similarly, numerical studies, see for example
[
2
]
,
[
10
]
, suggest that reconnection should be the generic outcome of vortex filament collisions in superconductors, as modelled by (
1.2
) and related equations.

Prior mathematical work on (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) does not yield any information as to whether vortex filament collisions result in reconnection. The only rigorous work of which we are aware on vortex dynamics in (
1.1
) (see
[
4
]
) ceases to hold before the first vortex collision, and the strongest results we know of on vortices in (
1.2
) (see
[
23
]
) yield a description of vortex filament dynamics that holds globally in time and allows for collisions, but loses uniqueness when collisions occur and is compatible with either reconnection or its nonoccurence.

Our main result about reconnection is a straightforward corollary of Theorems
1
and
2
, together with standard
facts about the geometry of the moduli space
M_{N}
. The natural setting is
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
, and for simplicity we consider solutions of (
1
) with
2
vortex filaments, as illustrated in Figure
1
, corresponding to maps into the
N=2
Moduli space
M_{2}
. In stating the theorem below we use the fact, reviewed in Section
2.1
below, that
M_{2}\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
, and we use a particular global choice of coordinates for
M_{2}
described in Theorem
4
.

Theorem 3
.

Assume that
d=3
. Let
q=(q_{1},q_{2}):{\mathbb{R}}\times(0,T)\to M_{2}\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
be a solution of (
1.8
) satisfying (
1.9
) and (
1.10
) with
\Theta<\infty
, and let
U_{\varepsilon}:{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\times(0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3+1}
be the solution of (
1
) with
\lambda=1
constructed in Theorem
1
.
Further assume that there exists
(\bar{x}_{3},\bar{t})\in{\mathbb{R}}\times(0,T_{0})
such that
D=q_{1}^{2}-4q_{2}
satisfies

D(\bar{x}_{3},\bar{t})=0,\qquad\{\partial_{t}D(\bar{x}_{3},\bar{t}),\partial_{3}D(\bar{x}_{3},\bar{t})\}\mbox{ are linearly independent over ${\mathbb{R}}$}.

(1.18)

Then for all
\delta>0
, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that the vortex filaments of
U_{\varepsilon}
reconnect in

\{(x_{3},t):|x_{3}-\bar{x}_{3}|<\frac{\delta}{\varepsilon},\ |t-\bar{t}|<\frac{\delta}{\varepsilon}\}\qquad\mbox{ if }0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}.

The same result holds in the setting of Theorem 2.

The proof consists in large part of a discussion intended to clarify what we mean by reconnection.

Remark 4
.

Although we require
(q_{x_{3}},\kappa_{0}q_{t})|_{t=0}\in H^{12}\times H^{12}
when
d=3
(see (
4.1
)), condition (
1.18
) is open with respect to topologies such as
(q,\kappa_{0}\partial_{t}q)|_{t=0}\in H^{2}\times H^{1}
, in the sense that a small
H^{2}\times H^{1}
perturbation of the initial data will lead to the existence of a perturbed point
(\tilde{x}_{3},\tilde{t})
such that
|(\tilde{x}_{3},\tilde{t})-(\bar{x}_{3},\bar{t})|
is small and (
1.18
) holds at
(\tilde{x}_{3},\tilde{t})
.

Moreover, as noted in Remark
1
, the estimates of Theorem
1
are stable with respect to perturbations of order
\varepsilon^{2}
in
H^{5}\times H^{4}
, as long as they satisfy compatibility and gauge conditions appearing in (
4.10
) and an orthogonality condition appearing in
(
5.3
). This last condition says in effect that the perturbations leave
(q,\kappa_{0}\partial_{t}q)|_{t=0}
invariant.

Theorems
3
and
1
thus provide some stability for the phenomenon of reconnection with respect to perturbations, albeit rather smooth ones.

We know of only a few other works that give rigorous treatments of issues connected to reconnection. A reduced model for a single vortex filament reconnecting with a domain boundary is derived formally in
[
3
]
and rigorously analyzed in
[
18
]
. Solutions of the Navier-Stokes and Gross-Pitaevskii equations with reconnecting vortex filaments are constructed in
[
7
]
,
[
8
]
.

1.2.
Notation

If it is unclear which connection
1
-form is used to form a covariant derivatives, we will explicitly write the
1
-form as a subscript, rather than simply indicating the index. For example, when considering a perturbation
A+\varepsilon^{2}\tilde{A}
of a
1
-form
A
, we may write

D_{A_{a}+\varepsilon^{2}\tilde{A}_{a}}\Phi=D_{A_{a}}-i\varepsilon^{2}\tilde{A}_{a}\Phi

We implicitly sum over repeated indices.
We do not raise and lower indices with a metric tensor, and we do not insist that
lower indices be paired with upper indices in implicit sums.
We impose the conventions that

•

indices
i,j,k
run from
1
to
d
. These correspond to all spatial directions. They will appear infrequently.

•

once we fix a vortex number
N
and consider maps into
M_{N}
or perturbations thereof (see Section
2.2
below), indices
\mu,\nu,\ldots
are understood to run from
1
to
2N
, the dimension of
M_{N}
.

•

indices
a,b,\ldots
run from
1
to
2
. These may be thought of as corresponding to “horizontal” directions, roughly orthogonal to the vortex filaments.

•

indices
\bar{a},\bar{b},\ldots
run from
3
to
d
.

We may sometimes explicitly indicate sums, as a reminder of the range over which the sum runs.

We will often write
U
to denote an element of
{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
or a function taking values in
{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
,
with components typically written

U=\begin{pmatrix}\Phi\\
A\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\Phi\\
A_{a}\\
A_{\bar{a}}\\
A_{0}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mbox{ also written as }\quad U=\begin{pmatrix}u\\
A_{\bar{a}}\\
A_{0}\end{pmatrix},\qquad\mbox{ where }u=\binom{\Phi}{A_{a}}.

Here
A_{a}
denotes
(A_{a})_{a=1,2}
and
A_{\bar{a}}
denotes
(A_{\bar{a}})_{\bar{a}=3,\ldots L,d}
.

Given a map
U:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times(0,T)\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
, we will write

\begin{aligned} S_{\phi}[U]&:=(\kappa_{0}D_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})D_{0}\Phi-D_{j}D_{j}\Phi+\frac{1}{2}(|\Phi|^{2}-1)\Phi\\
S_{j}[U]&:=(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})F_{0j}-\partial_{k}F_{kj}-(i\Phi,D_{j}\Phi),\qquad j=1,\ldots,d\\
S_{0}[U]&:=-\partial_{k}F_{k0}-(i\Phi,D_{0}\Phi)\end{aligned}\

(1.19)

and

S[U]=\begin{pmatrix}S_{\phi}[U]\\
S_{a}[U]\\
S_{\bar{a}}[U]\\
S_{0}[U]\end{pmatrix}\ =\ \begin{pmatrix}S_{u}[U]\\
S_{\bar{a}}[U]\\
S_{0}[U]\end{pmatrix},\quad\mbox{ with }S_{u}[U]=\binom{S_{\phi}[U]}{S_{a}[U]}.

(1.20)

With this notation, (
1
) can be written concisely as

S[U]=0.

We will often encounter functions with exponential decay in the
x_{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
directions. For these we will use the weighted norm

\|f\|_{W^{k,\infty}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}:=\sum_{|r|\leq k}\sup_{y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\partial^{r}f(y)|e^{\gamma|x_{a}|}

(1.21)

where the sum is over multiindices
r=(r_{1},\ldots,r_{d})
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
.
It is clear that if
\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}\geq\gamma
, then

\|fg\|_{W^{k,\infty}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C_{k}\|f\|_{W^{k,\infty}_{\gamma_{1}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\|g\|_{W^{k,\infty}_{\gamma_{2}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

In particular
W^{k,\infty}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
is an algebra for
\gamma\geq 0
.

For
k\geq j\geq 0
we will write
X^{k,j}_{\gamma}
to denote
H^{k}\cap W^{k-j,\infty}_{\gamma}
, which has the natural norm

\|\psi\|_{X^{k,j}_{\gamma}}:=\|\psi\|_{H^{k}}+\|\psi\|_{W^{k-j,\infty}_{\gamma}}.

(1.22)

1.3.
outline of the paper, and elements of the proof

Our overall strategy is similar to the one used in
[
5
]
for a semilinear scalar wave equation.
We seek a solution of the form

U_{\varepsilon}=U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}
,
where
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}
is an approximate solution that we construct by a rigorous asymptotic expansion, and
\tilde{U}
solves a perturbation equation.
Thus we aim to solve
S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}]=0
. By expanding the differential operator
S[\,\cdot\,]
around
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}
and rearranging, we rewrite the equation in the form

S^{\prime}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})=-S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]-{\mathcal{N}}_{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})

(1.23)

where
S^{\prime}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]
is a linear differential operator, displayed in (
4.25
), with coefficients that depend on
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}
, and
{\mathcal{N}}_{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})
is a remainder in which we collect term that are nonlinear in
\tilde{U}
.

To carry this out, we start in Section
2
by reviewing background about the 2d Abelian Higgs model.
In Section
3
we construct the approximate solution
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}
, starting from a solution
q
of (
1.8
). We require it to have several properties:

•

The
(\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},{A_{\varepsilon a}^{ap}})
components must be close to
(\varphi(q_{\varepsilon}),\mathcal{A}_{a}(q_{\varepsilon}))
as defined in (
1.14
).

•

The error term
S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]
, as defined in (
1.19
), must be small – this is what it means for
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}
to be an approximate solution.

•

The differential operator
S^{\prime}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]
needs certain good properties. Some of these follow from the closeness of

(\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},{A_{\varepsilon a}^{ap}})
to
(\varphi(q_{\varepsilon}),\mathcal{A}_{a}(q_{\varepsilon}))
, but we also require that
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}
be sufficiently smooth.

In Section
4
we rewrite (
1.23
) in a form more suitable for estimates, which end up being

(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}^{2}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{0})\tilde{U}+{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{U}+P\tilde{U}=-{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{U})-S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]

(1.24)

where
{\mathcal{M}}={\mathcal{M}}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]
is a second-order elliptic operator and
P
is a first-order operator with small coefficients.

In Section
5
we establish estimates for a linear model of (
1.23
):

(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}^{2}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{0})\tilde{U}+{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{U}+P\tilde{U}=\eta

where we regard the right-hand side as given.

In Section
6
we use the linear estimates of Section
5
in a contraction mapping argument to prove existence and estimates of
\tilde{U}
solving (
1.23
), thereby completing the proofs of Theorems
1
and
2
.
This argument determines the degree of smoothness needed in the linear estimates of Section
5
, and hence the smoothness required for the approximate solution
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}
, which appears in the coefficients of the modified system (
1.24
). It also imposes constraints on the smallness we require of
S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]
.

Section
7
contains the proof of Theorem
3
, and the proofs of various auxiliary facts, such as linear estimates used in the construction of
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}
, are presented in an appendix.

Gauge symmetry (
1.3
) gives rise to analytical issues at several stages in our argument. For example, (
1
) as rewritten in (
1.23
) retains its gauge symmetry. This has well-known unpleasant analytic consequences, including non-uniqueness of solutions and the impossibility of estimating any quantity that is not gauge-invariant. In passing from (
1.23
) to (
1.24
),
we modify the equation in a way that enforces a choice of gauge, guarantees uniqueness of solutions and facilitates estimates. The gauge we use, see (
4.6
), may be described as a perturbative Lorenz gauge in the hyperbolic case and a perturbative caloric gauge in the parabolic case. In the general cases it contains elements of both. We have not seen it in the prior literature.

The necessity of fixing a gauge also arises implicitly in the construction of
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}
; it can be seen in Lemmas
10
and
12
, as well as conclusion (
A.22
) of Proposition
7
. The gauge we use in this construction, which may be described as a perturbative horizontal Coulomb gauge, differs from that built into the modified system (
1.24
).

A key subtlety arises in the linear estimates of Section
5
, due to the failure of the quadratic form associated to the operator
{\mathcal{M}}
in (
1.24
) to fully control the
L^{2}
norm of mappings on which it operates. This necessitates estimates of a projection of
\tilde{U}
onto the poorly-controlled part, which we write
as

\sum_{\mu}c_{\mu}n_{\mu}=\sum_{\mu}c_{\mu}(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})n_{\mu}(y_{a};q_{\varepsilon}(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))\ ,

where
n_{\mu}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times M_{N}\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
for
\mu=1,\ldots,2N
are mappings introduced in Section
2
and
(c_{\mu}):{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}
are coefficients arising from the projection of
\tilde{U}
.
The most delicate part of the linear estimates involves controlling norms of
c_{\mu}
. These functions satisfy a wave equation
of the form

(\kappa_{0}\partial_{tt}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}-\Delta_{\bar{a}})c_{\mu}=H

where, roughly speaking,
H=O(\varepsilon)
. It follows from this that

\|c_{\mu}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}\approx t^{3}\int_{0}^{t}\|H(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,dt\approx t^{4}\varepsilon^{2}

which is too weak to control solutions for times of order
1/\varepsilon
, as we require. The same issue appears when trying to control higher derivatives. We address this problem by carefully exploiting the structure of the operator
P
in (
1.24
) to rewrite the equation satisfied by
c_{\mu}
in the form

(\kappa_{0}\partial_{tt}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}-\Delta_{\bar{a}})c_{\mu}=\partial_{0}h_{0}+\nabla_{\bar{a}}\cdot h_{\bar{a}}+h

where now
h=O(\varepsilon^{2})
. This and the divergence form of the other terms allow us to establish estimates showing that
c_{\mu}
remains small for times of order
1/\varepsilon
, as needed. These arguments appear in Section
5.2
.

Our strategy differs from that employed by Stuart
[
27
]
, who employs a modulation argument to obtain an exact splitting of a solution of (
1.1
) into a part in the moduli space and an orthogonal part. He then estimates the growth of the orthogonal part. We believe that this would be very hard to carry out in
3
or more space dimensions due to loss of derivatives in the modulation equation. Nonetheless, our work relies heavily on the foundational ideas developed in
[
27
]
.

1.4.
A useful lemma

We conclude this introduction with a lemma that is used in enforcing the choice of gauge built into the modified system (
1.24
).
It also shows that, as noted above,
the equation
S_{0}[U]=0
is redundant but not inconsistent in the parabolic case.

Lemma 1
.

For every sufficiently smooth
U=\binom{\Phi}{A}
,

(S_{\phi}[U],i\Phi)=-(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})S_{0}[U]+\partial_{j}S_{j}[U]

(1.25)

where
j
is summed from
1
to
d
.
In particular this holds for
U
of the form

U=U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}
, where

U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}\in L^{\infty}_{T}W^{2\ell+1,\infty}\mbox{ with }\partial_{t}U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}\in L^{\infty}_{T}W^{2\ell,\infty}\mbox{ and }\partial_{tt}U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}\in L^{\infty}_{T}W^{2\ell-1,\infty}

(1.26)

and

\tilde{U}\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{2\ell+1}\mbox{ with }\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{2\ell}\mbox{ and }\partial_{tt}\tilde{U}\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{2\ell-1}.

(1.27)

with
2\ell>d/2
.

We emphasize that (
1.25
) is an algebraic identity that does not rely on
U
solving any equation.

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle(S_{\phi}[U],i\Phi)

\displaystyle=((\kappa_{0}D_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})D_{0}\Phi,i\Phi)-(D_{j}D_{j}\Phi,i\Phi)

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})(D_{0}\Phi,i\Phi)-\partial_{j}(D_{j}\Phi,i\Phi).

On the other hand, the antisymmetry of
F_{jk}
implies that

\partial_{j}S_{j}[U]=-\partial_{j}(i\Phi,D_{j}\Phi)+(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})\partial_{j}F_{0j},

and it is clear that

-(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})S_{0}[U]=(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})(i\Phi,D_{0}\Phi)+(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})\partial_{j}F_{j0}.

The lemma follows directly from these three identities, since
F_{j0}=-F_{0j}
.

It is straightforward to check that (
1.26
), (
1.27
) sufficient to justify the computations above.
∎

The above lemma has the following consequence, which we will use later to fix the gauge in the perturbation equation (
1.23
).

Lemma 2
.

Assume that
U=\binom{\Phi}{A}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
and
Z:{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}\to{\mathbb{R}}
are sufficiently smooth solutions of the equations

\left.\begin{aligned} S_{\phi}[U]&=-i\Phi Z\\
S_{j}[U]&=-\partial_{j}Z\\
S_{0}[U]&=-\partial_{0}Z\end{aligned}\ \ \right\}

(1.28)

Then
Z
solves

(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})\partial_{0}Z-\Delta Z+|\Phi|^{2}Z=0.

(1.29)

In particular this holds if
U
has the form

U=U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}
for
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}
and
\tilde{U}
satisfying (
1.26
), (
1.27
), and if

Z\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{2\ell}\quad\mbox{ with }\ \ Z_{t}\in L^{\infty}_{T}H^{2\ell-1}

(1.30)

for
2\ell>d/2
.

Finally, assume in addition
Z=\kappa_{0}Z_{t}=0
at
t=0
, and that there exists some

c>0
such that for every
t\in(0,T)

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\nabla f(x)|^{2}+|\Phi(x,t)|^{2}f(x)^{2}\,dx\ \geq\ c\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f^{2}(x)\,dx\qquad\mbox{ for all }f\in H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})

(1.31)

then
Z
vanishes identically.

Proof.

Equation (
1.29
) follows directly from combining (
1.25
) and (
1.28
). It is straightforward to see that (
1.26
), (
1.27
), (
1.30
) are sufficient to justify these computations.

Next, we define

e(t):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\kappa_{0}(\partial_{0}Z)^{2}+|\nabla Z|^{2}+|\Phi|^{2}Z^{2}\,dx+\kappa_{0}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}(\partial_{0}Z)^{2}\,dx\Big|_{t}

We multiply (
1.29
) by
\partial_{0}Z
and integrate by parts to find that

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}e(t)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\frac{1}{2}(\partial_{0}|\Phi|^{2})Z^{2}\,dx

(1.32)

In view of a Sobolev embedding and our assumption that
2\ell>\frac{d}{2}
,

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}U\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\partial_{t}U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{\infty}}+\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq\|\partial_{t}U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}W^{2\ell,\infty}}+\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{2\ell}}.

It easily follows that
\|\partial_{t}|\Phi|^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times(0,T))}\leq C
.
Thus if (
1.31
) holds, we can integrate (
1.32
) from
0
to
t
to find that

e(t)\leq e(0)+C\int_{0}^{t}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}Z^{2}\,dx\,dt\leq e(0)+C\int_{0}^{t}e(s)\,ds.

If in addition
Z=\kappa_{0}\partial_{t}Z=0
at
t=0
, then
e(0)=0
, and Grönwall’s ineqiuality implies that

e(t)=0
for all
t
.
∎

2.
the 2d Abelian Higgs model

The 2d Euclidean Abelian Higgs model is a system of equations for a pair
u=(\phi,A):{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

obtained as the Euler-Lagrange equations of the functional

E(u):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}e(u)\,dx,\qquad e(u):=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{a=1}^{2}|D_{a}\phi|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(F_{12})^{2}+\frac{1}{8}(|\phi|^{2}-1)^{2},

(2.1)

where
F_{12}=\partial_{1}A_{2}-\partial_{2}A_{1}
and
D_{a}=\partial_{a}-iA_{a}
. We will sometimes tacitly identify
A
with the
1
-form
A_{1}dx_{1}+A_{2}dx_{2}
.
We define the
energy space

{\mathcal{E}}:=\left\{u\in H^{1}_{loc}({\mathbb{R}}^{2};{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}):\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}e(u)<\infty\right\}.

For
u\in{\mathcal{E}}
. we define the current

j(u):=((i\phi,D_{1}\phi),(i\phi,D_{2}\phi))

and the vorticity

\omega(u):=\ \frac{1}{2}\big[\partial_{1}j_{2}(u)-\partial_{2}j_{1}(u)+F_{12}\big].

The following identity is due to Bogomolnyi:

e(u)=\pm\omega(u)+\frac{1}{2}\left|(D_{1}\pm iD_{2})\phi\right|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\big(F_{12}\pm\frac{1}{2}(\left|\phi\right|^{2}-1)\big)^{2}.

(2.2)

In particular, if
u\in{\mathcal{E}}
, then
\omega(u)\in L^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
. Moreover, every
u\in{\mathcal{E}}
belongs to
exactly one of the sets

{\mathcal{E}}_{N}:=\left\{u\in{\mathcal{E}}:\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\omega(u)\ dy=\pi N\right\}

where
N
is an integer called the
vortex number
6
6

6

If
|\phi|^{2}-1
and
D_{A}\phi\to 0
sufficiently rapidly at
\infty
, then one can integrate by parts to find that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\omega=\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}F_{12}
. It is traditional to define the vortex number only for
u
satisfying these decay conditions, and to define it as
\frac{1}{2\pi}\int F_{12}
. The definition we employ, following
[
4
]
, is valid for all finite-energy
u
and agrees with the traditional definition whenever the latter makes sense.
, see for example Lemma 2.4 in
[
4
]
.
Noting that
\omega(\bar{\phi},-A)=-\omega(\phi,A)
, and because the phenomena we wish to study do not appear in
{\mathcal{E}}_{0}
we will always restrict our attention to
u\in{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
for
N\geq 1
. Then it follows from (
2.2
) that
u\in{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
minimizes the energy in
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
exactly when

\displaystyle(D_{1}+iD_{2})\phi

\displaystyle=0

(2.3)

\displaystyle F_{12}+\frac{1}{2}(|\phi|^{2}-1)

\displaystyle=0.

2.1.
the Moduli space

The first three conclusions of the following theorem constitute a mild reformulation of classical results due to Taubes, see
[
12
]
, and the final conclusion is taken from Theorem 2 in
[
22
]
.

Theorem 4
.

1
. For every
N\geq 1
and
q=(q_{1},\ldots,q_{N})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N}
, there exists a unique solution
\mathfrak{u}=(\varphi,\mathcal{A})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
of (
2.3
)
such that

\varphi(z)=f(z)\frac{p(z;q)}{\sqrt{1+|p(z;q)|^{2}}}\quad\mbox{ with }f(z)>0\mbox{ everywhere, }

(2.4)

where

p(z;q):=z^{N}+q_{1}z^{N-1}+\cdots+q_{N}.

(2.5)

Moreover,
f
is smooth and satisfies

c\leq f(z)\leq c^{-1}\qquad\mbox{ for some }c=c(q)\in(0,1).

(2.6)

This solution will be denoted
\mathfrak{u}(\cdot;q)=(\varphi,\mathcal{A})(\cdot;q)
when we wish to indicate its dependence on
q
, and for every
q
it satisfies

\nabla\cdot\mathcal{A}(\cdot;q)=0.

(2.7)

2
. Every solution of (
2.3
) in
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
is gauge-equivalent to
\mathfrak{u}(\cdot;q)
for some
q\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N}
, and every critical point in
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
of the
2d
energy (
2.1
) solves the first-order system (
2.3
).

3
. For every
\delta>0
and
k>0
, if
|q|\leq K
then

\mathfrak{u}(\cdot;q)
satisfies

|D_{1}\varphi|+|D_{2}\varphi|+\big(1-|\varphi|\big)\leq C\exp\big(-(1-\delta)|z|\big).

(2.8)

for
C=C(K,\delta)
.

4
. For every
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
\mathfrak{u}(z;q)
depends smoothly on
q\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N}
.

For any
q\in{\mathbb{C}}^{N}
, if
z_{j}
is a root of
p(\cdot;q)
of multiplicity
n_{j}
, then
(
2.4
), (
2.6
) imply that

\phi(z;q)\sim c_{j}\big(z-z_{j}\big)^{n_{j}}\qquad\mbox{ near }z_{j},\ \mbox{ for some $c_{j}\neq 0$. }

(2.9)

The roots
\{z_{1},z_{2},\cdots,z_{N}\}
, repeated according to multiplicity, are called the vortex centers and
\mathfrak{u}(\cdot;s)
is called an
N
-vortex solution. (Note that
q_{j}
is exactly the
j
th elementary symmetric polynomial of the roots
z_{1},\ldots,z_{N}
.)
The
N
-vortex
moduli space

M_{N}
is defined to be the space of equivalence classes of
N
-vortex solutions
modulo the gauge symmetry:

(\varphi(q),\mathcal{A}(q))\sim(e^{i\eta}\varphi(q),\mathcal{A}(q)+d\eta).

(2.10)

We will often identify
{\mathbb{C}}^{n}
with
{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}
, and we will write
q_{\mu}
,
\mu=1,\ldots,2N
, to denote (real) coordinates on
M_{N}
.

It is also convenient to write
\mathfrak{u}=(\varphi,\mathcal{A})
as functions of
x=(x_{1},x_{2})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
rather than
z=x_{1}+ix_{2}\in{\mathbb{C}}
. From now on, we will thus write for example
\mathfrak{u}(x;q)

in place of
\mathfrak{u}(z;q)
.

Theorem
4
implies that
M_{N}
is naturally parametrized by
{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}
, via the bijections

\displaystyle q\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}\cong\ C^{N}

\displaystyle\ \longleftrightarrow\ \mbox{roots (with multiplicity) of }p(\cdot;q)

\displaystyle\ \longleftrightarrow\ \mbox{the equivalence class of solutions represented by }\mathfrak{u}(\cdot;q).

2.2.
geometry of the moduli space

We next describe a Riemannian structure on
M_{N}
that arises from its character as a quotient space of a submanifold
7
7

7

A detailed development of this geometric perspective can be found in Section 5.2 of
[
6
]
.
of
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
.
A precise statement is given in Theorem
5
below, but the idea may be loosely described as follows: given
v=(v_{1},\ldots,v_{2N})\in T_{q}M_{N}
, one may find a smooth curve
q(t)
in
M_{N}
, defined for
t
in an interval
I
containing the origin, such that
q(0)=q
and
q^{\prime}(0)=v
. For any sufficiently smooth
\eta:I\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\eta(0)=0
, this can be lifted to a curve in
{\mathcal{E}}_{N}
of the form

t\mapsto w_{v,\eta}(t)=(e^{i\eta(t)}\varphi(q(t)),\mathcal{A}(q(t))+d\eta(t)).

This lifted curve has tangent vector

w^{\prime}_{v,\eta}(0)=(v_{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\varphi(q)+i\tilde{\chi}\varphi(q),v_{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{A}(q)+d\tilde{\chi})\quad\mbox{ for }\tilde{\chi}=\partial_{t}\eta|_{t=0}.

The Riemannian metric
g
is determined by requiring that
\eta
be chosen to minimize the
L^{2}
norm of
w_{v,\eta}^{\prime}(0)
. That is, we define

|v|_{g}^{2}=\min\{\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}|w_{v,\eta}^{\prime}(0)|^{2}\,dx:w\mbox{ as above}\}

It is straightforward to check that this amounts to requiring that
\eta
be chosen so that the lifted tangent vector
w^{\prime}_{v,\eta}(0)=:(\tilde{\Phi},\tilde{A})
satisfies

\nabla\cdot\tilde{A}-(i\varphi(q),\tilde{\Phi})=0

(2.11)

This amounts to a PDE for
\tilde{\chi}=\partial_{t}\eta|_{t=0}
, see (
2.13
) below.
We will refer to this as the
gauge orthogonality condition
, as it
states exactly that the lifted tangent vector is orthogonal to all

infinitessimal gauge transformations
at
\mathfrak{u}(q)
, that is, all vectors of the form

(i\tilde{\eta}\varphi(q),d\tilde{\eta}),\qquad\tilde{\eta}:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}\mbox{ sufficiently smooth}.

(2.12)

Such vectors are tangent at
\mathfrak{u}(q)
to the gauge equivalence class (
2.10
).
See also
[
27
]
, Section 2 for a more complete discussion.

A precise statement of most of the facts we will need is given in the following

Theorem 5
.

For every
q\in M_{N}
and
\mu=1,\ldots,2N
, there exists a unique solution
\chi_{\mu}(\cdot;q):{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
of the equation

-\Delta\chi_{\mu}+|\varphi(q)|^{2}\chi_{\mu}-(i\varphi(q),\partial_{\mu}\varphi(q))=0

(2.13)

that decays exponentially to
(i\varphi(q),\partial_{\mu}\varphi(q))
as
|z|\to\infty
. For this function, the vectors

\tilde{n}_{\mu}=\tilde{n}_{\mu}(q)=\binom{\tilde{n}_{\mu,\phi}}{\tilde{n}_{\mu,a}}(q):=\binom{\partial_{\mu}\varphi(q)-i\varphi(q)\chi_{\mu}}{\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{A}_{a}(q)-\partial_{a}\chi_{\mu}}

(2.14)

have the following properties:

1.

For every
\mu
, the map
q\mapsto\tilde{n}_{\mu}(q)
is a smooth map from
M_{N}\cong{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}
into
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})^{4}
with values in
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})^{4}
.

2.

Each
\tilde{n}_{\mu}(q)
satisfies the gauge orthogonality condition (
2.11
).

3.

At every
q
, the vectors
\{\tilde{n}_{\mu}\}_{\mu=1}^{2N}
are linearly independent in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})^{4}
.

This is proved in
[
22
]
, Theorem 2, relying in part on results of
[
27
]
and other earlier work.

Given a tangent vector
v=\sum_{\mu}v^{\mu}\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{\mu}}\in T_{q}M_{N}
, we will write

\chi_{v}=\sum_{\mu}v^{\mu}\chi_{\mu},\qquad\qquad\tilde{n}_{v}:=\sum_{\mu}v^{\mu}\tilde{n}_{\mu},

(2.15)

Note that
\chi_{v},\tilde{n}_{v}
are independent of the choice of coordinates on
M_{N}
.

Definition 1
.

For
\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{\mu}},\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{\nu}}\in T_{q}M_{N}
,
we define a Riemannian metric
g
on
M_{N}
by setting

g(\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{\mu}},\frac{\partial}{\partial q_{\nu}}):=g_{\mu\nu}(q):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\tilde{n}_{\mu}(q)\cdot\tilde{n}_{\nu}(q)\,dx_{a}=(\tilde{n}_{\mu},\tilde{n}_{\nu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

where we use the shorthand
\tilde{n}_{\mu}\cdot\tilde{n}_{\mu}=(\tilde{n}_{\mu,\phi},\tilde{n}_{\nu,\phi})+\tilde{n}_{\mu,a}\tilde{n}_{\nu,a}
. We will normally not explicitly indicate the dependence of
g_{\mu\nu}
on
q
.

The linear independence of
\{\tilde{n}_{\mu}\}_{\mu=1}^{2N}
implies that
(g_{\mu\nu})
is a nonsingular matrix for every
q
, which in turn implies that the coordinates
(q_{\mu})
fixed above are good coordinates for
M_{N}
in the sense that the metric tensor is smooth and nondegenerate when written with respect to this coordinate system.

It is sometimes convenient to use the notation

\partial_{\mu}:=\frac{\partial}{\partial q^{\mu}},\qquad\qquad{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\varphi(q):=(\partial_{\mu}-i\chi_{\mu})\varphi(q),

\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}(q):=\partial_{\mu}\chi_{\nu}(q)-\partial_{\nu}\chi_{\mu}(q),\qquad\qquad\mathcal{F}_{\mu a}(q):=\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{A}_{a}(q)-\partial_{a}\chi_{\mu}(q).

We emphasize that derivatives
\partial_{\mu},\partial_{\nu}
with indices
\mu,\nu\in 1,\ldots,2N
are derivatives with respect to
q\in M_{N}
, whereas
\partial_{a}
is a derivative with respect to the spatial variables
x_{a},a=1,2
.
With this notation,

\tilde{n}_{\mu}(q)=\binom{{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\varphi(q)}{\mathcal{F}_{\mu a}(q)}.

Lemma 3
.

Writing
\nabla^{g}
to denote the Levi-Civita connection on
(M_{N},g)
, the identity

g(\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{\mu}}\partial_{\nu},\partial_{\lambda})=\left(\binom{{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}\varphi(q)}{\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{F}_{\nu a}(q))},\tilde{n}_{\lambda}(q)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

(2.16)

holds. More generally, for any two vector fields
v=v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}
and
w=w^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}
on
M_{N}
,

g(\nabla^{g}_{v}w,\partial_{\lambda})=\left(\binom{{\mathcal{D}}_{v}{\mathcal{D}}_{w}\varphi(q)}{\partial_{v}\mathcal{F}_{wa}(q))},\tilde{n}_{\lambda}(q)\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

(2.17)

where
{\mathcal{D}}_{v}:=v^{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}
and
\mathcal{F}_{wa}=w^{\nu}\mathcal{F}_{\nu a}
.

Proof.

We will write
J_{\mu\nu}:=\binom{{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}\varphi(q)}{\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{F}_{\nu a}(q))}
.
Note that for any
\mu,\nu
,

J_{\mu\nu}-J_{\nu\mu}=-\binom{i\varphi\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}}{\partial_{a}\mathcal{F}_{\mu\nu}}

(2.18)

which is an infinitessimal gauge transformation, see (
2.12
), Since the gauge tangent vectors
\tilde{n}_{\lambda}
satisfy the gauge orthogonality condition (
2.11
), it follows that

(J_{\mu\nu},\tilde{n}_{\lambda})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}=(J_{\nu\mu},\tilde{n}_{\lambda})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\qquad\mbox{ for all }\mu,\nu,\lambda\in\{1,\ldots,2N\}.

(2.19)

It is clear that

J_{\mu\nu}=\partial_{\mu}\tilde{n}_{\nu}-\binom{i\chi_{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}\varphi(q)}{0},

(2.20)

so it follows from (
2.19
) that

2(J_{\mu\nu},\tilde{n}_{\lambda})_{L^{2}}=(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{n}_{\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\tilde{n}_{\mu},\tilde{n}_{\lambda})_{L^{2}}-(i(\chi_{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}\varphi+\chi_{\nu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\varphi),{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}\varphi)_{L^{2}}.

(2.21)

We now consider the right-hand side of (
2.16
). From the Koszul formula and the definition of the metric,

\displaystyle 2g(\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{\mu}}\partial_{\nu},\partial_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}g_{\nu\lambda}+\partial_{\nu}g_{\lambda\nu}-\partial_{\lambda}g_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}(\tilde{n}_{\nu},\tilde{n}_{\lambda})_{L^{2}}+\partial_{\nu}(\tilde{n}_{\nu},\tilde{n}_{\lambda})_{L^{2}}-\partial_{\lambda}(\tilde{n}_{\mu},\tilde{n}_{\nu})_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{n}_{\nu}+\partial_{\nu}\tilde{n}_{\mu},\tilde{n}_{\lambda})_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle+(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{n}_{\lambda}-\partial_{\lambda}\tilde{n}_{\mu},\tilde{n}_{\nu})_{L^{2}}+(\partial_{\nu}\tilde{n}_{\lambda}-\partial_{\lambda}\tilde{n}_{\nu},\tilde{n}_{\mu})_{L^{2}}.

In view of (
2.21
), to prove (
2.16
) it now suffices to check that

(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{n}_{\lambda}-\partial_{\lambda}\tilde{n}_{\mu},\tilde{n}_{\nu})_{L^{2}}+(\partial_{\nu}\tilde{n}_{\lambda}-\partial_{\lambda}\tilde{n}_{\nu},\tilde{n}_{\mu})_{L^{2}}=-(i(\chi_{\mu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}\varphi+\chi_{\nu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\varphi),{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}\varphi)_{L^{2}}.

(2.22)

To do this, note from (
2.18
) and (
2.20
) that

\partial_{\mu}\tilde{n}_{\lambda}-\partial_{\lambda}\tilde{n}_{\mu}=-\binom{i\varphi\mathcal{F}_{\mu\lambda}}{\partial_{a}\mathcal{F}_{\mu\lambda}}-\binom{i(\chi_{\lambda}\partial_{\mu}\varphi-\chi_{\mu}\partial_{\lambda}\varphi)}{0}.

Again using gauge orthogonality, we deduce that

\displaystyle(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{n}_{\lambda}-\partial_{\lambda}\tilde{n}_{\mu},\tilde{n}_{\nu})_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\lambda}(i\partial_{\mu}\varphi,{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}\varphi)_{L^{2}}+\chi_{\mu}(i\partial_{\lambda}\varphi,{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}\varphi)_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle=-\chi_{\lambda}(i\partial_{\mu}\varphi,\partial_{\nu}\varphi)_{L^{2}}+\chi_{\lambda}\chi_{\nu}(\partial_{\mu}\varphi,\varphi)_{L^{2}}+\chi_{\mu}(i\partial_{\lambda}\varphi,{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}\varphi)_{L^{2}}.

Since
(\partial_{\mu}\varphi,\varphi)=({\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\varphi,\varphi)=(i{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\varphi,i\varphi)
, we can rewrite as

(\partial_{\mu}\tilde{n}_{\lambda}-\partial_{\lambda}\tilde{n}_{\mu},\tilde{n}_{\nu})_{L^{2}}=-\chi_{\lambda}(i\partial_{\mu}\varphi,\partial_{\nu}\varphi)_{L^{2}}-\chi_{\nu}(-i\chi_{\lambda}\varphi,iD_{\mu}\varphi)_{L^{2}}-\chi_{\mu}(\partial_{\lambda}\varphi,i{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}\varphi)_{L^{2}}.

We arrive
at (
2.22
) and complete the proof of (
2.16
) by permuting the
\mu
and
\nu
indices, adding, and recalling that
(i\partial_{\mu}\varphi,\partial_{\nu}\varphi)_{L^{2}}=-(i\partial_{\nu}\varphi,\partial_{\mu}\varphi)_{L^{2}}
.

To prove (
2.17
), note that

\nabla^{g}_{v}\partial_{w}=v^{\mu}\nabla^{g}_{\mu}(w^{\nu}\partial_{\mu})=v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}w^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}+v^{\mu}w^{\nu}\nabla^{g}_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}=\partial_{v}w^{\nu}\partial_{\nu}+v^{\mu}w^{\nu}\nabla^{g}_{\nu}\partial_{\mu}

and similarly

\binom{D_{v}D_{w}\varphi(q)}{\partial_{v}\mathcal{F}_{wa}(q)}=v^{\mu}\binom{D_{\mu}(w^{\nu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}\varphi(q)}{\partial_{\mu}(w^{\nu}\mathcal{F}_{\nu a}(q)}=v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}w^{\nu}\,\tilde{n}_{\nu}+v^{\mu}w^{\nu}\binom{D_{\mu}D_{\nu}\varphi(q)}{\partial_{\mu}\mathcal{F}_{\nu a}(q)}.

So (
2.17
) follows from (
2.16
) and Definition
1
.
∎

Definition 2
.

We will write
\{n_{\mu}(q)\}_{\mu=1}^{2N}
to denote a fixed orthonormal basis for the vector space generated by
\{\tilde{n}_{\mu}(q)\}_{\mu=1}^{2N}
and depending smoothly on
q
, for example defined by
n_{\mu}:=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{2N}A_{\mu\alpha}\tilde{n}_{\alpha}
for the unique symmetric positive definite
(A_{\mu\alpha})
such that
\sum_{\alpha\beta}A_{\mu\alpha}A_{\nu\beta}g_{\alpha\beta}=\delta_{\mu\nu}
.

We close this section by recording more results on regularity and decay of the functions introduced above.

We may consider
\varphi,\mathcal{A},n_{\mu},
…as functions on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times M_{N}
. In fact we will always restrict our attention to situations where
q\in K\subset M_{N}
, where
K
is a fixed compact set.
It is clear that for any compact
K\subset M_{N}
there exists
C=C(K)
such for any
q\in K
and
v=v^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}\in T_{q}M_{N}
,

C^{-1}|v|^{2}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}}=\delta_{\mu\nu}v^{\mu}v^{\nu}\leq|v|^{2}_{g}=g_{\mu\nu}(q)v^{\mu}v^{\nu}\leq C|v|^{2}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}}

In view of this, for
q\in K
we can and will use the Euclidean norm (but writing
|\cdot|
rather than
|\cdot|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}}
) in defining norms of derivatives with respect to
q_{\mu},\mu=1,\ldots,2N

of
\varphi,\mathcal{A},n_{\mu}
etc, and we will often consider derivatives with respect to the Euclidean structure rather than covariant derivatives on
M_{N}
.

Lemma 4
.

Let
K
be a compact subset of
M^{n}
, and let
\partial^{r}
denote differentiation with respect to
x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and/or
q\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}\cong M_{N}
as indicated by a multi-index
r
.
Then there exists
C,\gamma
, possibly depending
K
and
r
, such that for any
q\in K
,

\displaystyle|\partial^{r}\varphi(x,q)|\leq C(|x|+1)^{-|r|},

\displaystyle|\partial^{r}\chi_{\lambda}(x,q)|,|\partial^{r}\mathcal{A}(x,q)|\leq C(|x|+1)^{-|r|-1},

\displaystyle|\partial^{r}(|\varphi(x;q)|^{2}-1)|,\ |\partial^{r}(D_{a}\varphi(x;q)|,\ |\partial^{r}\tilde{n}_{\mu}(x;q)|\leq Ce^{-\gamma|x|}.

Proof.

As noted in Proposition 3 of
[
9
]
, this is a straightforward consequence of basic theory as developed in
[
12
,
27
,
22
]
, etc.
∎

2.3.
The Higgs mechanism

Our main results rely heavily on the analysis of a linearization of the (
1
), and the linearization of the 2d elliptic Abelian Higgs model plays a crucial role in this analysis.

We will write
the
2d
equations as
S^{2d}[u]=0
, where

S^{2d}[u]:=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}S^{2d}_{\phi}[u]\\
S^{2d}_{b}[u]\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}-D_{a}D_{a}\Phi+\frac{\lambda}{2}(|\Phi|^{2}-1)\Phi\\
-\partial_{a}F_{ab}-(i\Phi,D_{b}\Phi)\end{array}\right)_{b=1,2}

(2.23)

For
u=(\Phi,A_{a})
and
\tilde{u}=(\tilde{\Phi},\tilde{A}_{a})
, we define the linearized operator as

{\mathcal{L}}[u](\tilde{u}):=\frac{d}{ds}\Big|_{s=0}S^{2d}[u+s\tilde{u}]

(2.24)

A calculation shows that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\phi}[u](\tilde{u})

\displaystyle=-D_{A_{a}}D_{A_{a}}\tilde{\Phi}+2i\tilde{A}_{a}D_{A_{a}}\Phi+i\Phi\partial_{a}\tilde{A}_{a}+\frac{1}{2}(|\Phi|^{2}-1)\tilde{\Phi}+(\Phi,\tilde{\Phi})\Phi

(2.25)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{a}[u](\tilde{u})

\displaystyle=-\partial_{b}\tilde{F}_{ba}-(i\Phi,D_{A_{a}}\tilde{\Phi})-(i\tilde{\Phi},D_{A_{a}}\Phi)+\tilde{A}_{a}|\Phi|^{2},

where we recall that repeated indices
a,b,...
are implicitly summed from
1
to
2
, and

D_{A_{a}}=\partial_{a}-iA_{a},\qquad\tilde{F}_{ab}=\partial_{a}\tilde{A}_{b}-\partial_{b}\tilde{A}_{a}.

Awkwardly,
{\mathcal{L}}[\mathfrak{u}(q)]
has a huge nullspace for
q\in M_{N}
, or for any gauge-equivalent
u
.
Indeed,
because

S^{2d}[e^{is\eta}\varphi(q),\mathcal{A}(q)+sd\eta]=0
for all
s
, and noting that

(e^{is\eta}\varphi(q),\mathcal{A}(q)+sd\eta)=\mathfrak{u}(q)+s(i\eta\varphi(q),d\eta)+O(s^{2}),

we find that

{\mathcal{L}}[\mathfrak{u}(q)](i\eta\varphi(q),d\eta)=0\qquad\mbox{ for all smooth enough }\eta.

(2.26)

In other words, the kernel of
{\mathcal{L}}[\mathfrak{u}(q)]
contains all infinitessimal gauge transformations at
\mathfrak{u}(q)
. To remedy this, following Stuart
[
27
]
, we will modify
{\mathcal{L}}
to obtain an operator with a finite-dimemsional nullspace. We define

L[u](\tilde{u}):={\mathcal{L}}[u](\tilde{u})+\left(\begin{array}[]{c}i\Phi(\nabla\cdot\tilde{A}-(i\Phi,\tilde{\Phi}))\\
\partial_{a}(\nabla\cdot\tilde{A}-(i\Phi,\tilde{\Phi}))\end{array}\right)_{a=1,2}.

(2.27)

One readily checks that

{L}[u](\tilde{u})=\begin{pmatrix}-D_{A_{a}}D_{A_{a}}\tilde{\Phi}+2i\tilde{A}_{a}D_{A_{a}}\Phi+\frac{1}{2}\big(3|\Phi|^{2}-1\big)\tilde{\Phi}\\
-\Delta\tilde{A}_{b}+|\Phi|^{2}\tilde{A}_{b}-2\big(i\tilde{\Phi},D_{A_{b}}\Phi\big)\end{pmatrix}_{{}_{b=1,2}}

(2.28)

It is immediate from the definitions that

{\mathcal{L}}[\mathfrak{u}(q)](\tilde{u})=L[u](\tilde{u})\qquad\mbox{ if $\tilde{u}$ is gauge-orthogonal, see \eqref{eq:go}.}

(2.29)

The following theorem plays a key role in our analysis.

Theorem 6

(
[
27
]
, Theorems 2.6 and 3.1)
.

For
q\in M_{N}
, the nullspace of
L[\mathfrak{u}(q)]
is
2N
-dimensional and is spanned by

\{\tilde{n}_{\mu}\}_{\mu=1}^{2N}
. Moreover, for every compact
K\subset M_{N}
there exists
\gamma=\gamma(K)>0
such that if
q\in K
and
\tilde{u}\in(H^{1})^{4}
satisfies
(\tilde{u},\tilde{n}_{\mu}(q))_{L^{2}}=0
for all
\mu=1,\ldots,2N
, then

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\tilde{u}\cdot L[\mathfrak{u}(q)]\tilde{u}\,dx\ \geq\ \gamma\|\tilde{u}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}.

(2.30)

Stuart
[
27
]
establishes (
2.30
) with the norm

\|\tilde{u}\|_{H^{1}_{q}}^{2}:=\|\tilde{A}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}|\tilde{\Phi}|^{2}+|(\nabla-i\mathcal{A}(q))\tilde{\Phi}|^{2}\,dx

on the right-hand side, but using the fact that
\sup_{q\in K}\|\mathcal{A}_{a}(\cdot;q)\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}<\infty
for
K
compact, this is easily seen to imply the estimate as we have stated it.

We remark that, since
\tilde{n}_{\mu}
is gauge-orthogonal, the theorem and (
2.29
) immediately imply that

{\mathcal{L}}[\mathfrak{u}(q)]\tilde{n}_{\mu}=0\qquad\mbox{ everywhere}.

(2.31)

3.
construction of approximate solutions

In this section we construct an approximate solution of the Abelian Higgs model.

We start with a solution of
(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times(0,T)\mapsto q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})\in M_{N}
of the geometric evolution equation (
1.8
) satisfying assumptions (
1.9
) and (
1.10
)
and that
\Theta
as defined in (
1.11
) is finite.

In coordinates, equation (
1.8
) takes the form

(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}\partial_{0}+\kappa_{1}\partial_{0}-\Delta_{\bar{a}})q^{\mu}=-\Gamma^{\mu}_{\nu\lambda}(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}q^{\nu}\partial_{0}q^{\lambda}-\partial_{\bar{a}}q^{\nu}\partial_{\bar{a}}q^{\lambda}),

where
\Gamma^{\mu}_{\nu\lambda}
are the Christoffel symbols. It follows
that
\partial_{0}^{2}q
has the same regularity as
\Delta_{\bar{a}}q
if
\kappa_{0}>0
:

\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{0}^{2}q\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{L-1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}\leq C.

(3.1)

We will write

q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t)=q(\varepsilon x_{\bar{a}},\varepsilon t)=q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}),\qquad(y_{a},y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}):=(x_{a},\varepsilon x_{\bar{a}},\varepsilon t).

The leading-order term in our approximate solution will have the form

U^{\varepsilon}_{0}(x,t)=\begin{pmatrix}u^{\varepsilon}\\
A^{\varepsilon}_{\bar{a}}\\
A^{\varepsilon}_{0}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{u}(x_{a};q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t))\\
\chi_{\partial_{\bar{a}}q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t)}(x_{a});q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t))\\
\chi_{\partial_{t}q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t)}(x_{a};q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t))\\
\end{pmatrix}

(3.2)

using notation introduced in Theorems
4
and
5
, see also (
2.15
). Since
|\partial_{t}q_{\varepsilon}|,|\partial_{\bar{a}}q_{\varepsilon}|\leq C\varepsilon
, it follows that

|A^{\varepsilon}_{\bar{a}}|,|A^{\varepsilon}_{0}|\leq C\varepsilon.

From the definitions, see again (
2.15
) and Theorem
5
, one finds that
A^{\varepsilon}_{0}
and
A^{\varepsilon}_{\bar{a}}
are chosen exactly to guarantee that

\binom{D_{\alpha}\Phi^{\varepsilon}}{\partial_{\alpha}A^{\varepsilon}_{a}-\partial_{a}A^{\varepsilon}_{\alpha}}=\sum_{\mu}\partial_{\alpha}q_{\varepsilon}^{\mu}\,\tilde{n}_{\mu}(q_{\varepsilon})=\tilde{n}_{\partial_{\alpha}q_{\varepsilon}}\qquad\qquad\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d.

(3.3)

In particular, for every
(x_{\bar{a}},t)
,

\binom{D_{\alpha}\Phi^{\varepsilon}}{\partial_{\alpha}A^{\varepsilon}_{a}-\partial_{a}A^{\varepsilon}_{\alpha}}(\cdot,x_{\bar{a}},t)\ \mbox{ satisfies gauge-orthogonality condition \eqref{eq:go}}.

(3.4)

We will construct an approximate solution by a formal expansion in powers of
\varepsilon
. In the statement below,
U^{\varepsilon}_{m}
collects all the terms up to order
\varepsilon^{2m}
in the expansion, apart from the leading term. Thus the size of
U^{\varepsilon}_{m}
reflects the size of the first correction term. The full ansatz, which involves some notation to be introduced below, is displayed in (
3.46
), and estimates for individual terms in the expansion appear in (
3.47
), (
3.48
).

In the result below, for a multiindex
r=(r_{0},\ldots,r_{d})
with each
r_{j}\in{\mathbb{Z}}_{\geq 0}
, we use the notation

\partial^{r}=\partial_{t}^{r_{0}}\partial_{x_{1}}^{r_{1}}\cdots\partial_{x_{d}}^{r_{d}},\qquad\qquad|r_{\bar{a}}|=r_{3}+\cdots+r_{d}.

Our estimates will control one time derivative if
\kappa_{0}>0
and no time derivatives if
\kappa_{0}=0
, so
we always assume

r_{0}\leq 1,\qquad\mbox{ and }r_{0}=0\ \ \mbox{ if }\kappa_{0}=0.

(3.5)

Proposition 1
.

Let
j=\lfloor d/2\rfloor
, and fix positive integers

L,m
such that

k_{m}:=L-4m>\frac{d}{2}\geq j>\frac{d}{2}-1.

(3.6)

Let
q:{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times(0,T)\to M_{N}
be a solution of (
1.8
) satisfying (
1.9
), (
1.10
) for this choice of
L
, with
\Theta<\infty
.

There exists
T_{0}\in(0,T]
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for every positive
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, there exists a map
U_{m}^{\varepsilon}:(0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
with the following properties:

1.
The map
U_{m,\varepsilon}
defined by

U_{m,\varepsilon}=U^{\varepsilon}_{0}+\varepsilon^{2}U_{m}^{\varepsilon}

is an approximate solution of (
1
) in the sense that for any
\gamma\in(0,1/2)
and any multi-index
|r|\leq k_{m}-j
satisfying (
3.5
), there exist a positive constant
8
8

8

All constants in the proposition, and throughout this section, can in principle be estimated in terms of
\kappa_{0},\kappa_{1}
, the set
K
from (
1.9
),
\Theta
from (
1.10
) and
\gamma\in(0,1)
, where the latter is only needed for estimates that contain a weight
e^{-\gamma|x_{a}|}
, such as (
