).

C
such that (using notation introduced in (
1.19
), (
1.20
))

\displaystyle|\partial^{r}S_{u}[U_{m,\varepsilon}]|(x,t)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2(m+1)+r_{0}+|r_{\bar{a}}|}e^{-\gamma|x_{a}|}

(3.7)

\displaystyle|\partial^{r}S_{\alpha}[U_{m,\varepsilon}]|(x,t)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2m+3+r_{0}+|r_{\bar{a}}|}e^{-\gamma|x_{a}|},\qquad\quad\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d

for all
(x,t)\in(0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. Moreover,

\|S[U_{m,\varepsilon}](\cdot,t)\|_{H^{k_{m}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}S[U_{m,\varepsilon}](\cdot,t)\|_{H^{k_{m}-1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C\varepsilon^{2m+2-\frac{d}{2}}

(3.8)

for all
t\in(0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)
.

2
There exists a constant
C
such that for any multi-index
|r|\leq k_{m}+2-j
satisfying (
3.5
),

\displaystyle|\partial^{r}u_{m}^{\varepsilon}|(x,t)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{r_{0}+|r_{\bar{a}}|}e^{-\gamma|x_{a}|}

(3.9)

\displaystyle|\partial^{r}A_{m,\alpha}^{\varepsilon}|(x,t)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{1+r_{0}+|r_{\bar{a}}|}e^{-\gamma|x_{a}|}\quad\mbox{ for }\alpha\in\{0,3,\ldots,d\}

for all
(x,t)\in(0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
, where

U^{m}_{\varepsilon}=\begin{pmatrix}u_{m}^{\varepsilon}\\
A_{m,{\bar{a}}}^{\varepsilon}\\
A_{m,0}^{\epsilon}\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{ with }u^{\varepsilon}_{m}=\begin{pmatrix}\Phi^{\varepsilon}_{m}\\
A^{\varepsilon}_{m,a}\end{pmatrix}.

Remark 5
.

Our full results, which are sharper and more detailed than the conclusions stated above,
are stated (after the change of variables described in
Section
3.1
below) in (
3.46
) – (
3.50
).
For example, they imply that if
r
is any multi-index such that
|r|\leq k_{m}
and satisfying (
3.5
), then

\displaystyle\|\partial^{r}S_{u}[U_{m,\varepsilon}](\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2m+2+r_{0}+|r_{\bar{a}}|-\frac{d}{2}},

(3.10)

\displaystyle\|\partial^{r}S_{\alpha}[U_{m,\varepsilon}](\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2m+3+r_{0}+|r_{\bar{a}}|-\frac{d}{2}},\qquad\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d

which is stronger than (
3.8
).

Note that as a result of (
3.9
) and the definition (
3.2
) of the leading-order term
U^{\varepsilon}_{0}
, for
|r|\leq k_{m}+2-j
such that
r_{0}\leq 1
the approximate solution

U_{m,\varepsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|\partial^{r}u_{m,\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T_{0})\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{r_{0}+|r_{\bar{a}}|}

(3.11)

\displaystyle\|\partial^{r}A_{m,\varepsilon,\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}((0,T_{0})\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{1+r_{0}+|r_{\bar{a}}|}\quad\mbox{ for }\alpha\in\{0,3,\ldots,d\}.

The rest of this section is devoted to the proof of the proposition.

3.1.
Change of variables

In our construction of an approximate solution, we will work with the variables
y=(y_{a},y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
defined by

(y_{a},y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})=(x_{a},\varepsilon x_{\bar{a}},\varepsilon t).

(3.12)

We will use the notation

\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{0}}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{0},\qquad\quad\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}=\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{\bar{a}}}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{\bar{a}}}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\partial_{\bar{a}},\qquad\quad\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}=\partial_{a}.

We will use the fonts
9
9

9

If these fonts look uncomfortably similar to
A,D,F
, one should just keep in mind that all computations in all proofs in this section are carried out with respect to the
y
variables.

{\rm A},{\rm D},{\rm F}
to denote the connection
1
-form, covariant derivative, and curvature
2
-form expressed with respect to these new variables.
Thus

A_{0}dt+\sum_{j=1}^{d}A_{j}dx^{j}={\rm A}_{0}dy_{0}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}{\rm A}_{j}dy^{j}

which implies that

\displaystyle{\rm A}_{\alpha}(y_{a},y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}A_{\alpha}(x_{a},\varepsilon x_{\bar{a}},\varepsilon t)\quad\mbox{ for }\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d,

\displaystyle{\rm A}_{a}(y_{a},y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})

\displaystyle=A_{a}(x_{a},\varepsilon x_{\bar{a}},\varepsilon t),\quad\mbox{ for }a=1,2.

Henceforth omitting the arguments when no confusion can result, we similarly have

{\rm D}_{a}=D_{a},\qquad\qquad\qquad{\rm D}_{\alpha}=\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}-i{\rm A}_{\alpha}=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}D_{\alpha}\ \ \mbox{ for }\ \ \alpha=0,3,\ldots,d

and
{\rm F}_{\alpha\beta}=\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}{\rm A}_{\beta}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\beta}{\rm A}_{\alpha}
, so that

{\rm F}_{\alpha\beta}=\begin{cases}F_{\alpha\beta}&\mbox{ if }\alpha,\beta\in\{1,2\}\\
\varepsilon^{-1}F_{\alpha\beta}&\mbox{ if }\alpha\in\{1,2\}\mbox{ and }\beta\not\in\{1,2\}\mbox{ or vice versa}\\
\varepsilon^{-2}F_{\alpha\beta}&\mbox{ if }\alpha,\beta\not\in\{1,2\}\end{cases}

We will also write
{\rm U}
rather than
U
to denote a map
\binom{\Phi}{{\rm A}}
where the
1
-form coefficients are written with respect to the
y=(y_{a},y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
variables.
We define

{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}_{u}[{\rm U}](y):=S_{u}[U](x,t),\qquad\qquad{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}_{\alpha}[{\rm U}](y):=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}S_{\alpha}[U](x,t),\ \ \ \alpha=0,3,\ldots,d

(3.13)

where
{\rm U}
and
U
related as above, with
{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}_{u}=\binom{{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}_{\phi}}{{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}_{a}}
as usual.
In the new variables, we will write our ansatz as

{\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}={\rm U}^{0}+\varepsilon^{2}{\rm U}_{m}^{\varepsilon}\qquad\mbox{ where }{\rm U}^{0}(y):=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm u}^{0}\\
{\rm A}^{0}_{\bar{a}}\\
{\rm A}^{0}_{0}\end{pmatrix}(y):=\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{u}(y_{a};q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))\\
\chi_{\partial_{\bar{a}}q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})}(y_{a})\\
\chi_{\partial_{t}q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})}(y_{a})\\
\end{pmatrix}.

(3.14)

To establish the estimates in Proposition
1
, it now suffices to prove, using notation introduced in (
1.22
), (
1.21
), that

\|{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}]\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}]\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}-1,j}_{\gamma}}\leq C\varepsilon^{2(m+1)}

(3.15)

and

\|{\rm U}^{\varepsilon}_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}+2,j}_{\gamma}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U}^{\varepsilon}_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}+1,j}_{\gamma}}\leq C.

(3.16)

Indeed, (
3.15
) contains estimates of

\|{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}]\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}H^{k_{m}}}
, and (
3.16
) contains estimates of

\|{\rm U}^{\varepsilon}_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{k_{m}+2-j}_{\gamma}}

that are equivalent to
(
3.10
) and (
3.9
) respectively.

3.2.
Some preliminary computations

It is straightforward to check that

{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}]={\rm S}^{0}[{\rm U}]+\varepsilon^{2}{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}]

(3.17)

for

{\rm S}^{0}[{\rm U}]=\begin{pmatrix}{\rm S}^{0}_{\phi}[{\rm U}]\\
{\rm S}^{0}_{a}[{\rm U}]\\
{\rm S}^{0}_{\bar{a}}[{\rm U}]\\
{\rm S}^{0}_{0}[{\rm U}]\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-{\rm D}_{a}{\rm D}_{a}\Phi+\frac{1}{2}(|\Phi|^{2}-1)\Phi\\
-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{b}{\rm F}_{ba}-(i\Phi,{\rm D}_{a}\Phi)\\
-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}{\rm F}_{a\bar{a}}-(i\Phi,{\rm D}_{\bar{a}}\Phi)\\
-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}{\rm F}_{a0}-(i\Phi,{\rm D}_{0}\Phi)\end{pmatrix}

(3.18)

and

{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}]=\begin{pmatrix}(\kappa_{0}{\rm D}_{0}{\rm D}_{0}+\kappa_{1}{\rm D}_{0}-{\rm D}_{\bar{a}}{\rm D}_{\bar{a}})\Phi\\
(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}+\kappa_{1}){\rm F}_{0a}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}{\rm F}_{\bar{a}a}\\
(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}+\kappa_{1}){\rm F}_{0\bar{a}}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{b}}{\rm F}_{\bar{b}\bar{a}}\\
-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{b}}{\rm F}_{\bar{b}0}\end{pmatrix}.

(3.19)

More than once we will encounter expressions of the form
{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}+\varepsilon^{p}\tilde{\rm U}]
, which we will expand in powers of
\varepsilon
.
To this end we introduce the notation

{\rm Z}=({\rm U},\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U},\nabla{\rm U}),\qquad\tilde{\rm Z}=(\tilde{\rm U},\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\tilde{\rm U},\nabla\tilde{\rm U}),

and we note that all nonlinear terms in
S^{0}[{\rm U}]
and
S^{1}[{\rm U}]
are polynomials in
{\rm Z}
of degree at most 3.
For
m=0,1
we can therefore expand

S^{m}[{\rm U}+\varepsilon^{p}\tilde{\rm U}]=S^{m}[{\rm U}]+\varepsilon^{p}\ell^{m}[{\rm U}](\tilde{\rm U})+\varepsilon^{2p}p^{m}_{2}({\rm Z},\tilde{\rm Z})+\varepsilon^{3p}p^{m}_{3}(\tilde{\rm Z})

(3.20)

where
\ell^{m}[{\rm U}]
is a linear operator on
\tilde{\rm U}
with coefficients depending on
{\rm U}
, and
p^{m}_{j}
is a polynomial in its arguments, at most cubic, and of order
j
in
\tilde{\rm Z}
, all uniquely determined by (
3.20
) and the definitions of
{\rm S}^{m}
,
m=0,1
. Noting that
{\rm S}^{0}_{u}[{\rm U}]=S^{2d}[{\rm u}]
as defined in (
2.23
),
we find that

\displaystyle\ell^{0}[{\rm U}](\tilde{\rm U})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\ell^{0}_{u}[{\rm U}]\\
\ell^{0}_{\bar{a}}[{\rm U}]\\
\ell^{0}_{0}[{\rm U}]\\
\end{pmatrix}(\tilde{\rm U})

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathcal{L}}[{\rm u}](\tilde{\rm u})\\
-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{b}\tilde{\rm F}_{b\bar{a}}+|\Phi|^{2}\tilde{\rm A}_{\bar{a}}-(i\tilde{\Phi},D_{{\rm A}_{\bar{a}}}\Phi)-(i\Phi,D_{{\rm A}_{\bar{a}}}\tilde{\Phi})\\
-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{b}\tilde{\rm F}_{b0}+|\Phi|^{2}\tilde{\rm A}_{0}-(i\tilde{\Phi},D_{{\rm A}_{0}}\Phi)-(i\Phi,D_{{\rm A}_{0}}\tilde{\Phi})\end{pmatrix}

(3.21)

where
\ell^{m}_{u}=\binom{\ell^{m}_{\phi}}{\ell^{m}_{a}}
and

\tilde{\rm F}_{\alpha\beta}=\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}\tilde{\rm A}_{\beta}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\beta}\tilde{\rm A}_{\alpha}.

Next, observing that
{\rm S}^{1}_{\alpha}
is linear for all
\alpha=0,\ldots,d
, we compute

\ell^{1}[{\rm U}](\tilde{\rm U})=\begin{pmatrix}\ell^{1}_{\phi}[{\rm U}](\tilde{\rm U})\\
(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}+\kappa_{1})\tilde{\rm F}_{0a}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}\tilde{\rm F}_{\bar{a}a}\\
(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}+\kappa_{1})\tilde{\rm F}_{0\bar{a}}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{b}}\tilde{\rm F}_{\bar{b}\bar{a}}\\
-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{b}}\tilde{\rm F}_{\bar{b}0}\end{pmatrix}

(3.22)

where

\displaystyle\ell^{1}_{\phi}[{\rm U}](\tilde{\rm U})

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}{\rm D}_{A_{0}}{\rm D}_{A_{0}}+\kappa_{1}{\rm D}_{A_{0}}-{\rm D}_{A_{\bar{a}}}{\rm D}_{A_{\bar{a}}})\tilde{\Phi}

(3.23)

\displaystyle-(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\tilde{\rm A}_{0}+\kappa_{1}\tilde{\rm A}_{0}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}\tilde{\rm A}_{\bar{a}})i\Phi-2i(\kappa_{0}\tilde{\rm A}_{0}{\rm D}_{0}\Phi-\tilde{\rm A}_{\bar{a}}{\rm D}_{\bar{a}}\Phi).

The leading-order term in the ansatz is designed exactly to satisfy the following lemma.

Lemma 5
.

{\rm S}^{0}[{\rm U}^{0}]=0
.

Proof.

The terms
{\rm S}^{0}_{u}=\binom{{\rm S}^{0}_{\phi}}{{\rm S}^{0}_{a}}
act only on
{\rm u}=\binom{\Phi}{{\rm A}_{a}}
and coincide with

S^{2d}
as defined in (
2.23
) on every 2d slice
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times\{(y_{\bar{a},a)}\}
.
Since
{\rm u}^{0}(\cdot,y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})=\mathfrak{u}(\cdot;q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))
belongs to the moduli space for every
(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
,
it follows that
{\rm S}^{0}_{u}[{\rm U}^{0}]=0
.

Next, recall from (
3.4
), rescaled to the
y
variables, that
{\rm A}^{0}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d
is chosen to guarantee that

\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}{\rm u}^{0}-\binom{i{\rm A}^{0}_{\alpha}\Phi^{0}}{d{\rm A}^{0}_{\alpha}}\ \ \mbox{ satisfies gauge-orthogonality condition \eqref{eq:go}}

with respect to the
y_{a}
variables for every
(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
.
This states exactly that

-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}(\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}{\rm A}^{0}_{\alpha}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}{\rm A}^{0}_{a})+(i\Phi^{0},{\rm D}_{\alpha}\Phi^{0})=0

which is equivalent to the assertion that
{\rm S}^{0}_{\alpha}[U^{0}]=0
for
\alpha=0,3,\ldots d
.
∎

The next lemma will be used at a later stage of the construction of approximate solutions.

Lemma 6
.

For
\mu=1,\ldots,2N
, let

N_{\mu}(y):=\begin{pmatrix}N_{\mu,u}\\
N_{\mu,\bar{a}}\\
N_{\mu,0}\end{pmatrix}(y):=\begin{pmatrix}n_{\mu}(y_{a};q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))\\
\xi_{\mu,\bar{a}}\\
\xi_{\mu,0}\end{pmatrix}

where
\xi_{\mu,\alpha}
solves, for every
(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times(0,T)
and
\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d
,

(-\Delta_{a}+|\varphi(q)|^{2})\xi_{\mu,\alpha}=2(in_{\mu,\phi}(\cdot,q),{\rm D}_{{\rm A}^{0}_{\alpha}}\varphi(\cdot;q))\qquad\mbox{ in }{\mathbb{R}}^{2}.

(3.24)

Then for any smooth functions
c_{\mu}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times(0,T)\to{\mathbb{R}}
for
\mu=1,\ldots,2N
,

\ell^{0}[{\rm U}_{0}](c_{\mu}N_{\mu})=0\qquad

where as usual the repeated
\mu
index is summed implicitly
\mu=1,\ldots,2N
.

Proof.

Because
{\mathcal{L}}
involves only derivatives with respect to the
y_{a}
variables,

\ell^{0}_{u}[U_{0}](c_{\mu}N_{\mu})\overset{\eqref{eq:wSe5}}{=}{\mathcal{L}}[\mathfrak{u}(q)](c_{\mu}n_{\mu})=c_{\mu}{\mathcal{L}}[\mathfrak{u}(q)](n_{\mu})\overset{\eqref{kercalL}}{=}0.

Next, for any
\alpha\in 0,3,\ldots,d
, we compute

\displaystyle\ell^{0}_{\alpha}[U_{0}](c_{\mu}N_{\mu})

\displaystyle=(-\Delta_{a}+|\varphi(q)|^{2})(c_{\mu}\xi_{\mu,\alpha})+\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}(c_{\mu}n_{\mu,a})

\displaystyle-(ic_{\mu}n_{\mu,\phi},{\rm D}_{{\rm A}_{\alpha}}\varphi(q))-(i\varphi(q),{\rm D}_{{\rm A}_{\alpha}}(c_{\mu}n_{\mu,\phi}).

Since
n_{\mu}(\cdot,q)
is gauge-orthogonal at
\mathfrak{u}(q)
,

\displaystyle\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}(c_{\mu}n_{\mu,a})

\displaystyle=\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}[c_{\mu}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}(n_{\mu,a})]=\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}[c_{\mu}(i\varphi(q),n_{\mu,\phi})]

\displaystyle=\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}(i\varphi(q),c_{\mu}n_{\mu,\phi})

\displaystyle=(i{\rm D}_{\alpha}\varphi(q),c_{\mu}n_{\mu,\phi})+(i\varphi(q),{\rm D}_{\alpha}(c_{\mu}n_{\mu,\phi}))

Substituting into the above and simplifying, we find that

\ell^{0}_{\alpha}[U_{0}](c_{\mu}N_{\mu})=(-\Delta_{a}+|\varphi(q)|^{2})(c_{\mu}\xi_{\mu,\alpha})-2c_{\mu}(in_{\mu,\phi},{\rm D}_{\alpha}\varphi(q))\overset{\eqref{ximualpha}}{=}0.

∎

The next two lemmas record some properties of the auxiliary functions introduced above.

Lemma 7
.

Assume (
1.9
) and (
1.10
), and let
j>\frac{d}{2}-1
.
Then

\displaystyle\|{\rm U}^{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T/\varepsilon}W^{L-j,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U}^{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T/\varepsilon}W^{L-j,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C(\Theta)

(3.25)

\displaystyle\|N_{\mu}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T/\varepsilon}W^{L-j,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}N_{\mu}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T/\varepsilon}W^{L-j,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C(\Theta)

Moreover, if
v\in H^{l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for
l\leq L
, then for any
t\in(0,T/\varepsilon)
,
{\rm U}^{0}(t)v,N_{\mu}(t)v\in H^{l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, and

\|{\rm U}^{0}v\|_{H^{l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\|N_{\mu}v\|_{H^{l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C(\Theta)\|v\|_{H^{l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

(3.26)

where
{\rm U}^{0}v
or
N_{\mu}v
may denote the product of
v
with any component of
{\rm U}^{0}(t)
or
N_{\mu}(t)
.
Similarly, for every
t\in(0,T/\varepsilon)
,

\left.\begin{aligned} \|(\nabla{\rm U}^{0})v\|_{H^{l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|(\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U}^{0})v\|_{H^{l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}&\leq C(\Theta)\|v\|_{H^{l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\\
\|(\nabla N_{\mu})v\|_{H^{l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|(\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}N_{\mu})v\|_{H^{l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}&\leq C(\Theta)\|v\|_{H^{l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\end{aligned}\right\}\quad\mbox{ for }l\leq L-1.

(3.27)

Remark 6
.

if
X
is a function space, we will say that a function
{\rm U}
is an
X
-multiplier
if

\|{\rm U}\psi\|_{X}\leq C\|\psi\|_{X}\qquad\mbox{ for all }\psi\in X.

For a vector-valued
{\rm U}
, we understand the left-hand side to mean the product of any component of
{\rm U}
with
\psi
. In all that follows, the constant for an
X
-multiplier will depend only on
\Theta
from (
1.11
).
It is easy to see that every
W^{k,\infty}
function is a
W^{k,\infty}_{\gamma}
multiplier, so the lemma implies that for every
t\in(0,T/\varepsilon)
,

\left.\begin{aligned} &({\rm U}^{0},\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U}^{0},\nabla{\rm U}^{0})(\cdot,t)\\
&(N_{\mu},\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}N_{\mu},\nabla N_{\mu})(\cdot,t)\end{aligned}\ \ \right\}\quad\mbox{ are $X^{l,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})$ -multipliers for $l\leq L-1$.}

Thus

\left.\begin{aligned} &({\rm U}^{0},\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U}^{0},\nabla{\rm U}^{0})\\
&(N_{\mu},\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}N_{\mu},\nabla N_{\mu})\end{aligned}\ \ \right\}\quad\mbox{ are $L^{\infty}_{T/\varepsilon}X^{l,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})$ -multipliers for $l\leq L-1$.}

Proof.

Note that the
{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
components of
{\rm U}^{0}
and
N_{\mu}
,
i.e.

{\rm U}^{0}_{u}(y)=\binom{\varphi}{\mathcal{A}_{a}}(y_{a};q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))\qquad N_{\mu,u}(y)=n_{\mu}(y_{a};q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))

all have the form

f(q)(y):=f(y_{a};q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))

(3.28)

for a function
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times M_{N}\to{\mathbb{C}}
or
{\mathbb{R}}
such that

|\partial^{r}f(y_{a},q)|\leq C(1+|y_{a}|)^{-|r|}

where
r
is a multiindex denoting differentiation with respect to both
y_{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and
q\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2N}\cong M_{N}
.
In Lemma
24
in Appendix
A.4
, we establish that such functions satisfy estimates (
3.25
), (
3.26
), subject to hypotheses (
1.9
), (
1.10
).

The other components of
{\rm U}^{0}
and
N_{\mu}
have the form

\partial_{\alpha}q(y_{\bar{a}})f(q)(y),\qquad\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d.

(3.29)

for
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times M_{N}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that
|\partial^{r}f(y_{a},q)|\leq C(1+|y_{a}|)^{-1-|r|}
, when
q\in K
compact
\subset M_{N}
.
Indeed, it follows from (
3.14
) and (
2.15
) that

{\rm U}^{0}_{\alpha}(y)=\partial_{\alpha}q^{\lambda}(y_{\bar{a}})\chi_{\lambda}(y_{a};q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})),\qquad\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d

and every
\chi_{\lambda}
has the required decay, see Lemma
4
.

Similarly, the
\alpha
components of
N_{\mu}
are defined
as the solutions
\xi_{\mu,\alpha}
of equation (
3.24
). Recall that, due to the choice (
3.14
) of
{\rm A}^{0}_{\alpha}
, we can rewrite
{\rm D}_{{\rm A}^{0}_{\alpha}}\varphi(y)
, which appears on the right-hand side of (
3.24
), as
\partial_{\alpha}q^{\nu}(y_{\bar{a}})\,\tilde{n}_{\nu,\phi}(y_{a};q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))
. From this one deduces that
\xi_{\mu,\alpha}
can be written as

\xi_{\mu,\alpha}(y)=\partial_{\alpha}q^{\nu}(y_{\bar{a}})\xi_{\mu\nu}(y_{a};q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))

where
\xi_{\mu\nu}(\cdot,q)
solves, for every
q\in M_{N}
,

(-\Delta_{a}+|\varphi(q)|^{2})\xi_{\mu\nu}=2(in_{\mu,\phi}(\cdot,q),\tilde{n}_{\nu,\phi}(\cdot;q))\qquad\mbox{ in }{\mathbb{R}}^{2}.

Regularity and decay properties of
n_{\mu}
imply that the right-hand side is smooth, with

|\partial^{r}(in_{\mu,\phi},\tilde{n}_{\nu,\phi})|(y_{a},q)\ \leq C(K,r)e^{-\gamma|y_{a}|}\qquad\mbox{ whenever }q\in K

for
\gamma<1
, so it follows from Lemma
21
that

\|\xi_{\mu\nu}(\cdot,q)\|_{W^{k,\infty}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\leq C(k,K)\qquad\mbox{ whenever }q\in K.

It is proved in Lemma
24
that functions of the form
10
10

10

Lemma
24
proves (
3.30
) only for
\alpha=\bar{a}=3,\ldots,d
, but the
proof also shows that the same inequality holds for
\alpha=0
.
shown in (
3.29
) satisfy

\|\partial_{\alpha}qf(q)v\|_{H^{l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C\|v\|_{H^{l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})},\qquad\mbox{ for }l\leq L,

(3.30)

completing the verification of (
3.26
). Estimate (

) for the
\alpha
components of
{\rm U}^{0}
and

follows easily from conclusion (

) of Lemma

and the embedding
H^{L}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})\hookrightarrow W^{L-j,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})
for

.

The verification of (

) is very similar. The main point is that every component of

\nabla{\rm U}^{0},\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U}^{0},\nabla N_{\mu},\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}N_{\mu}
either has the form (

) or(

), or else the form

\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}q(y_{\bar{a}})\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\beta}q(y_{\bar{a}})f(q)(y)\qquad\mbox{ or }\quad\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha\beta}q(y_{\bar{a}})f(q)(y).

∎

Lemma 8
.

Assume (
1.9
) and (
1.10
), and let
j>\frac{d}{2}-1
. Then
{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}]
satisfies

\|{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}]\|_{L^{\infty}_{T/\varepsilon}X^{L-1,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}]\|_{L^{\infty}_{T/\varepsilon}X^{L-2,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C(\Theta)

(3.31)

for every
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

Proof.

We first consider
{\rm S}^{1}_{u}[{\rm U}^{0}]
. Recall that the choice of
{\rm A}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d
implies that

\binom{{\rm D}_{\alpha}\varphi}{{\rm F}_{\alpha a}}(y)=\partial_{\alpha}q^{\mu}(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})\,\tilde{n}_{\mu}(y_{a};q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))=\partial_{\alpha}q^{\mu}\,n_{\mu}(q)(y)

It follows that

\displaystyle{\rm S}^{1}_{u}[{\rm U}^{0}]

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}+\kappa_{1})(\partial_{0}q^{\mu}\,\tilde{n}_{\mu}(q))-\partial_{\bar{a}}(\partial_{\bar{a}}q^{\mu}\tilde{n}_{\mu}(q))

\displaystyle-i(\kappa_{0}{\rm A}_{0}\,\partial_{0}q^{\mu}-i{\rm A}_{\bar{a}}\partial_{\bar{a}}q^{\mu})\binom{\tilde{n}_{\mu,\phi}(q)}{0}.

Using (
3.1
) and smoothness and decay
properties of
n_{\mu}(\cdot,q)
, see Lemma
4
, it is straightforward to verify that the
u
components of
{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}]
satisfy (
3.31
), for example by arguing along the lines of the proof of Lemma
25
.

The other components have the form

{\rm S}^{1}_{\alpha}[{\rm U}^{0}]=(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}+\kappa_{1}){\rm F}_{0\alpha}-\partial_{\bar{a}}{\rm F}_{\bar{a}\alpha},\qquad\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d.

(3.32)

So we need to understand regularity and decay properties of
{\rm F}_{\alpha\beta}
for
\alpha,\beta\in\{0,3,\ldots,d\}
. From the definitions,

\displaystyle{\rm F}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}(\partial_{\beta}q^{\mu}\chi_{\mu}(q))-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\beta}(\partial_{\alpha}q^{\mu}\chi_{\mu}(q))

(3.33)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\beta}q^{\mu}\,\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}q^{\nu}\,\partial_{\nu}\chi_{\mu}(q)-\partial_{\alpha}q^{\mu}\,\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\beta}q^{\nu}\,\partial_{\nu}\chi_{\mu}(q)

\displaystyle=\partial_{\beta}q^{\mu}\,\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}q^{\nu}\,(\partial_{\nu}\chi_{\mu}(q)-\partial_{\mu}\chi_{\nu}(q)).

We claim that

(\partial_{\nu}\chi_{\mu}-\partial_{\mu}\chi_{\nu})(q)\in W^{k,\infty}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\qquad\mbox{ for every $k\in{\mathbb{N}}$ and }\gamma\in(0,1),

(3.34)

with uniform estimates for
q
in a compact subset
K\subset M_{N}
.
To see this, note from the definitions (
2.13
) that
(\partial_{\nu}\chi_{\mu}-\partial_{\mu}\chi_{\nu})(\cdot,q)
solves

\displaystyle(-\Delta+|\varphi(q)|^{2})(\partial_{\nu}\chi_{\mu}-\partial_{\mu}\chi_{\nu})

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}|\varphi|^{2}\chi_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}|\varphi|^{2}\chi_{\mu}+

\displaystyle\partial_{\nu}(i\varphi,\partial_{\mu}\varphi)-\partial_{\mu}(i\varphi,\partial_{\nu}\varphi)

in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
. Lemma
21
implies that (
3.34
) will follow if show that the right-hand side belongs to
W^{k,\infty}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
for every
k
. This is the case for

\partial_{\mu}|\varphi|^{2}\chi_{\nu}-\partial_{\nu}|\varphi|^{2}\chi_{\mu}
, in view of Lemma
4
. The other terms are clearly smooth, so we only need to check exponential decay of all derivatives. To see this, first note that

\partial_{\nu}(i\varphi,\partial_{\mu}\varphi)-\partial_{\mu}(i\varphi,\partial_{\nu}\varphi)=(i\partial_{\nu}\varphi,\partial_{\mu}\varphi)-(i\partial_{\mu}\varphi,\partial_{\nu}\varphi)=2(i\partial_{\nu}\varphi,\partial_{\mu}\varphi).

For
|y_{a}|
large enough (depending on
q
)
we can write
\varphi(y_{a};q)=\rho(y_{a};q)e^{i\theta(y_{a};q)}
for
\rho:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to[0,\infty)
and
\theta:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}
smooth. One then checks that

(i\partial_{\nu}\varphi,\partial_{\mu}\varphi)=\rho\partial_{\mu}\rho\partial_{\nu}\theta-\rho\partial_{\nu}\rho\partial_{\mu}\theta

It is easy to see from the form (
2.4
) of
\varphi(q)

that
\partial_{\mu}\theta(y_{a})\in W^{k,\infty}
and all its derivatives bounded (in fact decaying algebraically) as
|y_{a}|\to\infty
,
so the desired exponential decay again follows from Lemma
4
. This completes the proof of
(
3.34
).

It follows from (
3.33
) and (
3.34
), along the same lines as the proof of Lemma
25
, that

\|{\rm F}_{\alpha\beta}\|_{L^{\infty}H^{L}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\|{\rm F}_{\alpha\beta}\|_{L^{\infty}W^{L-j,\infty}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C(K,\Theta).

Recalling (
3.1
), this and (
3.32
) imply the conclusion of the lemma.
∎

3.3.
First step of the construction

With these preliminaries, we now construct approximate solutions by an induction argument.

We first assume that
k_{1}=L-4m>\frac{d}{2}
, see
(
3.6
), and
we seek
{\rm U}^{1}
such that
{\rm U}_{1,\varepsilon}={\rm U}^{0}+\varepsilon^{2}{\rm U}^{1}
satisfies

\|{\rm S}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\|_{X^{L-4,j}_{\gamma}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm S}^{1}_{\varepsilon}\|_{X^{L-5,j}_{\gamma}}\leq C\varepsilon^{4}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ for }{{\rm S}^{1}_{\varepsilon}}:={\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{1,\varepsilon}]

(3.35)

and

\|{\rm U}^{1}\|_{X^{L-3,j}_{\gamma}}+\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U}^{1}\|_{X^{L-4,j}_{\gamma}}\leq C

(3.36)

where we recall that
X^{k,j}_{\gamma}=H^{k}\cap W^{k-j,\infty}_{\gamma}
, see (
1.21
) and (
1.22
), and all
constants may depend on
K,\Theta
and
\gamma
.

Using formulas in Section
3.2
above and Lemma
5
, we compute

{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{1,\varepsilon}]=\varepsilon^{2}\left(\ell^{0}[{\rm U}^{0}]({\rm U}^{1})+{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}]\right)+\varepsilon^{4}P

(3.37)

where
P
is a polynomial in
\varepsilon,{\rm Z}^{0}
and
{\rm Z}^{1}
, with
{\rm Z}^{j}:=({\rm U}^{j},\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U}^{j},\nabla{\rm U}^{j})
. Indeed,
from (
3.19
) and (
3.20
) we have

P=p^{0}_{2}+\varepsilon^{2}p^{0}_{3}+\varepsilon^{2}p^{1}_{2}+\varepsilon^{4}p^{1}_{3}.

(3.38)

with
p^{m}_{j}
of order
j
in
{\rm Z}^{1}
.

We will choose
{\rm U}^{1}
so that the coefficient of
\varepsilon^{2}
vanishes and (
3.36
) holds. It will then be a straightforward matter to verify (
3.35
).

To find
{\rm U}^{1}
, we start by choosing the
{\rm u}^{1}=\binom{\Phi^{1}}{{\rm A}^{1}_{a}}
components so that

\ell^{0}_{u}[{\rm U}^{0}]({\rm U}^{1})+{\rm S}^{1}_{u}[{\rm U}^{0}]=0.

(3.39)

We will solve for the
{\rm A}^{1}_{\alpha}
components for
\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d
at a second stage.
In view of (
3.21
), we can rewrite (
3.39
) as

{\mathcal{L}}[{\rm u}^{0}]({\rm u}^{1})+{\rm S}^{1}_{0,u}=0\qquad\mbox{ for }{\rm S}^{1}_{0}:={\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}].

(3.40)

Lemma 9
.

{\rm S}^{1}_{0,u}(\cdot,y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
is orthogonal to all the zero modes
n_{\mu}(\cdot;q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))
for every

(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
.

Proof.

The lemma follows directly from Lemma
3
and the assumption (
1.8
)
that
q
solves (
1
). Indeed, Lemma
3

implies that for any
\alpha,\beta\in\{0,3,\ldots,d\}
,

\displaystyle\left(\binom{{\rm D}_{\alpha}{\rm D}_{\beta}\Phi}{\partial_{\alpha}{\rm F}_{\beta a}},\tilde{n}_{\mu}\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\left(\partial_{\alpha}q^{\nu}\partial_{\beta}q^{\lambda}\binom{{\mathcal{D}}_{\nu}{\mathcal{D}}_{\lambda}\Phi}{\partial_{\nu}{\mathcal{F}}_{\lambda a}},\tilde{n}_{\mu}\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\alpha}q^{\nu}\partial_{\beta}q^{\lambda}\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{\nu}}\partial_{\lambda},\partial_{\mu})_{g}=(\nabla^{g}_{\alpha}\partial_{\beta}q,\partial_{\mu})_{g}

for any
\mu
, where
\partial_{\alpha}q=\partial_{\alpha}q^{\lambda}\partial_{\lambda}
and

\nabla^{g}_{\alpha}=\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{\alpha}q}=\partial_{\alpha}q^{\lambda}\nabla^{g}_{\partial_{\lambda}}
.

Thus

\displaystyle({\rm S}^{1}_{0,u},\tilde{n}_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\kappa_{0}\left(\binom{\kappa_{0}{\rm D}_{0}{\rm D}_{0}\Phi}{\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm F}_{0a}},\tilde{n}_{\mu}\right)+\kappa_{1}\left(\binom{\kappa_{0}{\rm D}_{0}\Phi}{{\rm F}_{0a}},\tilde{n}_{\mu}\right)-\left(\binom{{\rm D}_{\bar{a}}{\rm D}_{\bar{a}}}{\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}{\rm F}_{\bar{a}a}},\tilde{n}_{\mu}\right)

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}\nabla^{g}_{0}\partial_{0}q+\kappa_{1}\partial_{0}q-\nabla^{g}_{\bar{a}}\partial_{\bar{a}}q,\partial_{\mu})_{g}\overset{\eqref{dwm}}{=}0.

∎

Lemma 10
.

{\rm S}^{1}_{0,u}(\cdot,y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
satisfies the gauge-orthogonality condition (
2.11
) for every

(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
.

Proof.

It follows from computations in Section
3.2
that

{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}^{0}]={\rm S}^{0}[{\rm U}^{0}]+\varepsilon^{2}{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}]=\varepsilon^{2}{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}].

It then follows from the algebraic identity in Lemma
1
, rescaled to the
y
variables,
that

({\rm S}^{1}_{\phi}[{\rm U}^{0}],i\Phi)-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}]=-\varepsilon^{2}\big((\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}+\kappa_{1}){\rm S}_{0}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}]-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}{\rm S}_{\bar{a}}[{\rm U}^{0}]\big).

The lemma states that the left-hand side vanishes, which follows by taking the limit as
\varepsilon\to 0
of the above identity. Alternately, one can directly verify that the right-hand side vanishes for every
\varepsilon>0
.
∎

In view of Lemma
9
as well as Lemma
22
in Appendix
A
, for every
(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
we can find
{\rm u}^{1}(\cdot,y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}):{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2}

L[\mathfrak{u}(q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))]({\rm u}^{1}(\cdot,y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))+{\rm S}^{1}_{0,u}(\cdot,y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})\qquad\mbox{ in }{\mathbb{R}}^{2}.

Lemmas
22
and
10
imply that

{\rm u}^{1}(\cdot,y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
satisfies the gauge orthogonality condition (
2.11
).
It follows that

{\mathcal{L}}[\mathfrak{u}(q)]({\rm u}^{1})=L[\mathfrak{u}(q)]({\rm u}^{1})

and hence that
{\rm u}^{1}
solves (
3.40
).

Now it follows from estimates of
{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}]
in Lemma
8
and the elliptic estimates in Proposition
7

that

\|{\rm u}^{1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}X^{L-1,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm u}^{1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}X^{L-2,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C.

(3.41)

To complete the construction of
{\rm U}^{1}
it now suffices to choose the remaining components such that

\ell^{0}_{\alpha}[{\rm U}^{0}]({\rm U}^{1})+{\rm S}^{1}_{\alpha}[{\rm U}^{0}]=0\qquad\qquad\mbox{ for }\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d.

This equation states that

(-\partial_{a}\partial_{a}+|\varphi(q)|^{2}){\rm A}^{1}_{\alpha}=-\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{a}{\rm A}^{1}_{a}-(i\Phi^{1},{\rm D}_{{\rm A}^{0}_{\alpha}}\varphi(q))-(i\varphi(q)),{\rm D}_{{\rm A}^{0}_{\alpha}}\Phi^{1})-{\rm S}^{1}_{\alpha}[{\rm U}^{0}].

(3.42)

The right-hand side contains only components of
{\rm U}^{0}
and
{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}]
, as well as components
\Phi^{1},{\rm A}^{1}_{a}
of
{\rm U}^{1}
that have
already been found. Writing
rhs
to denote the right-hand side,
it follows from Lemma
7
, Lemma
8
and (
3.41
)
that

\|\mbox{rhs}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}X^{L-3,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}(\mbox{rhs})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}X^{L-4,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C.

(3.43)

It follows from Proposition
6
that (
3.42
) has a unique solution that satisfies

\|{\rm A}^{1}_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}X^{L-3,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm A}^{1}_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}X^{L-4,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}C(\Theta,K,\gamma).

(3.44)

Thus we have completed the construction of
{\rm U}^{1}
satisfying equation (
3.39
) and estimates (
3.36
).

Finally, to verify (
3.35
), note from (
3.37
) and (
3.38
) that (
3.39
) implies that

{\rm S}_{\varepsilon}={\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{1,\varepsilon}]=\varepsilon^{4}p^{0}_{2}+\varepsilon^{6}(p^{1}_{2}+p^{0}_{3})+\varepsilon^{8}p^{1}_{3}

(3.45)

where
p^{l}_{j}
are implicitly defined in (
3.20
). Every term of
p^{l}_{j}
is a product of
j
components
of
{\rm Z}^{1}=({\rm U}^{1},\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U}^{1},\nabla{\rm U}^{1})
and at most
3-j
components of

{\rm Z}^{0}=({\rm U}^{0},\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U}^{0},\nabla{\rm U}^{0})
. We readily deduce estimates of norms of
{\rm Z}^{1}
from (
3.36
).
It follows from Lemma
7
that multiplication by
{\rm Z}^{0}
preserves membership in
X^{k,j}_{\gamma}
for
\frac{d}{2}<k\leq L-1
,
so it follows that

\|p^{l}_{j}\|_{X^{L-4,j}_{\gamma}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}p^{l}_{j}\|_{X^{L-5,j}_{\gamma}}\leq C(\|{\rm Z}^{1}\|_{X^{L-4,j}_{\gamma}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm Z}^{1}\|_{X^{L-5,j}_{\gamma}})\leq C.

(Note that we have used (
3.1
).)
This and (
3.45
) imply (
3.35
).

3.4.
completion of proof

We now complete the proof by induction on
m
, with
L
fixed. The base case is provided by section
3.3
.

We will take our approximate solution
{\rm U}_{1,\varepsilon}
to equal
{\rm U}^{0}+\varepsilon^{2}{\rm U}^{1}
,
and for
m\geq 2
we will take
{\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}
to have the form

{\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}={\rm U}^{0}+\varepsilon^{2}({\rm U}^{1}+c^{2}_{\mu}N_{\mu})+\cdots+\varepsilon^{2(m-1)}({\rm U}^{m-1}+c^{m}_{\mu}N_{\mu})+\varepsilon^{2m}{\rm U}^{m}.

(3.46)

The induction hypothesis is that for
m\geq 1
such that

k_{m}:=L-4m>d/2,

there exists
T_{0}\in(0,T]
and
functions

{\rm U}^{m}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times[0,T_{0})\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
and

c^{m}_{\mu}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times[0,T_{0})\to{\mathbb{R}}
for

\mu=1,\ldots,2N
, such that
the following estimates hold for
m\geq 2
:

\|c^{m}_{\mu}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}H^{k_{m}+2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}c^{m}_{\mu}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}H^{k_{m}+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}\leq C,

(3.47)

\|{\rm U}^{m}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}+2,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U}^{m}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}+1,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C,

(3.48)

and such that the error of approximation
{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{\varepsilon,m}]

admits an expansion

{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{\varepsilon,m}]=\varepsilon^{2(m+1)}\sum_{j=0}^{2m}\varepsilon^{2j}{\rm S}_{m,j}

(3.49)

where every
{\rm S}_{m,j}(y_{a},y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
is a function independent of
\varepsilon
such that
for every
m,j

\|{\rm S}_{m,j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm S}_{m,j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}-1,j}_{\gamma}}\leq C.

(3.50)

(Note that (
3.48
) does not hold for
m=1
. However, the weaker estimate established in (
3.36
) suffices for our construction.)

We assume that
k_{m+1}>\frac{d}{2}
, see (
3.6
), and we will construct
c_{\mu}^{m+1}
and
{\rm U}^{m+1}

such that the induction hypotheses hold with
m
replaced by
m+1
.

We first define

{\rm V}_{m,\varepsilon}={\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}+c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu}

for functions
c^{m+1}_{\mu}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times(0,T)\to{\mathbb{R}}
that will be chosen to satisfy an orthogonality condition, as specified in the following lemma.

Lemma 11
.

There exists
T_{0}\in(0,T)
such that for every
m\geq 1
such that
k_{m}>d/2
and every
\mu=1,\ldots,2N
, there exist
c^{m+1}_{\mu}\in C(0,T_{0},H^{k_{m}+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}))
such that
{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm V}_{\varepsilon,m}]
admits an expansion

{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm V}_{\varepsilon,m}]=\varepsilon^{2(m+1)}\sum_{j=0}^{2m}\varepsilon^{2j}{\rm S}^{m}_{j}

(3.51)

with the
u
-components of the leading term orthogonal to the zero modes:

({\rm S}^{m}_{0,u},n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}=0\qquad\mbox{ for }\mu=1,\ldots,2N,\quad\mbox{ for every }(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})\ .

(3.52)

Moreover,
\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}^{j}c^{m+1}_{\mu}\in C(0,T_{0},H^{k_{m}+1-j}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})
for
j=1,2
,
with

\|c^{m+1}_{\mu}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}H^{k_{m}+1}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}c^{m+1}_{\mu}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}H^{k_{m}}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}c^{m+1}_{\mu}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}H^{k_{m}-1}}\leq C,

(3.53)

and every
{\rm S}^{m}_{j}
satisfies the estimate

\|{\rm S}^{m}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm S}^{m}_{j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}-1,j}_{\gamma}}\leq C

(3.54)

Proof.

Tp establish the lemma, we will show that the orthogonality condition (
3.52
) reduces to a system of equations for
c^{m+1}_{\mu}
for
\mu=1,\ldots,2N
, see (
3.68
). We then prove that this system has a solution satisfying the estimates in (
3.53
), and that (
3.54
) follows.

In carrying out this argument, we will prove a priori estimates for
c^{m+1}_{\mu}
satisfying (
3.47
), for some
T_{0}>0
to be determined. These estimates will imply existence of
c^{m+1}_{\mu}
with the stated properties, via a fixed-point argument.

Using computations from Section
3.2
above, we find that

\displaystyle{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm V}_{m,\varepsilon}]

\displaystyle={\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{2m}c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu}]

(3.55)

\displaystyle={\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}]+\varepsilon^{2m}\ell^{0}[{\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}](c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})

\displaystyle+\varepsilon^{2m+2}\ell^{1}[{\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}](c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})+\varepsilon^{4m}P_{m,\varepsilon}

where
P_{\varepsilon}
is a polynomial in
{\rm Z}_{m,\varepsilon},\tilde{\rm Z}^{m+1}
and
\varepsilon
, for

\displaystyle{\rm Z}_{m,\varepsilon}

\displaystyle=({\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon},\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon},\nabla{\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}),

(3.56)

\displaystyle\tilde{\rm Z}^{m+1}

\displaystyle=(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu},\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu}),\nabla(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})).

This follows directly from (
3.18
) and (
3.20
), with
P_{m,\varepsilon}=p^{0}_{2}+\varepsilon^{2}p^{1}_{2}+\varepsilon^{2m}p^{0}_{3}+\varepsilon^{2m+2}p^{1}_{3}
.

We now expand the right-hand side of (
3.55
) as a polynomial in
\varepsilon
, with coefficients that are
functions of
y
. The expansion of
{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}]
is given in (
3.49
). From the definition (
3.21
), we see that the coefficients of the operator
\ell^{0}[{\rm U}]
are polynomials, at most quadratic, in
{\rm Z}=({\rm U},\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U},\nabla{\rm U})
. By expanding, we thus find certain linear operators
\Lambda^{0}_{j}
whose coefficients are polynomials, at most quadratic, in
{\rm Z}^{j}=({\rm U}^{j},\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm U}^{j},\nabla{\rm U}^{j})

for
j=0,\ldots,m
and
\tilde{\rm Z}^{j}
, defined as in (
3.56
), for
j=2,\ldots,m
, such that

\displaystyle\ell^{0}[{\rm U}_{\varepsilon,m}](c_{\mu}^{m+1}N_{\mu})

\displaystyle=\ell^{0}[{\rm U}^{0}](c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})+\sum_{j=1}^{2m}\varepsilon^{2j}\Lambda^{0}_{j}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})

(3.57)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{2m}\varepsilon^{2j}\Lambda^{0}_{j}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})

by Lemma
6
.

We claim that
\Lambda^{0}_{j}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})(\cdot,t)\in X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}=H^{k_{m}}\cap W^{k_{m}-j,\infty}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
for every
t\in(0,T_{0})
, and

\|\Lambda^{0}_{j}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})(\cdot,t)\|_{X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C\Theta_{1}(t)

(3.58)

for

\Theta_{1}(t):=\|c^{m+1}_{\mu}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{k_{m}+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}c^{m+1}_{\mu}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{k_{m}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}

(3.59)

for every
t\in(0,T_{0})
.
This is clear, since
\Lambda^{0}_{j}
is a first-order differential operator with coefficients that are products of functions that either belong to
X^{k_{m}+1,j}_{\gamma}\subset X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}
, which is an algebra, or are
X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}
multipliers. This follows from the induction hypothesis, Lemma
7
and Remark
6
.

Similarly, we can expand

\ell^{1}[{\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}](c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})=\ell^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}](c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})+\sum_{j=1}^{2m}\varepsilon^{2j}\Lambda^{1}_{j}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})

for first-order linear operators
\Lambda^{1}_{j}
, and

P_{m,\varepsilon}=\sum_{j=0}^{m}\varepsilon^{2j}P_{m,j}.

Recalling that the polynomial terms are at most cubic in
c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu}
, we find by the same considerations that lead to (
3.58
) that

\|\Lambda^{1}_{j}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu}(\cdot,t))\|_{X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C\Theta_{1}(t)

(3.60)

and

\displaystyle\|P_{m,j}(\cdot,t)\|_{X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\Theta_{1}(t)+\Theta_{1}(t)^{3}\right)

(3.61)

for
t\in(0,T_{0})
.
Combining these, keeping track of the powers of
\varepsilon
that appear in the expansion, and noting that
4m=2(m+1)
when
m=1
, we find that (
3.51
) holds, with

{\rm S}^{m}_{0}=\begin{cases}\ell^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}](c^{2}_{\mu}N_{\mu})+\Lambda^{0}_{1}(c^{2}_{\mu}N_{\mu})+{\rm S}_{1,0}+P_{1,0}&\mbox{ if }m=1\\
\ell^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}](c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})+\Lambda^{0}_{1}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})+{\rm S}_{m,0}&\mbox{ if }m\geq 2\end{cases}

(3.62)

and for
j\geq 1
,

{\rm S}^{m}_{j}=\mbox{a sum of certain }S_{m,k_{0}},\ \Lambda^{0}_{k_{1}}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu}),\ \Lambda^{1}_{k_{2}}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu}),\ P_{m,k_{3}}

(3.63)

for
k_{0},\ldots,k_{3}
depending on
m,j
in a way that will not matter for us.

We next consider the orthogonality condition (
3.52
).
Using the definitions (
3.22
), (
3.23
) of
\ell^{1}
,
we check that

\ell^{1}_{\phi}[{\rm U}^{0}](c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})=[(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}+\kappa_{1}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}})c^{m+1}_{\mu}]n_{\mu,\phi}+\mbox{Tri}

(3.64)

where
Tri
denotes a trilinear function of
({\rm Z}^{0},{\rm Z}^{0},\tilde{\rm Z}^{m+1})
. Arguing as above,
we see that

\displaystyle\|\mbox{Tri}\|_{X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\Theta_{1}(t)

(3.65)

Similarly, recalling that
\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}c^{m+1}_{\mu}=0
,

\displaystyle\ell^{1}_{a}[{\rm U}^{0}](c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}+\kappa_{1})\left(\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}n_{\mu,a})-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu,0})\right)

(3.66)

\displaystyle-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}\left(\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}n_{\mu,a})-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu,\bar{a}})\right)

\displaystyle=[(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}+\kappa_{1}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}})c^{m+1}_{\mu}]n_{\mu,a}+\mbox{Bi}

where
Bi
denotes a bilinear function of
(c^{m+1}_{\mu},\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}c^{m+1}_{\mu},\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}c^{m+1}_{\mu})
and terms
drawn from

N_{\mu},\ \nabla N_{\mu},\,\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}N_{\mu},\ \kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\nabla N_{\mu},\ \kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}N_{\mu},\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}\nabla N_{\mu}.

In addition,
\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}N_{\mu}
appears with a coefficient of
\kappa_{0}
except when multiplying
\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}c^{m+1}_{\mu}
.
As above
11
11

11

The constant diverges as
\kappa_{0}\searrow 0
but it finite for
\kappa_{0}=0
. This is due to the presence of an unfavorable term
\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}c^{m+1}_{\mu}\cdot\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}n_{\mu,a}
, which we can only say is bounded by
\kappa_{0}^{-1}\Theta(\kappa_{0}\|c^{m+1}_{\mu}\|_{H^{k_{m}+2}})
, but which vanishes when

.

\displaystyle\|\mbox{Bi}\|_{X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\Theta_{1}(t)

(3.67)

We thus obtain

\ell^{1}_{u}[{\rm U}^{0}](c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})=[(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}+\kappa_{1}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}})c^{m+1}_{\mu}]n_{\mu}+\mbox{Bi}+\mbox{Tri},

with (
3.67
), (

) holding.

Recalling that
\{n_{\mu}(\cdot;q)\}_{\mu=1}^{2N}
are orthonormal in

for every

,
we deduce from the above that the orthogonality condition (

) holds if and only if

(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}+\kappa_{1}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}})c^{m+1}_{\nu}=rhs_{\nu},\qquad\nu=1,\ldots,2N

(3.68)

for

rhs_{\nu}(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}):=\big((RHS),n_{\nu}(q))_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})}

with

RHS=\begin{cases}-S_{1,0,u}-\Lambda^{0}_{1,u}(c^{2}_{\mu}N_{\mu})-Bi-Tri-P_{1,0,u}&\mbox{if }m=1\\
-S_{m,0,u}-\Lambda^{0}_{1,u}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})-Bi-Tri&\mbox{if }m\geq 2.\end{cases}

Note in addition that for

,

is a linear function of

and its first derivatives, and for
m=1
it has polynomial nonlinearities that are at most cubic.

Since
X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}\subset H^{k_{m}}
, we see from
(
3.50
), (
3.58
), (
3.60
), and (
3.61
)
that

\|RHS(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{k_{m}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C(1+\Theta_{1}(t)+\delta_{m1}\Theta_{1}(t)^{3}).

where
\delta_{m1}
is a Kronecker delta. Since
n_{\mu}
is a
H^{k_{m}}
multiplier, see Lemma
7
, we know that
\|RHS\cdot n_{\mu}(q)(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{k_{m}}}\leq C\|RHS(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{k_{m}}}
. Lemma
26
in the appendix implies that

\displaystyle\|rhs(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{k_{m}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\|RHS(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{k_{m}}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\Theta_{1}(t)+\delta_{m1}\Theta_{1}(t)^{3}).

(3.69)

For initial conditions
c^{m+1}_{\nu}=\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}c^{m+1}_{\nu}=0
, standard theory guarantees that (
3.68
), (
3.69
) has a unique solution on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times[0,T_{0})
for some
T_{0}\in(0,T]
, satisfying

\|c^{m+1}_{\mu}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}H^{k_{m}+1}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}c^{m+1}_{\mu}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}H^{k_{m}}}\leq C.

Moreover,
T_{0}
admits a lower bound that depends only on

.
We review this theory for the nonlinear case
m=1
in Lemma
23
, in Appendix
A.3
. The linear case is very similar. Note that it is only for
m=1
that the equation (
3.68
) is nonlinear and we may have to decrease the interval of existence
T_{0}
.

It follows from (
3.68
) that
\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}c^{m+1}_{\mu}
has the same regularity as
\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}c^{m+1}_{\mu}
, so (
3.53
) follows by
taking
T_{0}
smaller if necessary. With control over
\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}c^{m+1}_{\mu}
and over
\Theta_{1}(t),0<t<T_{0}
, we can go back and, by the same reasoning used to
deduce (
3.58
), (
3.60
), (
3.61
),
conclude that

\left.\begin{aligned} \|\Lambda^{0}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\Lambda^{0}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}-1,j}_{\gamma}}\\
\|\Lambda^{1}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\Lambda^{1}(c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}-1,j}_{\gamma}}\\
\|P_{m,j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}P_{m,j}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}-1,j}_{\gamma}}\end{aligned}\right\}\leq C.

Then (
3.54
) follows immediately from these estimates, (
3.50
) and (
3.63
).

∎

Having fixed
c^{m+1}_{\mu}
and hence
{\rm V}_{\varepsilon,m}
and
{\rm S}^{m}_{j},j=0,\ldots,2m
, we now establish another
important property of
{\rm S}^{m}_{0}
.

Lemma 12
.

{\rm S}^{m}_{0,u}(\cdot,y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})=\binom{{\rm S}^{m}_{0,\phi}}{{\rm S}^{m}_{0,a}}(\cdot,y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
satisfies the gauge-orthogonality condition (
2.11
) for every
(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
.

Proof.

As with Lemma
10
, we start from the algebraic identity in Lemma
1
, which after rescaling to the
y
variables implies that

0=({\rm S}^{\varepsilon}_{\phi}[{\rm V}_{\varepsilon,m}],i\Phi_{\varepsilon,m})-\partial_{a}{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}_{a}[{\rm V}_{\varepsilon,m}]+\varepsilon^{2}\left(-(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}+\kappa_{1}){\rm S}^{\varepsilon}_{0}[{\rm V}_{\varepsilon,m}]-\partial_{\bar{a}}{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}_{\bar{a}}[{\rm V}_{\varepsilon,m}]\right)

Now we use (
3.51
) and (
3.46
) to expand this identity in powers of
\varepsilon
.
This yields

0=\varepsilon^{2m+2}\left((S^{m}_{0,\phi},i\varphi(q))-\partial_{a}{\rm S}^{m}_{0,a}\right)+\varepsilon^{2m+4}\big(\ \cdots\ \big)+\cdots

Since this identity holds for every
\varepsilon
and the coefficient functions are independent of
\varepsilon
, it follows that

(S^{m}_{0,\phi},i\varphi(q))-\partial_{a}{\rm S}^{m}_{0,a}=0

which is what we need to prove.
∎

We are now ready to complete the induction argument. We must find
{\rm U}^{m+1}
such that

{\rm U}_{m+1,\varepsilon}={\rm U}_{m,\varepsilon}+\varepsilon^{2m}c_{\mu}^{m+1}N_{\mu}+\varepsilon^{2m+2}{\rm U}^{m+1}={\rm V}_{\varepsilon,m}+\varepsilon^{2m+2}{\rm U}^{m+1}

satisfies the conclusions of the proposition.
Using (
3.49
) and computations from Section
3.2
above, we compute

{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{m+1,\varepsilon}]=\varepsilon^{2m+2}\left({\rm S}^{m}_{0}+\ell^{0}[{\rm U}^{0}]({\rm U}^{m+1})\right)+\varepsilon^{2m+4}\sum_{j=0}^{2(m+1)}\varepsilon^{2j}{\rm S}_{m+1,j}.

(3.70)

The sum on the right-hand side gathers all the terms arising from
the polynomial terms
p^{m}_{j}
for
m=0,1
and
j=2,3
, appearing in (
3.20
), together with

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2m+2}\sum_{j=1}^{2m}\varepsilon^{2j}{\rm S}^{m}_{j}\qquad\mbox{ from \eqref{wSe.expand}}

(3.71)

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2m+4}\ell^{1}[{\rm V}_{\varepsilon,m}]({\rm U}^{m+1}),

\displaystyle\varepsilon^{2m+2}\left(\ell^{0}[{\rm V}_{\varepsilon,m}]({\rm U}^{m+1})-\ell^{0}[{\rm U}^{0}]({\rm U}^{m+1})\right).

For the last of these, notice that by expanding
\ell^{0}[{\rm V}_{\varepsilon,m}]
as
in (
3.57
), we obtain a polynomial in
\varepsilon^{2}
with coefficient functions independent of
\varepsilon
, whose leading term is of order
\varepsilon^{2m+4}
as required.

We must choose
{\rm U}^{m+1}
so that

\ell^{0}[{\rm U}^{0}]({\rm U}^{m+1})+{\rm S}^{m}_{0}=0

(3.72)

This can be done exactly as in Step 3 above: we first consider the components
{\rm U}^{m+1}_{u}=:{\rm u}^{m+1}=\binom{\Phi^{m+1}}{{\rm A}_{a}^{m+1}}
. As above, we take
{\rm u}^{m+1}
to solve the system of equations

L[\mathfrak{u}(q)]({\rm u}^{m+1})=-{\rm S}^{m}_{0,u}\qquad\mbox{ on }{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times(0,T_{0}).

The orthogonality condition established in Lemma
11
provides exactly the condition for solvability of this system, see Proposition
7
in Appendix
A.2
. It follows from the proposition and the regularity (
3.54
) of the right-hand side that there exists a unique solution satisfying (
A.20
), with the estimates

\|{\rm u}^{m+1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m},j}_{\gamma}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm u}^{m+1}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}-1,j}_{\gamma}}\leq C.

(3.73)

It follows from Lemmas
12
and
22
that
{\rm u}^{m+1}(\cdot,y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
is gauge-orthogonal for every
(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})
and hence that

\ell^{0}_{u}[{\rm U}^{0}]({\rm U}^{m+1})\overset{\eqref{eq:wSe5}}{=}{\mathcal{L}}[\mathfrak{u}(q)]({\rm u}^{m+1})\overset{\eqref{L=calL}}{=}L[\mathfrak{u}(q)]({\rm u}^{m+1})=-{\rm S}^{m}_{0,u}

in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times(0,T_{0})
. Thus the
u
-components of (
3.72
) are satisfied.

To complete the construction of
{\rm U}^{m+1}
it now suffices to choose
{\rm A}^{m+1}_{0},{\rm A}^{m+1}_{\bar{a}}
such that

\ell^{0}_{\alpha}[{\rm U}^{0}]({\rm U}^{m+1})=-{\rm S}^{m}_{0,\alpha}\quad\mbox{ on }(0,T_{0})\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ for }\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d.

This equation states that

\displaystyle(-\partial_{a}\partial_{a}+|\varphi(q)|^{2}){\rm A}^{m+1}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\partial_{\alpha}\partial_{a}{\rm A}^{m+1}_{a}-(i\Phi^{m+1},{\rm D}_{{\rm A}^{0}_{\alpha}}\varphi(q))

\displaystyle-(i\varphi(q)),{\rm D}_{{\rm A}^{0}_{\alpha}}\Phi^{m+1})-{\rm S}^{m}_{0,\alpha}.

The right-hand side contains only components of
{\rm U}^{0}
and components
\Phi^{m+1},{\rm A}^{m+1}_{a}
of
{\rm U}^{m+1}
that have already been found.
The first term on the right-hand side is the least regular, and
from (
3.73
) it satisfies

\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}{\rm A}^{m+1}_{a}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}-2,j}_{\gamma}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\alpha}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{a}{\rm A}^{m+1}_{a}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}-3,j}_{\gamma}.}\leq C.

It therefore follows from Proposition
6
that for
\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d
,
this equation has a unique solution that satisfies

\|{\rm A}^{m+1}_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}-2,j}_{\gamma}}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm A}^{m+1}_{\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}-3,j}_{\gamma}.}\leq C.

Since
{\rm U}^{m+1}=({\rm u}^{m+1},{\rm A}^{m+1}_{\bar{a}},{\rm A}^{m+1}_{0})
is as regular as the least regular of its components, it follows that
{\rm U}^{m+1}
has the regularity required by the induction hypothesis (
3.48
)

To complete the induction proof, we consider
{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{m+1,\varepsilon}]
.
In view of (
3.70
) and our choice of
{\rm U}^{m+1}
, we see that

{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{m+1,\varepsilon}]
consists of a finite number of terms of order
\varepsilon^{2m+4}
or smaller, as required by (
3.49
), so it only remains to
show that they have the regularity required by the induction hypothesis (
3.50
). For the terms
{\rm S}^{m}_{j}
appearing in (
3.71
), this has been established in (
3.54
). Every other term in the (
3.71
) is a product of functions involving at most two derivatives of
{\rm U}^{m+1}

multiplied by functions arising from earlier stages in the construction that are more regular or are multipliers in more regular spaces, see Remark
6
.
It follows that
{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{m+1,\varepsilon}]
has the regularity of
\ell^{1}[{\rm V}_{\varepsilon,m}]({\rm U}^{m+1})
, the worst term appearing in the expansion. This and our estimates of
{\rm U}^{m+1}
imply that

\|{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{m+1,\varepsilon}]\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}-4,j}_{\gamma}}+\kappa_{0}\|{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}_{m+1,\varepsilon}]\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}X^{k_{m}-5,j}_{\gamma}}\leq C.

Since
k_{m}-4=k_{m+1}
, this completes the induction step.

4.
the modified system

We henceforth fix a solution
q:{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times(0,T)\to M_{N}
of (
1.8
) satisfying our standing assumptions (
1.9
), (
1.10
), and with
\Theta<\infty
, see (
1.11
). For the choice of
L
in (
1.10
) and (
1.11
),
we fix an integer
\ell
such that
2\ell>\frac{d}{2}+1
, and we assume that the parameters in Proposition
1
satisfy

m>\frac{d}{4}+1,\qquad j=\lfloor d/2\rfloor>\frac{d}{2}-1,\qquad L\geq 4m+2\ell+j-1.

(4.1)

For example we can take
m=\lfloor d/4\rfloor+2
and
\ell=\lfloor(d+2)/4\rfloor+1
. For these values,
the choice

L=2d+11
from Theorems
1
and
2
satisfies the requirement from (
4.1
), but
L
can be taken a bit smaller if
d=1,2
, or
3\mod 4
.

Given (
4.1
), the approximate solution
U_{m,\varepsilon}=U^{\varepsilon}_{0}+\varepsilon^{2}U^{\varepsilon}_{m}
provided by the proposition satisfies

\displaystyle\|S[U_{m,\varepsilon}]\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}H^{k_{m}}}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{9/2}\qquad\mbox{ with }k_{m}\geq d+1,

(4.2)

\displaystyle\|U_{m,\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{2\ell+1,\infty}}+\varepsilon^{-1}\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{0}U_{m,\varepsilon}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{2\ell,\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C,

(4.3)

\displaystyle\|U^{\varepsilon}_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{2\ell+1,\infty}}+\varepsilon^{-1}\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{0}U^{\varepsilon}_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{2\ell,\infty}}

\displaystyle\leq C.

(4.4)

We now set
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}:=U_{m,\varepsilon}
for the above choice of
m
.
We will prove the main theorem by finding
\tilde{U}=\binom{\tilde{\Phi}}{\tilde{A}}
that is small in suitable norms, and such that

S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}]=0.

(4.5)

This is now a system of equations for
\tilde{U}
. In this section we will rewrite (
4.5
) in a way that will facilitate our later analysis.

These equations inherit a gauge symmetry from (
1
). A key point in our rewriting of (
4.5
) will be to eliminate this unwanted degree of freedom
by looking for solutions that satisfy the gauge condition

G[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U}):=-(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})\tilde{A}_{0}+\partial_{k}\tilde{A}_{k}-(i\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},\tilde{\Phi})=0

(4.6)

We will use the abbreviations

q_{\varepsilon}=q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t)=q(\varepsilon x_{\bar{a}},\varepsilon t),\qquad L[q_{\varepsilon}]:=L[\mathfrak{u}(q_{\varepsilon})],

(4.7)

and we introduce the norm
\vvvert\,\cdot\,\vvvert
defined by

\vvvert U\vvvert_{k}:=\sup_{0<t<T_{0}/\varepsilon}\left(\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}U(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\|U(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{k+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\right)

(4.8)

This same norm will appear later in a fixed-point argument used to prove our main existence theorems.

We will prove

Proposition 2
.

There exist constant
c_{2},\varepsilon_{0}>0
, linear differential operators
{\mathcal{M}},P
and a nonlinear operator
{\mathcal{N}}
, all acting on functions

{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times[0,\frac{T_{0}}{\varepsilon})\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
, such that if
\tilde{U}
is a solution of

(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}^{2}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{0})\tilde{U}+{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{U}+P\tilde{U}=-{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{U})-S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]

(4.9)

such that
\vvvert\tilde{U}\vvvert_{2\ell}<c
,
with initial data such that

G[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})=0\ \ \mbox{ and }\ \ S_{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}]=0\qquad\mbox{ at }t=0,

(4.10)

and if
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
, then

G[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})=0\ \ \mbox{ and }\ \ S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}]=0\qquad\mbox{ in }{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times[0,\frac{T_{0}}{\varepsilon}).

(4.11)

Moreover,

{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{U}(x_{a},x_{\bar{a}},t)=\begin{pmatrix}(-\Delta_{x_{\bar{a}}}+L[q_{\varepsilon}])\tilde{u}\\
(-\Delta+|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2})\tilde{A}_{\bar{a}}\\
(-\Delta+|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2})\tilde{A}_{0}\end{pmatrix}

(4.12)

and

P=P_{1}+P_{2}
where

\displaystyle P_{1}\tilde{U}

\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}P_{1,\phi}\tilde{U}\\
P_{1,a}\tilde{U}\\
P_{1,\bar{a}}\tilde{U}\\
P_{1,0}\tilde{U}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}2i\left[-\kappa_{0}\tilde{A}_{0}D^{\epsilon}_{0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{A}_{\bar{a}}D^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}-\kappa_{0}{A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}}\partial_{0}\tilde{\Phi}+{A_{\varepsilon\bar{a}}^{ap}}\partial_{\bar{a}}\tilde{\Phi}\right]\\
0\\
-2(i\tilde{\Phi},D^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap})\\
-2(i\tilde{\Phi},D^{\epsilon}_{0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap})\end{pmatrix}

(4.13)

and
P_{2}
is a first-order linear differential operator with smooth coefficients, say
P_{2,\alpha\beta}
, satisfying

\|P_{2,\alpha\beta}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{2\ell,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}.

(4.14)

Finally,
if
k>d/2
and
\vvvert\tilde{U}\vvvert_{k},\vvvert\tilde{V}\vvvert_{k}\leq 1
, then

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{U})(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}H^{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\vvvert\tilde{U}\vvvert_{k}^{2},

(4.15)

\displaystyle\|{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{U})(\cdot,t)-{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{V})(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}H^{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\vvvert\tilde{U}\vvvert_{k}+\vvvert\tilde{V}\vvvert_{k}\right)\left(\vvvert\tilde{U}-\tilde{V}\vvvert_{k}\right)

where
C
depends on
d,k
and
\vvvert U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}\vvvert_{k}
.

The equation
S_{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}]=0
is an elliptic equation and if
\kappa_{0}>0
imposes a constraint on the initial data, as is standard in Yang-Mills-Higgs theories. If
\kappa_{0}=0
it holds automatically.

From the explicit form of
P_{1}
and estimates (
3.11
) of the approximate solution, as well as the choice (
4.1
) of
L
, we see that the coefficients of
P_{1}
, say
P_{1,\alpha\beta}
, satisfy

\|P_{1,\alpha\beta}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{2\ell,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C\varepsilon.

(4.16)

Similarly, the coefficients of
{\mathcal{M}}
, say
{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha\beta}
, satisfy

\|{\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha\beta}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{2\ell,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C.

(4.17)

Proof.

Step 1
. For simplicity we will write
G(\tilde{U})
instead of
G[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})
. We will enforce the gauge condition (
4.6
) by solving
the system

\displaystyle S_{\phi}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}]

\displaystyle=i(\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{\Phi})G(\tilde{U})

(4.18)

\displaystyle S_{j}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}]

\displaystyle=\partial_{j}G(\tilde{U}),\qquad\qquad j=1,\ldots,d

\displaystyle S_{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}]

\displaystyle=\partial_{0}G(\tilde{U}).

We first claim that if
\tilde{U}
solves (
4.18
) with initial data as described above, then
G(\tilde{U})=0
and hence
\tilde{U}
solves (
4.5
). To see this, note that our choice of initial data immediately implies that
G(\tilde{U})=0
at
t=0
. Moreover, in the hyperbolic case
\kappa_{0}>0
, it follows from (
4.10
) and (
4.18
) that
\partial_{0}G(\tilde{U})\overset{\eqref{modify1}}{=}S_{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}]\overset{\eqref{modified.data}}{=}0
at
t=0
. We wish to apply Lemma
2
to conclude that
G(tU)
vanishes identically. To do this we must verify some assumptions. Regularity condition (
1.26
) on
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}
holds due to (
4.3
), and condition (
1.27
) on
\tilde{U}
is exactly the assumption that
\vvvert\tilde{U}\vvvert_{2\ell}<\infty
, which also is easily seen to imply assumption (
1.30
).
We must also check that there exists some
c>0
such that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\nabla f(x)|^{2}+|\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{\Phi}(x,t)|^{2}f^{2}(x)\,dx\geq c\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}f^{2}(x)\,dx

for all
f\in H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
.
To do this, note that
\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}=\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon^{2}\Phi^{\varepsilon}_{m}
, elementary inequalities
imply that

|\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{\Phi}|^{2}=|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon^{2}\Phi^{\varepsilon}_{m}+\tilde{\Phi}|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2}-|\varepsilon^{2}\Phi^{\varepsilon}_{m}+\tilde{\Phi}|^{2}.

For any fixed
t
, since we continue to assume (
1.9
), we can use Lemma
18
to compute

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\nabla f|^{2}+|\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{\Phi}|^{2}f^{2}\,

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}|\nabla_{a}f|^{2}+|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2}f^{2}\,dx_{a}\,dx_{\bar{a}}

\displaystyle\hskip 100.00015pt-\|\Phi^{\varepsilon}_{m}+\tilde{\Phi}|^{2}_{L^{\infty}}\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{c}{2}-\|\varepsilon^{2}\Phi^{\varepsilon}_{m}+\tilde{\Phi}|^{2}_{L^{\infty}}\right)\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

where the constant
c
comes from (
18
). Since
\|\Phi^{\varepsilon}_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C
, and because

\|\tilde{\Phi}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\vvvert\tilde{\Phi}\vvvert_{2\ell}<Cc_{2}
by assumption, we can fix
\varepsilon_{0}
and
c_{2}
so small that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\nabla f|^{2}+|\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{\Phi}|^{2}f^{2}\,\geq\frac{c}{4}\|f\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

whenever
\vvvert\tilde{U}\vvvert_{2\ell}<c_{2}
and
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
. Thus the hypotheses of Lemma
2
are satisfied, and it follows that
G(\tilde{U})=0
.

Step 2
.
Our goal now is to rewrite (
4.18
) in the form given in the statement of the proposition.
Toward this end, we define

\displaystyle S^{\prime}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})

\displaystyle:=\frac{d}{ds}S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+s\tilde{U}]\Big|_{s=0},

(4.19)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{N}}^{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})

\displaystyle:=S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}]-S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]-S^{\prime}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})

(4.20)

These are operators that act on sufficiently smooth functions
\tilde{U}:[0,\frac{T_{0}}{\varepsilon})\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
.
With this notation, (
4.18
) can be rewritten

\displaystyle S_{\phi}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]+S_{\phi}^{\prime}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})-i\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}G(\tilde{U})

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{\phi}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})+i\tilde{\Phi}G(\tilde{U})

(4.21)

\displaystyle S_{j}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]+S_{j}^{\prime}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})-\partial_{j}G(\tilde{U})

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{j}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})

\displaystyle S_{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]+S_{0}^{\prime}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})-\partial_{0}G(\tilde{U})

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U}).

In view of (
4.21
), to complete the proof, it suffices to show that

\left(\begin{aligned} S^{\prime}_{\phi}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})-i\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}G(\tilde{U})\\
S^{\prime}_{j}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})-\partial_{j}G(\tilde{U})\\
S^{\prime}_{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})-\partial_{0}G(\tilde{U})\\
\end{aligned}\right)=(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}^{2}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{0})\tilde{U}+{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{U}+P\tilde{U}

(4.22)

for
{\mathcal{M}}
and
P
as described in the statement of the Proposition, and that

{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{U}):=\begin{pmatrix}{\mathcal{N}}^{0}_{\phi}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})-i\tilde{\Phi}G(\tilde{U})\\
{\mathcal{N}}_{j}^{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})\\
{\mathcal{N}}_{0}^{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})\end{pmatrix}_{j=1,\ldots,d}\quad\mbox{ satisfies \eqref{aux.props2}.
}

(4.23)

Step 3: proof of (
4.23
)

First note that
S[U]
is a (constant coefficient) cubic polynomial function of
U
,
\nabla_{t,x}U
and
\nabla^{2}_{t,x}U
, where

\nabla_{t,x}^{k}\tilde{U}
denotes the collection of all partial derivatives in all the
t,x
variables of order
k
. Moreover, all second derivatives appear linearly in
S[U]
. It then follows from the definition (
4.20
) that
{\mathcal{N}}^{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})
is a cubic polynomial in
\tilde{U}
and
\nabla_{t,x}\tilde{U}
containing only terms that are quadratic or cubic, and with coefficients that are linear functions
12
12

12

that is, polynomials of degree at most
1
.
of
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}
. The same is clearly true of
i\tilde{\Phi}G(\tilde{U})
, so it remains true of
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{U})
. That is,
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{U})
has the form

{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{U})=\sum_{\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}}L_{\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j}}(U_{\varepsilon}^{ap})Z_{\alpha_{i}}Z_{\alpha_{j}}+\sum_{\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k}}C_{\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k}}Z_{\alpha_{i}}Z_{\alpha_{j}}Z_{\alpha_{k}},

(4.24)

where
Z_{\alpha}
denotes a generic component of
\tilde{U}
or
\nabla_{t,x}\tilde{U}
, every

L_{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}}(U_{\varepsilon}^{ap})
is a linear function of
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}
, and every
C_{\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k}}
is a constant.

When
\kappa_{0}>0
, the first inequality in (
4.15
) follows directly from the standard fact that

\|uv\|_{H^{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C_{s,d}\|u\|_{H^{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\|v\|_{H^{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

(which of course implies the corresponding inequality for products of more terms).
The second inequality follows by similar considerations, since

x_{i}x_{j}-y_{i}y_{j}=(x_{i}-y_{i})x_{j}+y_{i}(x_{j}-y_{j})

and

x_{i}x_{j}x_{k}-y_{i}y_{j}y_{k}=(x_{i}-y_{i})x_{j}x_{k}+y_{i}(x_{j}-y_{j})x_{k}+y_{i}y_{j}(x_{k}-y_{k}).

When
\kappa_{0}=0
, it is straightforward to check that
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{U})
as written in (
4.24
) does not contain any terms involving components of
\partial_{t}\tilde{U}
, so both
inequalities of (
4.15
) continue to hold, by the same arguments.

Step 4: proof of (
4.22
)

Introducing the notation

D^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}:=\partial_{\alpha}-i{A_{\varepsilon\alpha}^{ap}},\qquad\tilde{F}_{\alpha\beta}:=\partial_{\alpha}\tilde{A}_{\beta}-\partial_{\beta}\tilde{A}_{\alpha},\qquad F^{ap}_{\varepsilon,\alpha\beta}:=\partial_{\alpha}{A_{\varepsilon\beta}^{ap}}-\partial_{\beta}{A_{\varepsilon\alpha}^{ap}},

we find after a calculation that

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{\phi}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}D^{\epsilon}_{0}D^{\epsilon}_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}D^{\epsilon}_{0})\tilde{\Phi}-D^{\epsilon}_{j}D^{\epsilon}_{j}\tilde{\Phi}+2i(-\kappa_{0}\tilde{A}_{0}D^{\epsilon}_{0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{A}_{j}D^{\epsilon}_{j}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap})

(4.25)

\displaystyle+i\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}(-(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})\tilde{A}_{0}+\partial_{j}\tilde{A}_{j})+\frac{1}{2}(|\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}|^{2}-1)\tilde{\Phi}+(\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},\tilde{\Phi})\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{j}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1})\tilde{F}_{0j}-\partial_{k}\tilde{F}_{kj}-(i\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},D^{\epsilon}_{j}\tilde{\Phi})-(i\tilde{\Phi},D^{\epsilon}_{j}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap})+\tilde{A}_{j}|\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}|^{2}

\displaystyle S_{0}^{\prime}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})

\displaystyle=-\partial_{k}\tilde{F}_{k0}-(i\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},D^{\epsilon}_{0}\tilde{\Phi})-(i\tilde{\Phi},D^{\epsilon}_{0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap})+\tilde{A}_{0}|\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}|^{2}

(Recall that repeated indices
j,k
are summed from
1
to
d
.)
The terms
S^{\prime}_{\phi}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})
and
S^{\prime}_{a}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U}),a=1,2
contain the degenerate operator

{\mathcal{L}}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]
defined in (
2.25
).
The gauge condition (
4.6
)
will effectively replace
{\mathcal{L}}[u_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]
by the coercive operator
L[u_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]
from (
2.28
).
To start, we compute

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{\phi}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})-i\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}G(\tilde{U})

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}D^{\epsilon}_{0}D^{\epsilon}_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}D^{\epsilon}_{0})\tilde{\Phi}-D^{\epsilon}_{j}D^{\epsilon}_{j}\tilde{\Phi}+\frac{1}{2}(3|\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}|^{2}-1)\tilde{\Phi}

(4.26)

\displaystyle+2i(-\kappa_{0}\tilde{A}_{0}D^{\epsilon}_{0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{A}_{j}D^{\epsilon}_{j}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap})

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{j}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})-\partial_{j}G(\tilde{U})

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{0})\tilde{A}_{j}-\partial_{k}\partial_{k}\tilde{A}_{j}-2(i\tilde{\Phi},D^{\epsilon}_{j}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap})+\tilde{A}_{j}|\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}|^{2}

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})-\partial_{0}G(\tilde{U})

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{0})\tilde{A}_{0}-\partial_{k}\partial_{k}\tilde{A}_{0}-2(i\tilde{\Phi},D^{\epsilon}_{0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap})+\tilde{A}_{0}|\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}|^{2}.

We now consider various terms on the right-hand side, with the aim of eventually rewriting everything as in (
4.22
).

Step 4a
.
First,

D^{\epsilon}_{0}\tilde{\Phi}=\partial_{0}\tilde{\Phi}-i{A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}}\tilde{\Phi}

and

D^{\epsilon}_{0}D^{\epsilon}_{0}\tilde{\Phi}=\partial_{0}\partial_{0}\tilde{\Phi}-2i{A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}}\partial_{0}\tilde{\Phi}-i(\partial_{0}{A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}})\tilde{\Phi}-({A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}})^{2}\tilde{\Phi}.

Using these we find that

\displaystyle(\kappa_{0}D^{\epsilon}_{0}D^{\epsilon}_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}D^{\epsilon}_{0})\tilde{\Phi}-2i\kappa_{0}\tilde{A}_{0}D^{\epsilon}_{0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}^{2}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{0})\tilde{\Phi}

(4.27)

\displaystyle-2i\kappa_{0}(\tilde{A}_{0}D^{\epsilon}_{0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+{A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}}\partial_{0}\tilde{\Phi})

\displaystyle+P_{2a,\phi}\tilde{\Phi}

where
P_{2a,\phi}=-\kappa_{0}[i\partial_{0}{A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}}+{A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}}^{2}]+i\varepsilon\kappa_{1}{A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}}
. Recalling that
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}=U_{j,\varepsilon}
, it then follows from (
4.3
) that

\|P_{2a,\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{2\ell,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}.

Indeed, the choices in (
4.1
) are made exactly to guarantee this and similar inequalities below.

Step 4b
.
Next, splitting the remaining terms in

S^{\prime}_{\phi}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})-i\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}G(\tilde{U})
into sums over
a=1,2
and
\bar{a}=3,\ldots,d
and recalling the definition of
L[u_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]
from (
2.28
), we find that

-D^{\epsilon}_{j}D^{\epsilon}_{j}\tilde{\Phi}+\frac{1}{2}(3|\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}|^{2}-1)\tilde{\Phi}+2i\tilde{A}_{j}D^{\epsilon}_{j}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}=L_{\phi}[u_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]\tilde{u}-D^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}D^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}\tilde{\Phi}+2i\tilde{A}_{\bar{a}}D^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}.

Computing as in Step 3a, we find that

-D^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}D^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}\tilde{\Phi}+2i\tilde{A}_{\bar{a}}D^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}=-\partial_{\bar{a}}\partial_{\bar{a}}\tilde{\Phi}+2i(\tilde{A}_{\bar{a}}D^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+{A_{\varepsilon\bar{a}}^{ap}}\partial_{\bar{a}}\tilde{\Phi})+P_{2b,\phi}\tilde{\Phi}

where
P_{2b,\phi}=-[i\partial_{\bar{a}}{A_{\varepsilon\bar{a}}^{ap}}+{A_{\varepsilon\bar{a}}^{ap}}{A_{\varepsilon\bar{a}}^{ap}}]
. It again follows from (
4.3
)
that

\|P_{2b,\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{2\ell,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}.

Next, we claim that

L_{\phi}[u_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{u})=L_{\phi}[q_{\varepsilon}](\tilde{u})+P_{2c,\phi}\tilde{u}+P_{2d,\phi}\nabla\tilde{u}

where

\|P_{2c,\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{2\ell,\infty}}\leq C\varepsilon^{2},\qquad\|P_{2d,\phi}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{2\ell,\infty}}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}

Indeed, recalling from Proposition
1
that

u_{\varepsilon}^{ap}=u_{m,\varepsilon}=\mathfrak{u}(q_{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon^{2}u^{\varepsilon}_{m}\quad\mbox{ with $u^{\varepsilon}_{m}$ satisfying \eqref{good3}}

the above estimate holds because the coefficients of the first-order operator
L_{\phi}[u]
depend smoothly, indeed polynomially, on components of
u
.

Combining this with (
4.27
), we find that

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{\phi}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})-i\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}G(\tilde{U})

(4.28)

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{0})\tilde{\Phi}-\partial_{\bar{a}}\partial_{\bar{a}}\tilde{\Phi}+L_{\phi}[q_{\varepsilon}]\tilde{u}

\displaystyle+2i[(\tilde{A}_{\bar{a}}D^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+{A_{\varepsilon\bar{a}}^{ap}}\partial_{\bar{a}}\tilde{\Phi})-\kappa_{0}(\tilde{A}_{0}D^{\epsilon}_{0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+{A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}}\partial_{0}\tilde{\Phi})]-i\varepsilon\kappa_{1}{A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}}\tilde{\Phi}

\displaystyle P_{2,\phi}\tilde{u}

where

P_{2,\phi}\tilde{U}:=(P_{2a,\phi}+P_{2b,\phi}+P_{2c,\phi})\tilde{u}+P_{2d,\phi}\nabla\tilde{u}.

This verifies (
4.14
) for
P_{2,\phi}
.

Step 4c
. Similar but easier arguments show that for
\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d

\displaystyle S^{\prime}_{\alpha}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})-\partial_{\alpha}G(\tilde{U})

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{0})\tilde{A}_{\alpha}-\partial_{\bar{a}}\partial_{\bar{a}}\tilde{A}_{\alpha}+(-\partial_{a}\partial_{a}+|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2})\tilde{A}_{\alpha}

(4.29)

\displaystyle-2(i\tilde{\Phi},D^{\epsilon}_{\alpha}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap})+P_{2,\alpha}\tilde{U}

where

P_{2,\alpha}\tilde{U}:=(|\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}|^{2}-|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2})\tilde{A}_{\alpha},\qquad\|P_{2,\alpha}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{2\ell,\infty}}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}

For
a=1,2
,
we start as in (
4.29
), then
use the definition (
2.28
) of
L[u]

to obtain

S^{\prime}_{a}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})-\partial_{a}G(\tilde{U})=(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}\partial_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{0})\tilde{A}_{a}-\partial_{\bar{a}}\partial_{\bar{a}}\tilde{A}_{a}-L_{a}[q_{\varepsilon}]\tilde{u}+P_{2,a}\tilde{U}.

(4.30)

where

P_{2,a}\tilde{U}=(|\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}|^{2}-\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2})\tilde{A}_{a}-2(i\tilde{\Phi},D^{\epsilon}_{a}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}-(\partial_{a}-i\mathcal{A}(q_{\varepsilon})_{a})\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})).

And again it follows from (
3.11
) that

\|P_{2,a}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{2\ell,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}.

The remaining conclusions of the proposition follow by comparing
(
4.28
), (
4.29
), and (
4.30
) with (
4.9
) – (
4.14
).
∎

5.
linear estimates for the modified system

In this section,
q:{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times[0,T)\to M_{N}
continues to denote a fixed solution of (
1.8
)
satisfying (
1.9
), (
1.10
) and with
\Theta<\infty
, see (
1.11
).
We continue to assume that (
4.1
) holds with some
\ell
such that
2\ell>\frac{d}{2}+2
.

We consider the linear system

\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}^{2}\tilde{U}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{0}\tilde{U}+{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{U}+P\tilde{U}=\eta

(5.1)

where the operators
{\mathcal{M}}
and
P
, encoding properties of
q
, are as described in Proposition
2
,
and
\eta:[0,\frac{T_{0}}{\varepsilon})\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
is a given function,
with components that we will as usual write as

\eta=\begin{pmatrix}\eta_{u}\\
\eta_{\bar{a}}\\
\eta_{0}\end{pmatrix}\quad\mbox{ where }\eta_{u}=\binom{\eta_{\phi}}{\eta_{a}}.

Equation (
5.1
) is defined for functions
\tilde{U}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times[0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
, where
T_{0}\leq T
is the time from the construction
of the approximate solution.
Existence of solutions of (
5.1
) is standard.

For
\tilde{U}=(\tilde{u},\tilde{A}_{\bar{a}},\tilde{A}_{0})^{T}
solving (
5.1
),
we define

c_{\mu}(x_{\bar{a}},t):=(\tilde{u},n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{(x_{\bar{a}},t)})}:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\tilde{u}(x_{a},x_{\bar{a}},t)\cdot n_{\mu}(x;q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t))\,dx_{a}

(5.2)

where
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{(x_{\bar{a}},t)}:={\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times\{(x_{\bar{a}},t)\}
and
\{n_{\mu}\}_{\mu=1}^{2N}
is the orthonormal basis introduced in Definition (
2
).

We will prove

Theorem 7
.

Assume that

\eta\in L^{2}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}(H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d};{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1})),

and let
\tilde{U}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times(0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
solves (
5.1
)
with initial data such that

c_{\mu}(x_{\bar{a}},0)=\kappa_{0}\partial_{t}c_{\mu}(x_{\bar{a}},0)=0.

(5.3)

Then there exists a constant
C>0
, independent of
\varepsilon
and
\eta
, such that

\displaystyle\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}+\|\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ce^{Ct/\varepsilon}\left(\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}(\cdot,0)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}+\|\tilde{U}(\cdot,0)\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 40.00006pt+\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{3}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{C\varepsilon(t-s)}\|\eta(s,\cdot)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}\,ds

for every
t\in(0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)
.

The Theorem will follow rather directly from 3 propositions that we will state below, after introducing some notation.

Next, for
\ell\geq 0
we define

\displaystyle Q_{\ell}^{\perp}(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}({\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}\tilde{U}(\cdot,t))\cdot({\mathcal{M}}^{\ell+1}\tilde{U}(\cdot,t))

\displaystyle Q_{\ell}^{\top}(t)

\displaystyle:=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}}\sum_{\mu}|\Delta^{\ell}c_{\mu}(\cdot,t)|^{2}

\displaystyle Q_{\ell}(t)

\displaystyle:=\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}({\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}\tilde{U})(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}+Q_{\ell}^{\perp}(t)+Q_{\ell}^{\top}(t)

and

{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(t):=Q_{\ell}(t)+Q_{0}(t).

(5.4)

Several times in the proof of the theorem we will establish estimates by differentiating (
5.1
) up to
2\ell
times
with respect to the spatial variables. These computations are all jutified, since the coefficients appearing in the definitions of
{\mathcal{M}}
and
P
belong to
