W^{2\ell,\infty}
, see (
4.14
), (
4.16
) and (
4.17
).

The first proposition, whose proof is deferred to Section
5.4
, implies that
it suffices to estimate
{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(t)
for
t\in[0,T/\varepsilon).

Proposition 3
.

There exist positive constants
c<C
such that

c{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(t)\leq C\left(\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}+\|\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}^{2}\right)\leq C{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(t).

(5.5)

for every
t\in[0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)
.

We will first control the part of
Q_{\ell}
that, roughly speaking, is orthogonal to the kernel of
{\mathcal{M}}
.

Proposition 4
.

There exists a constant
C
such that

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left(\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+Q_{\ell}^{\perp}(t)\right)\leq C\varepsilon{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(t)+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\|\eta(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}

(5.6)

for all
t\in[0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)
.

This will be proved in Section
5.1
.

The other ingredient in the proof is provided by the next Proposition, which is the most subtle part of the proof of Theorem
7
. It is proved in Section
5.2
.

Proposition 5
.

There exists a constant
C
such that

Q^{\top}_{\ell}(t)\leq C\kappa_{0}^{2}{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(0)+C\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(s)\,ds+\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{3}}\int_{0}^{t}\|\eta(s,\cdot)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}\,ds

(5.7)

for all
t\in[0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)
.

We now show that as remarked above, these three propositions easily imply the conclusion of Theorem
7
.

Proof of Theorem
7
.

We integrate (
5.6
) from
0
to
t
, add to (
5.7
), and add the result to the same inequality for
\ell=0
to obtain

{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(t)\leq C{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(0)+C\varepsilon\int_{0}^{t}{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(s)\,ds+\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{3}}\int_{0}^{t}\|\eta(s,\cdot)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}\,ds.

Then Grönwall’s inequality implies that

{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(t)\leq e^{C\varepsilon t}{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(0)+\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{3}}\int_{0}^{t}e^{C\varepsilon(t-s)}\|\eta(s,\cdot)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}\,ds.

The Theorem now follows from Proposition
3
.
∎

5.1.
Proof of Proposition
4

We start with a lemma that is exactly the
\ell=0
case of Proposition
4
.

Lemma 13
.

There exists a constant
C
, such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left(\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+Q_{0}^{\perp}(t)\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon(\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{1}}^{2})+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\|\eta(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon Q_{0}(t)+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\|\eta(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}

for all
t\in[0,T/\varepsilon)
.

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\left(\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+Q_{0}^{\perp}(t)\right)

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\kappa_{0}\tilde{U}_{t}\cdot\tilde{U}_{tt}+\tilde{U}_{t}\cdot{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{U}+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{U}\cdot{\mathcal{M}}_{t}\tilde{U}

\displaystyle=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\tilde{U}_{t}\cdot(-\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\tilde{U}_{t}-P\tilde{U}-\eta)+\tilde{U}\cdot{\mathcal{M}}_{t}\tilde{U}

where
{\mathcal{M}}_{t}
denotes the operator obtained by differentiating the coefficients of
{\mathcal{M}}
with respect to
t
.
The definition (
4.12
) of
{\mathcal{M}}
implies that

\int\tilde{U}\cdot{\mathcal{M}}_{t}\tilde{U}\leq C\varepsilon\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}.

(5.8)

If
\kappa_{1}>0
we have the pointwise inequality

\tilde{U}_{t}\cdot(-\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\tilde{U}_{t}-P\tilde{U}-\eta)\leq\frac{1}{2\varepsilon\kappa_{1}}(|P\tilde{U}|^{2}+|\eta|^{2}).

Integrating and using the estimate
\|P\tilde{U}\|\leq C\varepsilon\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{1}}
from Proposition
2
, as well as the
\ell=0
case of (
5.5
),
we find that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+Q_{0}^{\perp}(t)\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\|\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\|\eta(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ell.energy}}{\leq}C\varepsilon{\mathfrak{Q}}_{0}(t)+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\|\eta(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

On the other hand, if
\kappa_{1}=0
then
\kappa_{0}>0
, so we may compute

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+Q_{0}^{\perp}(t)\right)

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{Mt.est}}{\leq}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}-\tilde{U}_{t}\cdot(P\tilde{U}+\eta)+C\varepsilon\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\int\frac{\varepsilon}{2}|\tilde{U}_{t}|^{2}+\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}(|P\tilde{U}|^{2}+|\eta|^{2})+C\varepsilon\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon(\|\tilde{U}_{t}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{1}}^{2})+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\|\eta(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{ell.energy}}{\leq}C\varepsilon Q(t)+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\|\eta(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

∎

We can now complete the

Proof of Proposition
4
.

By applying the operator
{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}
to equation (
5.1
), we find that
\tilde{U}_{\ell}:={\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}\tilde{U}
satisfies

\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}^{2}\tilde{U}_{\ell}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{0}\tilde{U}_{\ell}+{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{U}_{\ell}+P\tilde{U}_{\ell}=\eta_{\ell}

(5.9)

where

\eta_{\ell}=\kappa_{0}[\partial_{0}^{2},{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}]\tilde{U}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}[\partial_{0},{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}]\tilde{U}+[P,{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}]\tilde{U}+{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}\eta

and
[A,B]
denotes the commutator
AB-BA
. These computations and similar ones below are justified because
all coefficients in (
5.1
) are bounded in
L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{2\ell,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, see
(
4.14
), (
4.16
), (
4.17
).

In view of (
5.9
) we can apply the previous lemma to
\tilde{U}_{\ell}
to find that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\left(\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\int{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell+1}\tilde{U}\cdot{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}\tilde{U}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 50.00008pt\leq C\varepsilon\left(\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}\tilde{U}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}\right)+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\|\eta_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Thus Proposition
3
and the definitions implies that

\frac{d}{dt}\left(\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+Q_{\ell}^{\perp}(t)\right)\leq C\varepsilon{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(t)+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\|\eta_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\qquad\mbox{ at every }t\in(0,T_{0}/\varepsilon).

To complete the proof, it therefore suffices to prove that

\|\eta_{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\|\eta\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}+C\varepsilon^{2}{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(t)

(5.10)

We consider each of the terms in
\eta_{\ell}
in turn. Due to the appearance of time derivatives in the estimates, we will sometimes write
{\mathcal{M}}(t)
to explicitly record the dependence of
{\mathcal{M}}
on
t
.

First, from the definition (
4.12
) of
{\mathcal{M}}
, we see that

{\mathcal{M}}(t)=-\Delta+L_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),1}

where
L_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),1}
is a first-order linear differential operator whose coefficients depend on
q_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,t)=q(\varepsilon\cdot,\varepsilon t)
.
Thus

{\mathcal{M}}(t)^{\ell}=(-\Delta)^{\ell}+L_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),\ell}

where
L_{q_{\varepsilon},\ell}
is a linear differential operator of order at most
2\ell-1
whose coefficients depend smoothly on
q_{\varepsilon}
. Since
(-\Delta)^{\ell}
clearly commutes with
\partial_{t}^{2}
,

[\partial_{0}^{2},{\mathcal{M}}(t)^{\ell}]\tilde{U}=[\partial_{0}^{2},L_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),\ell}]\tilde{U}=(\partial_{t}^{2}L_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),\ell})\tilde{U}+2(\partial_{t}L_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),\ell})\partial_{t}\tilde{U}

where
(\partial_{t}^{k}L_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),\ell})
denotes the differential operator, still of order at most
2\ell-1
, obtained by differentiating the coefficients of
L_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),\ell}
as indicated. From the fact that
\partial_{t}^{k}q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t)=\varepsilon^{k}(\partial_{t}^{k}q)\varepsilon x_{\bar{a}},\varepsilon t)
as well as the smooth dependence of the coefficients of
L_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),\ell}
on
q_{\varepsilon}
, we infer that

\left\|\kappa_{0}[\partial_{0}^{2},{\mathcal{M}}(t)^{\ell}]\tilde{U}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\kappa_{0}\left(\varepsilon^{2}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell-1}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{4}\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell-1}}^{2}\right).

Very similar arguments show that

\left\|\varepsilon\kappa_{1}[\partial_{0},{\mathcal{M}}(t)^{\ell}]\tilde{U}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\varepsilon^{4}\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell-1}}^{2}.

It follows from (
4.14
), (
4.16
) that

\left\|[P,{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}]\tilde{U}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}.

Finally, it is clear that

\left\|{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}\eta\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\|\eta\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}\ .

Claim (
5.10
) follows by adding up the previous inequalities and using (
5.5
) to bound the right-hand sides in terms of
{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(t)
.

∎

5.2.
Proof of Proposition
5

For this proof we will need to write the PDE satisfied by
c_{\mu}
in a way that carefully exploits the structure of (
5.1
), in particular that of the linear operator
P
. The following decomposition of
\tilde{A}_{\alpha}
for
\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d
will play a crucial role in our arguments.

Lemma 14
.

Assume that

\|\tilde{U}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}H^{2\ell+1}}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}H^{2\ell}}<\infty.

For
\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d
exist functions
f_{\alpha}
and
\chi_{\alpha}
such that

\tilde{A}_{\alpha}=f_{\alpha}+\partial_{0}\chi_{\alpha}

(5.11)

and

\displaystyle\|\chi_{\alpha}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{0}\tilde{A}_{\alpha}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}}+C\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\|\tilde{A}_{\alpha}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}}

(5.12)

\displaystyle\|f_{\alpha}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\left(\|\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{0}\tilde{A}_{\alpha}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}}\right)+\|\eta(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}}

for all
t\in[0,T/\varepsilon)
.

Proof.

We first note that if
\psi\in H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
is a weak solution of

(-\Delta+|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2})\psi=g\in H^{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}),

(5.13)

then
\psi\in H^{k+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
and

\|\psi\|_{H^{k+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C\|g\|_{H^{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}.

(5.14)

Since
|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2}
is smooth, standard elliptic regularity implies that
\psi\in H^{k+2}_{loc}
. The

k=0
case of (
5.14
) then follows directly from Lemma
18
. The general case follows by induction.
Indeed, if
\psi
solves (
5.13
), then for any
i\in\{1,\ldots,d\}
we see by differentiating (
5.13
) that
\psi_{i}:=\partial_{i}\psi
solves

(-\Delta+|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2})\psi_{i}=\partial_{i}g-(\partial_{i}|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2})\psi\in H^{k-1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d}).

So the induction hypothesis implies that

\|\partial_{i}\psi\|_{H^{k}}\leq C(\|\partial_{i}g\|_{H^{k-1}}+\|\psi\|_{H^{k-1}})\leq C\|g\|_{H^{k}}

for every
i
, which immediately implies (
5.14
).

Next, we define
\chi_{\alpha}
as the solution of the equation

(-\Delta+|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2})\chi_{\alpha}(\cdot,t)=-\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}\tilde{A}_{\alpha}(\cdot,t)-\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\tilde{A}_{\alpha}(\cdot,t).

Then the first estimate in (
5.12
) follows directly from (
5.14
).

We are forced by (
5.11
) to define

f_{\alpha}=\tilde{A}_{\alpha}-\partial_{0}\chi_{\alpha}
.
Writing
\varphi
for
\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})
and recalling the definition (
4.12
) of the operator
{\mathcal{M}}
in (
5.1
), we find that
f_{\alpha}
solves

\displaystyle(-\Delta+|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2})f_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=(-\Delta+|\varphi|^{2})(\tilde{A}_{\alpha}-\partial_{0}\chi_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=(-\Delta+|\varphi|^{2})\tilde{A}_{\alpha}-\partial_{0}[(-\Delta+|\varphi|^{2})\chi_{\alpha})]+\partial_{0}|\varphi|^{2}\chi_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}^{2}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{0}-\Delta+|\varphi|^{2})\tilde{A}_{\alpha}+\partial_{0}|\varphi|^{2}\chi_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{lin.system}}{=}-P_{\alpha}\tilde{U}+\eta_{\alpha}+\partial_{0}|\varphi|^{2}\chi_{\alpha}.

(5.15)

Recall from (
4.3
) that

\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{0}|\varphi|^{2}\|_{W^{2\ell,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C\varepsilon

Note also that
H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
is an algebra, as we have assumed that
2\ell>\frac{d}{2}
. It thus follows from (
5.14
) and properties of the operator
P
in Proposition
2
and our earlier estimate of
\chi_{\alpha}
that

\displaystyle\|f_{\alpha}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq\|P_{\alpha}\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell}}+\|\eta_{\alpha}\|_{H^{2\ell}}+\|\partial_{0}|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2}\chi_{\alpha}\|_{H^{2\ell}}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\left(\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell}}\right)+\|\eta_{\alpha}\|_{H^{2\ell}}

at time
t
.
∎

Lemma 15
.

c_{\mu}
satisfies the initial value problem

\displaystyle(\kappa_{0}\partial_{tt}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}-\Delta_{\bar{a}})c_{\mu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{0}h_{0}+\nabla_{\bar{a}}\cdot h_{\bar{a}}+h,

(5.16)

\displaystyle c_{\mu}=\kappa_{0}\partial_{t}c_{\mu}=0

\displaystyle\mbox{ at }t=0

where the functions on the right-hand side satisfy the estimate

\displaystyle\|h_{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\kappa_{0}\left(\|\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\right)

(5.17)

\displaystyle\|h_{\bar{a}}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\|\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\|h(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\left(\|\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\right)

\displaystyle+C\|\eta(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}.

Proof.

We will write
(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)
as an abbreviation for
(\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}
.

The initial conditions for
c_{\mu}
have been assumed in (
5.3
).

Next, note that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}c_{\mu}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{t}\tilde{u},n_{\mu})+(\tilde{u},\partial_{t}n_{\mu})

\displaystyle\partial_{tt}c_{\mu}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{tt}\tilde{u},n_{\mu})+2\partial_{t}(\tilde{u},\partial_{t}n_{\mu})-(\tilde{u},\partial_{tt}n_{\mu}).

and similarly
\partial_{\bar{a}\bar{a}}c_{\mu}
for
\bar{a}=3,\ldots,d
.
Next, note that because
L[\mathfrak{u}(q)]
is self-adjoint and
L[\mathfrak{u}(q)]n_{\mu}(q)=0
(as follows from Theorem
6
and Definition
2
),

({\mathcal{M}}_{u}\tilde{U},n_{\mu})=(-\Delta_{\bar{a}}\tilde{u}+L[\mathfrak{u}(q)]\tilde{u},n_{\mu})=-\Delta_{\bar{a}}c_{\mu}+2\nabla_{\bar{a}}\cdot(\tilde{u},\nabla_{\bar{a}}n_{\mu})-(\tilde{u},\Delta_{\bar{a}}n_{\mu})

where we write
{\mathcal{M}}_{u}\tilde{U}
to denote the relevant rows of
{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{U}
(and similarly
P_{u}\tilde{U}
below).
We now take the
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
inner product of the relevant components of (
5.1
) with
n_{\mu}
and use the above computations to rewrite, leading to

\displaystyle(\kappa_{0}\partial_{tt}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}-\Delta_{\bar{a}})c_{\mu}

\displaystyle=2\kappa_{0}\partial_{t}(\tilde{u},\partial_{t}n_{\mu})-2\partial_{\bar{a}}(\tilde{u},\partial_{\bar{a}}n_{\mu})

\displaystyle-(\tilde{u},(\kappa_{0}\partial_{t}^{2}-\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}-\Delta_{\bar{a}})n_{\mu})-(P_{u}\tilde{U},n_{\mu})+(\eta_{u},n_{\mu}).

We next write
P=P_{1}+P_{2}
as in Proposition
2
(and thus
P_{u}=P_{1u}+P_{2u}
) and we set

h_{0,i}:=2\kappa_{0}(\tilde{u},\partial_{t}n_{\mu}),\qquad h_{\bar{a},i}:=-2(\tilde{u},\partial_{\bar{a}}n_{\mu}),\qquad

and

h_{,i}:=-(\tilde{u},(\kappa_{0}\partial_{t}^{2}-\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}-\Delta_{\bar{a}})n_{\mu})-(P_{2u}\tilde{U},n_{\mu})+(\eta_{u},n_{\mu}),

so that

(\kappa_{0}\partial_{tt}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}-\Delta_{\bar{a}})c_{\mu}=\partial_{0}h_{0,i}+\partial_{\bar{a}}h_{\bar{a},i}+h_{,i}-(P_{1u}\tilde{U},n_{\mu})\ .

Below we will look more carefully at
(P_{1u}\tilde{U},n_{\mu}).
First, however, we note that

\displaystyle\|h_{0,i}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\kappa_{0}\|\tilde{u}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

(5.18)

\displaystyle\|h_{\bar{a},i}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\|\tilde{u}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\qquad\qquad\mbox{ for }\bar{a}=3,\ldots,d,

\displaystyle\|h_{,i}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\|\tilde{u}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+C\|\eta(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}.

Indeed, these follow from the estimate for
P_{2}
in Proposition
2
, see (
4.14
), and the fact that
n_{\mu}=n_{\mu}(x_{a};q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}},t))=n_{\mu}(x_{a};q(\varepsilon x_{\bar{a}},\varepsilon t))
.

Writing
n_{\mu}=(n_{\mu,\phi},n_{\mu,a})^{T}
, we use the definition (
4.13
) of
P_{1}
and substitute
\tilde{A}_{\alpha}=f_{\alpha}+\partial_{0}\chi_{\alpha}
for
\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d
to find
that

\displaystyle(P_{1u}\tilde{U},n_{\mu})

\displaystyle=-\kappa_{0}(f_{0}(2iD^{\epsilon}_{0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},n_{\mu,\phi})+\partial_{0}\chi_{0}(2iD^{\epsilon}_{0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},n_{\mu,\phi}))+f_{\bar{a}}(2iD^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},n_{\mu,\phi})

\displaystyle+\partial_{0}\chi_{\bar{a}}(2iD^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},n_{\mu,\phi})-\kappa_{0}(2i{A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}}\partial_{0}\tilde{\Phi},n_{\mu,\phi})+(2i{A_{\varepsilon\bar{a}}^{ap}}\partial_{\bar{a}}\tilde{\Phi},n_{\mu,\phi})

\displaystyle=\partial_{0}h_{0,ii}+\partial_{\bar{a}}h_{\bar{a},ii}+h_{,ii}

for

\displaystyle h_{0,ii}

\displaystyle:=-\kappa_{0}\chi_{0}(2iD^{\epsilon}_{0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},n_{\mu,\phi})+\chi_{\bar{a}}(2iD^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},n_{\mu,\phi})-\kappa_{0}(2i{A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}}\tilde{\Phi},n_{\mu,\phi})

\displaystyle h_{\bar{a},ii}

\displaystyle:=(2i{A_{\varepsilon\bar{a}}^{ap}}\tilde{\Phi},n_{\mu,\phi})

\displaystyle h_{,ii}

\displaystyle:=-\kappa_{0}f_{0}(2iD^{\epsilon}_{0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},n_{\mu,\phi})+\kappa_{0}\chi_{0}\partial_{0}(2iD^{\epsilon}_{0}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},n_{\mu,\phi})+f_{\bar{a}}(2iD^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},n_{\mu,\phi})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-\chi_{\bar{a}}\partial_{0}(2iD^{\epsilon}_{\bar{a}}\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap},n_{\mu,\phi})+\kappa_{0}(2i\partial_{0}({A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}})\tilde{\Phi},n_{\mu,\phi})+(2i{A_{\varepsilon 0}^{ap}}\tilde{\Phi},\partial_{0}n_{\mu,\phi})

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad-(2i\partial_{\bar{a}}({A_{\varepsilon\bar{a}}^{ap}})\tilde{\Phi},n_{\mu,\phi})-(2i{A_{\varepsilon\bar{a}}^{ap}}\tilde{\Phi},\partial_{\bar{a}}n_{\mu,\phi})

Then Lemma
14
and properties of the approximate solution imply that for every
t
,

\displaystyle\|h_{0,ii}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\kappa_{0}\varepsilon\left(\|\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\right)

(5.19)

\displaystyle\|h_{\bar{a},ii}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\|h_{,ii}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}\left(\|\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\right)

\displaystyle+C\varepsilon\|\eta(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\ .

It follows from this and (
5.18
) that conclusions (
5.17
) hold if we define

h=h_{,i}+h_{,ii}\ \ \qquad\mbox{and}\ \ \qquad h_{\alpha}=h_{\alpha,i}+h_{\alpha,ii}\ \ \mbox{ for }\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d.

∎

We now present the

proof of Proposition
5
.

The desired estimate follows directly from Lemma
15
and basic estimates for hyperbolic and parabolic equations, recalled in Section
5.3
below. We first check this in the case
\kappa_{0}>0
. Then, since
c_{\mu}(\cdot,0)=\partial_{t}c_{\mu}(\cdot,0)=0
by assumption,
Lemma
16
implies that for
0<t<T/\varepsilon
,

\displaystyle Q_{\ell}^{\top}(t)

\displaystyle\leq\|c_{\mu}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2)}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{3}\int_{0}^{t}\|h(s,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{2\ell}}\,ds+t\int_{0}^{t}\left(\|h_{0}(s,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{2\ell}}+\sum_{\bar{a}=3}^{d}\|h_{\bar{a}}(s,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{2\ell}}\,\right)ds

\displaystyle+Ct^{2}\|h_{0}(\cdot,0)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}.

Using Lemma
15
to estimate the right-hand side and Proposition (
3
) to express the result in terms of
{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}
yields (
5.7
).

When
\kappa_{0}=0
, the proof is essentially the same, but using Lemma
17
instead of Lemma
16

∎

5.3.
auxiliary linear hyperbolic and parabolic estimates

We have used the following basic estimates in the proof of Proposition
5
above.

Lemma 16
.

Assume that
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{1+n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
solves

\kappa_{0}\partial_{tt}f+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}f-\Delta f=\partial_{0}h_{0}+\cdots+\partial_{n}h_{n}+h

(5.20)

with
\kappa_{0}>0
and
\kappa_{1}\geq 0
,
for
h_{\alpha},h:{\mathbb{R}}^{1+n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that

h\in L^{2}_{t}H^{2\ell},\qquad h_{0}\in H^{1}_{t}H^{2\ell},\qquad h_{j}\in L^{2}_{t}H^{2\ell+1}\quad\mbox{ for }j=1,\ldots,n.

and with initial data
(f,\partial_{t}f)|_{t=0}\in H^{2\ell+1}\times H^{2\ell}
.
Then there exists a constant
C
that may depend on
\kappa_{0}
but is independent of
\kappa_{1}\varepsilon>0
such that

\displaystyle\|f(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq Ct^{3}\int_{0}^{t}\|h(s,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{2\ell}}\,ds+t\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{\alpha=0}^{n}\|h_{\alpha}(s,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{2\ell}}\,ds

(5.21)

\displaystyle+C\|f(\cdot,0)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}+Ct^{2}\left(\|\nabla f(\cdot,0)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}f(\cdot,0)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}+\|h_{0}(\cdot,0)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}\right).

Proof.

It suffices to prove the lemma for
\ell=0
, since the general case then follows by differentiating the equation and applying the
\ell=0
case.

Several times during the proof we will use the fact that for any function
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{1+n}\to{\mathbb{R}}

and
t>0
,

\|f(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq 2\left(\|f(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+t\int_{0}^{t}\|f_{t}(s,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,ds\right).

(5.22)

as long as the right-hand side makes sense and is bounded (that is,
f(\cdot,0)\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
, and
f(\cdot,x)\in H^{1}((0,\infty))
for
a.e.

x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
). Indeed, for almost every
x
we have

\displaystyle f(x,t)^{2}=\left(f(0,x)+\int_{0}^{t}f_{t}(s,x)\,ds\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2f(0,x)^{2}+2\left(\int_{0}^{t}f_{t}(x,s)\,ds\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 2f(0,x)^{2}+2t\int_{0}^{t}f_{t}(x,s)^{2}\,ds.

We obtain (
5.22
) by integrating both sides of this inequality.

We now consider several cases.

Case 1
:
h_{0}=h_{1}=\cdots=h_{n}=h=0
. Then basic estimates (that is, (
5.23
) below, with
h=0
) imply that

\kappa_{0}\|f_{t}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla f(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\kappa_{0}\|f_{t}(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla f(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

for all
t
. We deduce (
5.21
) from this and (
5.22
).

Case 2
:
h_{0}=\cdots h_{n}=0
, and
(f,f_{t})|_{t=0}=0
. Then the equation becomes

\kappa_{0}\partial_{tt}f+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}f-\Delta f=h,\qquad(f,f_{t})|_{t=0}=0.

We recall the standard argument. Basic energy estimates imply that

\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\int f_{t}^{2}\,dx+\frac{d}{dt}\frac{1}{2}\int(\kappa_{0}f_{t}^{2}+|\nabla f|^{2})\,dx=\int hf_{t}\leq\left(\int h^{2}\ dx\ \int f_{t}^{2}\,dx\right)^{1/2}.

(5.23)

Defining
\eta(t):=\sqrt{\int\kappa_{0}f_{t}(\cdot,t)^{2}+|\nabla f(\cdot,t)|^{2}\,dx}
and
H(t):=\|h(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}
, we deduce that

\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\eta^{2}(t)\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}_{0}}\eta(t)H(t)\qquad\mbox{ and thus }\eta^{\prime}(t)\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}_{0}}H(t).

The assumptions of Case 2 imply that
\eta(0)=0
, so

\int(\kappa_{0}f_{t}^{2}+|\nabla f|^{2})\,dx\Big|_{t}=\eta^{2}(t)\leq\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\kappa}_{0}}\int_{0}^{t}\|h(s,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}\,ds\right)^{2}\leq\frac{t}{\kappa_{0}}\int_{0}^{t}\|h(s,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,ds.

(5.24)

Then since we have assumed that
f=0
when
t=0
, we can combine this with (
5.22
) to find that

\|f(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq Ct\int_{0}^{t}s\int_{0}^{s}\|h(r,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,dr\,ds\leq Ct^{3}\int_{0}^{t}\|h(s,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,ds

where the constant depends on
\kappa_{0}
and blows up as
\kappa_{0}\searrow 0
. Thus (
5.21
) holds in this case.

Case 3
: There exists some
j\in\{0,\ldots,n\}
such that
h_{k}=0
if
k\neq j
, and in addition
h=0
, and
(f,f_{t})|_{t=0}=0
.

Then the equation becomes

\kappa_{0}\partial_{tt}f+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}f-\Delta f=\partial_{j}h_{j},\qquad\qquad(f,f_{t})|_{t=0}=0.

(5.25)

where here we do
not
implicitly sum over
j
.

Case 3a
.
j=0
. Then we define

F_{0}(x,t)=\int_{0}^{t}f(s,x)\,ds,\qquad H_{0}(x,t)=\int_{0}^{t}\partial_{t}h_{0}(s,x)\,ds=h_{0}(x,t)-h_{0}(0,x).

Clearly

\kappa_{0}\partial_{tt}F_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}F_{0}-\Delta F_{0}=H_{0},\qquad\qquad(F_{0},\partial_{t}F_{0})|_{t=0}=(0,0).

So we deduce from (
5.24
) that

\displaystyle\|f(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\|\partial_{t}F_{0}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{t}{\kappa_{0}}\int_{0}^{t}\|H_{0}(s,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,ds

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2t}{\kappa_{0}}\int_{0}^{t}\|h_{0}(s,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,ds+\frac{2t^{2}}{\kappa_{0}}\|h_{0}(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

This establishes (
5.21
) in this case.

Case 3b
:
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. We may assume without loss of generality that
j=1
. We may then argue as in Case 3a, defining

F_{1}(t,x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\int_{-\infty}^{x_{1}}f(t,s,x_{2},\ldots x_{n})\,ds,

and noting that
\|f(\cdot,t)|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\|\partial_{1}F_{1}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\|\nabla F_{1}(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}
, with
F_{1}
solving
\partial_{tt}F_{1}-\Delta F_{1}=h_{1}
and
(F_{1},\partial_{t}F_{1})|_{t=0}=(0,0)
.

Since (
5.20
) is linear, the lemma follows from Cases 1, 2 and 3.
∎

Our next lemma establishes a similar estimate to that of Lemma
16
when
\kappa_{0}=0
, that is, in the case of a pure heat flow.

Lemma 17
.

Assume that
f:{\mathbb{R}}^{1+n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
solves

\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}f-\Delta f=\partial_{1}h_{1}+\cdots+\partial_{n}h_{n}+h

(5.26)

with
\kappa_{1}>0
,
for
h_{j},h:{\mathbb{R}}^{1+n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
such that

h\in L^{2}_{t}H^{2\ell},\qquad h_{j}\in L^{2}_{t}H^{2\ell+1}\quad\mbox{ for }j=1,\ldots,n.

and with initial data
f(\cdot,t)\in H^{2\ell}
.
Then there exists a constant
C
that may depend on
\kappa_{1}
but is independent of
\varepsilon>0
, such that

\displaystyle\|f(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{t}{\varepsilon^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\|h(s,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{2\ell}}\,ds+\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\|h_{j}(s,\cdot)\|^{2}_{H^{2\ell}}\,ds

(5.27)

\displaystyle+C\|f(\cdot,0)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}.

Proof.

It suffices to prove the lemma for
\ell=0
, since the general case then follows by differentiating the equation and applying the
\ell=0
case.

We consider several cases.

Case 1
:
h_{1}=\cdots=h_{n}=h=0
. Then basic estimates (that is, (
5.28
) below, with
h=0
) imply that

\|f(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\|f(\cdot,0)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

for all
t
, immediately implying (
5.27
).

Case 2
:
h_{0}=\cdots=h_{n}=0
, and
f|_{t=0}=0
. Then the equation becomes

\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}f-\Delta f=h,\qquad f|_{t=0}=0.

Multiplying the equation by
f
and integrating by parts, we obtain

\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\frac{d}{dt}\int f^{2}\,dx+\int|\nabla f|^{2}\,dx=\int fh\,dx\leq\ \left(\int h^{2}\,dx\int f^{2}\,dx\right)^{1/2}.

(5.28)

Arguing as in Lemma
16
, we find that

\|f(\cdot,t)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\frac{t}{(\varepsilon\kappa_{1})^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\|h(s,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\,ds.

Case 3
: There exists some
j\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
such that
h_{k}=0
if
k\neq j
, and in addition
h=0
, and
f|_{t=0}=0
.

We may assume without loss of generality that
j=1
. Then the equation becomes

\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}f-\Delta f=\partial_{1}h_{1},\qquad\qquad f|_{t=0}=0.

(5.29)

Consider the equation obtained by formally integrating (
5.29
) with respect to the
x_{1}
variable:

\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t}F_{1}-\Delta F_{1}=h_{1},\qquad\qquad\left.F_{1}\right|_{t=0}=0.

This equation is clearly solvable, and
\partial_{1}F_{1}
solves (
5.29
) and hence equals
f
.
We multiply both sides by
\partial_{t}F_{1}
and integrate by parts to find that

\displaystyle\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\int(\partial_{t}F_{1})^{2}\,dx+\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{dt}\int|\nabla F_{1}|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle=\int h_{1}\partial_{t}F_{1}\,dx

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{4\varepsilon\kappa_{1}}\int h_{1}^{2}\,dx+\int\varepsilon\kappa_{1}(\partial_{t}F_{1})^{2}\,dx.

Discarding
\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\int(\partial_{t}F_{1})^{2}\,dx
on both sides and integrating with respect to
t
,
we obtain

\left.\int f^{2}\,dx\right|_{t}\leq\left.\int|\nabla F_{1}|^{2}\,dx\right|_{t}\leq\frac{C}{\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\|h_{1}(s,\cdot)\|^{2}\,ds.

Since (
5.26
) is linear, the lemma follows from Cases 1, 2 and 3.
∎

5.4.
Proof of Proposition
3

We next present the proof of Proposition
3
.
We start with the following basic elliptic estimate.

Lemma 18
.

For every compact
K\subset M_{N}
, there exists
c<C
such that if
q\in K

c\|f\|_{H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}^{2}\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}f(-\Delta f+|\varphi(q)|^{2}f)\leq\|f\|_{H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}^{2}

for every
f\in H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
.

We recall the standard proof.

Proof.

Since
|\varphi(\cdot;q)|\leq 1
everywhere on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
for every
q
, the inequality on the right is obvious.

For the other inequality, since
K
is compact and
|\varphi(x;q)|\to 1
as
|x|\to\infty
for every

q
, we can fix a function
V:{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{R}}
and a number
R>0
such that

V(x)\geq\frac{1}{2}\mbox{ if }|x|\geq R,\qquad 0\leq V(x)\leq|\varphi(x;q)|^{2}\ \ \mbox{ for every $q\in K$ and $x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}$}.

Then it suffices to show that there exists
c>0
such that

c\|f\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}^{2}\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}(|\nabla f|^{2}+Vf^{2})

as the desired inequalities then follow easily.
This can be proved by an argument by contradiction. Indeed, if we fix functions
f_{k}
such that

1=\|f_{k}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\ \geq\ k\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}(|\nabla f_{k}|^{2}+Vf_{k}^{2})\ \geq\ \frac{k}{2}\int_{\{x:|x|\geq R\}}f_{k}^{2}

then it is easy to check that
f_{k}
converges weakly in
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
and strongly in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, after passing to a subsequence, to a nonzero limit, say
f\in H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, supported on
\{x:|x|\leq R\}
. It follows that

0<\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}(|\nabla f|^{2}+Vf^{2})\leq\liminf_{k}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}(|\nabla f_{k}|^{2}+Vf_{k}^{2})=0,

a contradiction.
∎

Remark 7
.

In fact the previous Lemma holds with a constant independent of
q\in M_{N}
. This follows from the above considerations combined with a symmetrization argument (replacing
V
by its radial increasing symmetrization and
f
by its radial decreasing symmetrization), the Hardy-Littlewood and Polya-Szego inequalities, and the observation that for any
q\in M_{N}
,

\frac{1}{8}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}(1-|\varphi(q)|^{2})^{2}\leq E(\mathfrak{u}(q))=\pi N,

and thus
\{x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}:|\varphi(x;q)|^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\}
has measure bounded by some constant
C_{N}
depending only on
N
.

Let
q:{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\to M_{N}
temporarily denote a map satisfying

\mbox{Image}(q)\subset K\mbox{ compact }\subset M_{N},\mbox{ with }\|\partial_{\bar{a}}q\|_{H^{L}}\leq\Theta<\infty

and let

{\mathcal{M}}_{q}\tilde{U}(t,x_{a},x_{\bar{a}})=\begin{pmatrix}(-\Delta_{\bar{a}}+L[q_{\varepsilon}])\tilde{u}\\
(-\Delta+|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2})\tilde{A}_{\bar{a}}\\
(-\Delta+|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2})\tilde{A}_{0}\end{pmatrix},\qquad q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}}):=q(\varepsilon x_{\bar{a}}).

Thus
{\mathcal{M}}_{q}
denotes the operator
{\mathcal{M}}
based around a generic map
q:{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\to M_{N}
, independent of
t
.
Given
\tilde{U}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
of the form

\tilde{U}=\begin{pmatrix}\tilde{u}\\
\tilde{A}_{\bar{a}}\\
\tilde{A}_{0}\end{pmatrix}\qquad\mbox{ with }\qquad\tilde{u}=\binom{\tilde{\Phi}}{\tilde{A}_{a}}

where as usual
a=1,2
and
\bar{a}=3,\ldots,d
, we will write

c_{q,\mu}(x_{\bar{a}})=(\tilde{u}(\cdot,x_{\bar{a}}),n_{\mu}(\cdot;q_{\varepsilon}(x_{\bar{a}}))_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

where
q
now denotes a map as above. We will also write

Q_{q,\ell}^{\perp}(\tilde{U}):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{\ell+1}\tilde{U}\cdot{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{\ell}\tilde{U},\qquad\qquad Q_{q,\ell}^{\top}(\tilde{U}):=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}}\sum_{\mu}|(-\Delta_{\bar{a}})^{\ell}c_{q,\mu}|^{2}

The
\ell=0
case of (
5.5
) is an immediate consequence of the next lemma.

Lemma 19
.

There exist positive constants
c<C
such that for any
\tilde{U}:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
,

c\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}\leq Q_{q,0}^{\perp}(\tilde{U})+Q_{q,0}^{\top}(\tilde{U})\leq C\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}.

Proof.

The inequality on the right is rather clear and we omit the details of its verification.

For the inequality on the left, we start from the definition (
4.12
) of
{\mathcal{M}}_{q}
and write

-\Delta=-\Delta_{\bar{a}}-\Delta_{a}
,
to find that

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\mathcal{M}}_{q}\tilde{U}\cdot\tilde{U}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}}\sum_{\mu}|c_{q,\mu}|^{2}\ =\ \int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}\tilde{U}\cdot(-\Delta_{\bar{a}}\tilde{U})

\displaystyle+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}}\left(\sum_{\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\tilde{A}_{\alpha}(-\Delta_{a}+|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2})\tilde{A}_{\alpha}\,dx_{a}\right)dx_{\bar{a}}

\displaystyle+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\tilde{u}\cdot L[q_{\varepsilon}]\tilde{u}\ dx_{a}\,+\sum_{\mu}|c_{q,\mu}|^{2}\right)dx_{\bar{a}}.

The first integral on the right-hand side becomes
\|\nabla_{\bar{a}}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}
after integrating by parts.
For every
\alpha
, Lemma
18
implies that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\tilde{A}_{\alpha}(-\Delta_{a}+|\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})|^{2})\tilde{A}_{\alpha}\,dx_{a}\right)dx_{\bar{a}}\geq c\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}(|\nabla_{a}\tilde{A}_{\alpha}|^{2}+\tilde{A}_{\alpha}^{2})\,dx_{a}\,dx_{\bar{a}}.

Similarly, Stuart’s coercivity result, Theorem
6
, implies that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}}\left(\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}\tilde{u}\cdot L[q_{\varepsilon}]\tilde{u}dx_{a}\,+\sum_{\mu}|c_{q,\mu}|^{2}\right)dx_{\bar{a}}\geq c\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}(|\nabla_{a}\tilde{u}|^{2}+|\tilde{u}|^{2})\,dx_{a}\,dx_{\bar{a}}.

Adding up these estimates, we obtain

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\mathcal{M}}_{q}\tilde{U}\cdot\tilde{U}+\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}}\sum_{\mu}|c_{q,\mu}|^{2}\geq c\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}|\nabla_{\bar{a}}\tilde{U}|^{2}+|\nabla_{a}\tilde{U}|^{2}+|\tilde{U}|^{2}\ =\ c\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}.

∎

Lemma 20
.

for any
\ell\geq 1
, there exist positive constants
c<C
such that for any
\tilde{U}\in H^{2\ell+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d};{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1})
,

\displaystyle c\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}\leq Q_{q,\ell}^{\perp}(\tilde{U})+Q_{q,\ell}^{\top}(\tilde{U})+\|\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq C\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}.

Proof.

Again we will prove only the estimate on the left, since the other is straightforward.

Define

c_{q,\mu}^{\ell}:=(({\mathcal{M}}_{q,u}^{\ell}(\cdot,x_{\bar{a}})\tilde{u},n_{\mu}(\cdot,x_{\bar{a}}))_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})},

where

{\mathcal{M}}_{q,u}\tilde{u}:=(-\Delta_{\bar{a}}+L[q_{\varepsilon}])\tilde{u}\ =\ \mbox{ the $u$ components of }{\mathcal{M}}_{q}\tilde{U}.

Then applying the previous lemma with
\tilde{U}
replaced by
{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{\ell}\tilde{U}
implies that

c\|{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{\ell}\tilde{U}\|_{H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}\leq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{\ell+1}\tilde{U}\cdot{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{\ell}\tilde{U}+\sum_{\mu}\|c_{q,\mu}^{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(5.30)

To prove the Lemma, it therefore suffices to prove the following two inequalities.
First, there exists
C>0
such that

\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}\leq C\|{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{\ell}\tilde{U}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+C\|\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

(5.31)

and second, for any
\delta>0
and any
\mu=1,\ldots,2N
, there exists
C
such that

\|c_{q,\mu}^{\ell}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq\delta\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}+2\|(-\Delta_{\bar{a}})^{\ell}c_{q,\mu}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C\|\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

(5.32)

Indeed, for a suitably small choice of
\delta
in (
5.32
), these imply that

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{higherest1},\eqref{higherest0}}{\leq}C\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{\ell+1}\tilde{U}\cdot{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{\ell}\tilde{U}+C\sum_{\mu}\|c_{q,\mu}^{\ell}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+C\|\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

\displaystyle\overset{\eqref{higherest2}}{\leq}C\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d}}{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{\ell+1}\tilde{U}\cdot{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{\ell}\tilde{U}+C\sum_{\mu}\|(-\Delta)^{\ell}c_{q,\mu}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+C\|\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}

from which the desired conclusion follows.

Proof of (
5.31
)
:
From the definition (
4.12
) of
{\mathcal{M}}_{q}
, we see that

{\mathcal{M}}_{q}\tilde{U}=-\Delta\tilde{U}+L_{q,1}\tilde{U}

where

L_{q,1}
is a first-order differential operator with smooth coefficients. Thus

{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{\ell}\tilde{U}=(-\Delta)^{\ell}\tilde{U}+L_{q,\ell}\tilde{U}

(5.33)

where
L_{q,\ell}
is a differential operator of order at most
2\ell-1
with smooth coefficients.
Thus

\displaystyle\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|\nabla(-\Delta)^{\ell}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|\nabla{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{\ell}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\nabla L_{q,\ell}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\|{\mathcal{M}}_{q}^{\ell}\tilde{U}\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}+\|\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right).

(5.34)

We now use the fact that for every
\delta>0
there exists
C=C_{\delta}
such that

\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}\leq\delta\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}+C\|\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

We deduce (
5.31
) by inserting this into (
5.34
), choosing
\delta
small enough that
C\delta<1/2
, then rearranging.

Proof of (
5.32
)
:
Recall that for every
q\in M_{N}
,
L[q]
is self-adjoint and
L[q]n_{\mu}=0
. Thus for any
\tilde{u}\in H^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2};{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2})
and any
\mu\in\{1,\ldots,2N\}
,

\displaystyle({\mathcal{M}}_{q,u}\tilde{u},n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

\displaystyle=((-\Delta_{\bar{a}}+L[q_{\varepsilon}])\tilde{u},n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

\displaystyle=(-\Delta_{\bar{a}}\tilde{u},n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

\displaystyle=-\Delta_{\bar{a}}(\tilde{u},n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}+2(\nabla_{\bar{a}}\tilde{u},\nabla_{\bar{a}}n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}+(\tilde{u},\Delta_{\bar{a}}n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}.

Since
\nabla_{\bar{a}}^{k}n_{\mu}=O(\varepsilon^{k})
for every
k
, we deduce that

\left\|({\mathcal{M}}_{q,u}\tilde{u},n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}-(-\Delta_{\bar{a}})(\tilde{u},n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\right\|_{H^{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}^{2}\leq C\epsilon^{2}\|\tilde{u}\|_{H^{k+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}

(5.35)

for every
k
.
Next, we write a telescoping sum

\displaystyle c_{q,\mu}^{\ell}-(-\Delta_{\bar{a}})^{\ell}c_{q,\mu}

\displaystyle=({\mathcal{M}}_{q,u}^{\ell}\tilde{u},n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}-(-\Delta_{\bar{a}})^{\ell}(\tilde{u},n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-1}(-\Delta_{\bar{a}})^{j}\left(({\mathcal{M}}_{q,u}^{\ell-j}\tilde{u},n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}-(-\Delta_{\bar{a}})({\mathcal{M}}_{q,u}^{\ell-j-1}\tilde{u},n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\right)

and apply (
5.35
) to each term in the sum (with
\tilde{u}
replaced by
{\mathcal{M}}_{q,u}^{\ell-j-1}\tilde{u}
) to deduce that

\displaystyle\|c_{q,\mu}^{\ell}-(-\Delta_{\bar{a}})^{\ell}c_{q,\mu}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=0}^{\ell-1}C\varepsilon^{2}\|{\mathcal{M}}_{q,u}^{\ell-j-1}\tilde{u}\|_{H^{2j+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}\ \leq\ C\varepsilon^{2}\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell-1}}.

This immediately implies (
5.32
) if
\varepsilon
is sufficiently small relative to
\delta
. If not, we can argue as at the end of the proof of (
5.31
) to conclude.
∎

We are now ready to present the

Proof of Proposition
3
.

We will only prove the inequality

\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}+\|\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}\leq C{\mathfrak{Q}}(t)

(5.36)

as the other inequality is straightforward, and we will sometimes omit the argument
(\cdot,t)
.

For
t\in[0,T/\varepsilon)
and we
q_{\varepsilon}(t)
to denote the map
x_{\bar{a}}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\to q(\varepsilon x_{\bar{a}},\varepsilon t)
where
q
is the given solution of (
1
). Thus we can write

Q_{\ell}^{\perp}(t)=Q_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),\ell}^{\perp}(\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)),\qquad Q_{\ell}^{\top}(t)=Q_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),\ell}^{\top}(\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)).

It follows directly from Lemmas
19
and
20
that

\|\tilde{U}(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}\leq C\left(Q_{\ell}^{\perp}(t)+Q_{\ell}^{\top}(t)+Q_{0}^{\perp}(t)+Q_{0}^{\top}(t)\right)

(5.37)

We will also write
{\mathcal{M}}_{q_{\varepsilon}(t)}^{\ell}=(-\Delta)^{\ell}+L_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),\ell}
where
L_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),\ell}
is a differential operator of order at most
2\ell-1
with coefficients depending on
q_{\varepsilon}
.
With this notation,

\displaystyle\partial_{t}({\mathcal{M}}_{q_{\varepsilon}(t)}^{\ell}\tilde{U})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}(-\Delta)^{\ell}\tilde{U}+\partial_{t}(L_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),\ell}\tilde{U})

\displaystyle=\partial_{t}(-\Delta)^{\ell}\tilde{U}+(\partial_{t}L_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),\ell})\tilde{U}+L_{q_{\varepsilon}(t),\ell}\partial_{t}\tilde{U}.

It follows that

\|(-\Delta)^{\ell}\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}^{2}\leq C\left(\|\partial_{t}{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell-1}}^{2}+\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell-1}}^{2}\right).

We combine this with the standard inequalities

\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}\leq\|(-\Delta)^{\ell}\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+C\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}

and, for any
\delta>0
,

\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell-1}}^{2}\leq\delta\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}+C_{\delta}\|\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}.

Choosing
\delta>0
small enough and rearranging, we deduce that

\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}\leq C\left(\|\partial_{t}{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell-1}}^{2}\right).

Thus

\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}+\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}\leq C\kappa_{0}\left(\|\partial_{t}{\mathcal{M}}^{\ell}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\right)+C\|\tilde{U}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}.

The conclusion (
5.36
) follows by combining this with (
5.37
) and recalling the definition (
5.4
) of
{\mathfrak{Q}}_{\ell}(t)
.
∎

6.
proof of Theorems
1
and
2

In this section we prove our main theorems. We first restate Theorem
1
.

Theorem 1, full version
.

Let
q:{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times(0,T)\to M_{N}
be a solution of (
1.8
) satisfying (
1.9
) and (
1.10
) for
L=2d+11
,
and assume that
\Theta<\infty
, where
\Theta
is defined in (
1.11
).

Let
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}:(0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
denote the approximate solution constructed in Proposition
1
.

Then there exist constants
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and
0<c_{0}<c_{1}0
such that, setting
\ell=\lfloor d/4\rfloor+2
, if
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
and
(\tilde{U}^{0}_{0},\tilde{U}^{0}_{1})\in H^{2l+1}\times H^{2l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d};{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1})
satisfies

\|\tilde{U}^{0}_{0}\|_{H^{2l+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\tilde{U}^{0}_{1}\|_{H^{2l}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}<c_{0}\varepsilon^{2}

(6.1)

as well as the compatibility and gauge conditions appearing in (
4.10
) as well as the orthogonality condition appearing in (
5.3
),
then there exists a map
\tilde{U}:(0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1}
such that

U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}\mbox{ solves \eqref{dAHM}},\quad\qquad\ (\tilde{U},\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}\tilde{U})_{t=0}=(\tilde{U}^{0}_{0},\kappa_{0}\tilde{U}^{0}_{1})

and

\|\tilde{U}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}H^{2\ell+1}}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{0}\tilde{U}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}H^{2\ell}}<c_{1}\varepsilon^{2}.

(6.2)

The solution
U_{\varepsilon}:=U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}
of (
1
) thereby constructed satisfies

\displaystyle\|\Phi_{\varepsilon}-\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}(\Phi_{\varepsilon}-\varphi(q_{\varepsilon}))\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2},

(6.3)

\displaystyle\|A_{\varepsilon a}-\mathcal{A}_{a}(q_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}(A_{\varepsilon a}-\mathcal{A}_{a}(q_{\varepsilon}))\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}

\displaystyle\|A_{\varepsilon\alpha}-\chi_{\partial_{\alpha}q_{\varepsilon}}(q_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}(A_{\varepsilon\alpha}-\chi_{\partial_{\alpha}q_{\varepsilon}}(q_{\varepsilon}))\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}

\displaystyle\leq C\varepsilon^{2}

for
a=1,2
and
\alpha=0,3,\ldots,d
.

Remark 8
.

It is easy to see that there exist initial data satisfying conditions (
6.1
), (
4.10
) and (
5.3
).

Indeed, if
\kappa_{0}=0
we can simply take
\tilde{U}^{0}_{0}=0
.

In the hyperbolic case
\kappa_{0}>0
, we must prescribe
\tilde{U}^{0}_{0}
and
\tilde{U}^{0}_{1}
. We can choose

\tilde{\Phi}^{0}_{0}=\tilde{\Phi}^{0}_{1}=0,\qquad\tilde{A}^{0}_{i}=\partial_{0}\tilde{A}^{0}_{i}=0\ \ \mbox{ for }i=1,\ldots,d.

(6.4)

Then the requirement that
S_{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}]=0
forces us to choose
\tilde{A}_{0}
satisfying

0=S_{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}]\Big|_{t=0}=(-\Delta+|\Phi_{\varepsilon}^{ap}(\cdot,0)|^{2})\tilde{A}^{0}_{0}+S_{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\cdot,0).

(6.5)

We fix
\partial_{0}\tilde{A}_{0}|_{t=0}=\tilde{A}^{1}_{0}
by specifying that

G[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\tilde{U})\Big|_{t=0}\ =\ \kappa_{0}\tilde{A}^{1}_{0}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\tilde{A}^{0}_{0}=0.

(6.6)

Next, recall that condition (
5.3
) states that
c_{\mu}=\kappa_{0}\partial_{t}c_{\mu}=0
when
t=0
, where

c_{\mu}(x_{\bar{a}},t)=(\tilde{u},n_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}_{(x_{\bar{a}},t)})}

This is clear for the above choice of initial data, which implies that
\tilde{u}=\kappa_{0}\partial_{t}\tilde{u}=0
at
t=0
.

Finally(
6.4
), (
6.5
), our estimate (
4.2
) of
\|S_{0}[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}
, and elliptic estimates imply that

\|\tilde{U}^{0}_{0}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}=\|\tilde{A}_{0}^{0}\|_{H^{2\ell+1}}^{2}\leq C\|S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\cdot,0)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}\leq C\varepsilon^{9}

and then we see from (
6.6
) that

\kappa_{0}\|\tilde{U}^{0}_{1}\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}=\kappa_{0}\|\tilde{A}^{0}_{1}\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}=\frac{(\varepsilon\kappa_{1})^{2}}{\kappa_{0}}\|\tilde{A}^{0}_{0}\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}\leq C\varepsilon^{11}.

Proof.

For
\kappa_{0}\geq 0
let
X_{\kappa_{0}}
denote the space

X_{\kappa_{0}}:=\begin{cases}C^{0}([0,T_{0}/\varepsilon);H^{2\ell+1})&\mbox{ if }\kappa_{0}=0\\
C^{0}([0,T_{0}/\varepsilon);H^{2\ell+1})\cap C^{1}([0,T_{0}/\varepsilon);H^{2\ell})&\mbox{ if }\kappa_{0}>0\end{cases}

where
H^{s}
denotes
H^{s}({\mathbb{R}}^{d};{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{d+1})
.
This is a Banach space with the norm
\vvvert\,\cdot\,\vvvert_{2\ell}
defined in (
4.8
):

\vvvert U\vvvert_{2\ell}:=\sup_{0<t<T_{0}/\varepsilon}\left(\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}U(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\|U(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\right).

For
\tilde{V}\in X_{\kappa_{0}}
we define
F(\tilde{V}):=\tilde{U}
, where

\tilde{U}
solves the modified system (
4.9
), with
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{U})
replaced by
{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{V})
, that is:

(\kappa_{0}\partial_{tt}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t})\tilde{U}+{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{U}+P\tilde{U}=-S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}]-{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{V}),

with initial data
(\tilde{U},\kappa_{0}\partial_{t}\tilde{U})|_{t=0}=(\tilde{U}^{0}_{0},\kappa_{0}\tilde{U}^{0}_{1})
.
It follows from Theorem
7
and our assumed bounds (
6.1
) on the initial data that
F
maps
X_{\kappa_{0}}
to
X_{\kappa_{0}}
, with the estimate

\displaystyle\vvvert F(\tilde{V})\vvvert_{2\ell}^{2}=\vvvert\tilde{U}\vvvert_{2\ell}^{2}\leq Cc_{0}^{2}\varepsilon^{4}+\frac{C}{\varepsilon^{4}}\sup_{0<t<T_{0}/\varepsilon}\|S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\cdot,t)+{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{V})(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}.

Recall from (
4.2
) that

\sup_{0<t<T_{0}/\varepsilon}\|S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}](\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}\leq C\varepsilon^{9}.

Putting these together and recalling (
4.15
), we find that

\vvvert F(\tilde{V})\vvvert_{2\ell}^{2}\leq Cc_{0}^{2}\varepsilon^{4}+C\varepsilon^{5}+C\varepsilon^{-4}\vvvert\tilde{V}\vvvert_{2\ell}^{4}

if
\vvvert\tilde{V}\vvvert_{2\ell}\leq 1
.

It follows that there
c_{0},c_{1},\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that if we define

{\mathcal{B}}:=\{\tilde{U}\in X_{\kappa_{0}}:\vvvert\tilde{U}\vvvert_{2\ell}\leq c_{1}\varepsilon^{2}\}

then
F
maps
{\mathcal{B}}
into
{\mathcal{B}}
whenever
0<\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
.

We claim that in addition,
F:{\mathcal{B}}\to{\mathcal{B}}
is a contraction mapping with respect to the natural
\vvvert\,\cdot\,\vvvert_{2\ell}
norm, and after possibly shrinking
c_{0},c_{1},\varepsilon_{0}
. To verify this, we fix

\tilde{V}_{1},\tilde{V}_{2}\in{\mathcal{B}}
, and let
\tilde{W}:=F(\tilde{V}_{1})-F(\tilde{V}_{2})
.
Then
\tilde{W}
solves

(\kappa_{0}\partial_{tt}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{t})\tilde{W}+{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{W}+P\tilde{W}={\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{V}_{2})-{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{V}),

with
\tilde{W}(\cdot,0)=0
and (if
\kappa_{0}>0
)
\partial_{t}\tilde{W}(\cdot,0)=0
. So Theorem
7
implies that

\vvvert\tilde{W}\vvvert_{2\ell}^{2}\leq C\varepsilon^{-4}\sup_{0<t<T_{0}/\varepsilon}\|{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{V}_{2})(\cdot,t)-{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{V})(\cdot,t)\|_{H^{2\ell}}^{2}.

Appealing to (
4.15
), we deduce that

\vvvert\tilde{W}\vvvert_{2\ell}\leq C\varepsilon^{-4}(\vvvert\tilde{V}_{1}\vvvert_{2\ell}+\vvvert\tilde{V}_{1}\vvvert_{2\ell})^{2}\vvvert\tilde{V}_{1}-\tilde{V}_{2}\vvvert_{2\ell}^{2}

Since
\tilde{V}_{1},\tilde{V}_{2}\in{\mathcal{B}}
, we conclude that (after possibly shrinking
c_{1}
, then adjusting
c_{0},\varepsilon_{0}
as necessary)

\vvvert F(\tilde{V}_{1})-F(\tilde{V}_{2})\vvvert_{2\ell}^{2}=\vvvert\tilde{W}\vvvert_{2\ell}^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}\vvvert\tilde{V}_{1}-\tilde{V}_{2}\vvvert_{2\ell}^{2}.

Thus the Contraction Mapping Principle implies that there is a unique
\tilde{U}\in{\mathcal{B}}
such that
F(\tilde{U})=\tilde{U}
. This says exactly that
\tilde{U}
solves (
4.9
) with the required initial data, and hence that
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}
solves (
1
), with

\vvvert\tilde{U}\vvvert_{2\ell}\leq c_{0}\varepsilon^{2}
, which is exactly (
6.2
).

Finally, to prove (
6.3
), recall that the approximate solution

U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}=U_{m,\varepsilon}
has the form
U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}=U^{\varepsilon}_{0}+\varepsilon^{2}U^{\varepsilon}_{m}
, where
U^{\varepsilon}_{0}
is defined in (
3.2
), so the actual solution
U=U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}+\tilde{U}
satisfies

\|U-U^{\varepsilon}_{0}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq\varepsilon^{2}\|U^{\varepsilon}_{m}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\|\tilde{U}\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}.

(6.7)

We have chosen
\ell
such that
2\ell+1>\frac{d}{2}+2
, so
H^{2\ell+1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})\hookrightarrow W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
, and (
6.3
) follows from (
4.4
) and (
6.2
). The estimates of
\|\partial_{t}(U-U^{\varepsilon}_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}}W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}
follow in exactly the same way.
∎

Theorem
2
has a full version, exactly along the same lines as Theorem
1
above. We now present its proof.

Proof of Theorem
2
.

The present theorem differs from Theorem
1
in two ways.

First, we consider (
1.15
) on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R}\times(0,T)
, and similarly we study (
1
) on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R/\varepsilon}\times(0,T_{0}/\varepsilon)
. This has essentialy no impact on our arguments.
At various points in the proof of Theorem
1
we use PDE estimates for functions whose spatial domain is
{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}
or
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}
. The introduction of
y_{\bar{a}}
-periodicity in Theorem
2
requires versions of these estimates for which the spatial domain is changed to
{\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R}
or
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R}
.
These hold, with the proofs essentially unchanged from the non-periodic case. (There are some places in which arguments could be simplified in the
y_{\bar{a}}
-periodic setting, as a consequence of the fact that
L^{\infty}\subset L^{2}
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R}
.)

The other new feature compared to Theorem
1
, the extension to the near-critical case
\lambda=1+\iota\varepsilon^{2}
, introduces a few changes. To construct approximate solutions, we start by changing variables as in Section
3.1
. Defining
{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}[{\rm U}]
as in (
3.13
), it remains the case that

{\rm S}^{\varepsilon}={\rm S}^{1}+\varepsilon^{2}{\rm S}^{1}
as in (
3.17
), and
{\rm S}^{0}
does not change at all, see (
3.18
). The nonzero
\iota
appears only in
{\rm S}^{1}
, which becomes

{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}]=\begin{pmatrix}(\kappa_{0}{\rm D}_{0}{\rm D}_{0}+\kappa_{1}{\rm D}_{0}-{\rm D}_{\bar{a}}{\rm D}_{\bar{a}})\Phi+\frac{\iota}{2}(|\Phi|^{2}-1)\Phi\\
(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}+\kappa_{1}){\rm F}_{0a}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}{\rm F}_{\bar{a}a}\\
(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}+\kappa_{1}){\rm F}_{0\bar{a}}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{b}}{\rm F}_{\bar{b}\bar{a}}\\
-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{b}}{\rm F}_{\bar{b}0}\end{pmatrix}.

(6.8)

It follows that
\ell^{1}_{\phi}[{\rm U}]
, see (
3.22
), (
3.23
), changes to

\displaystyle\ell^{1}_{\phi}[{\rm U}](\tilde{\rm U})

\displaystyle=(\kappa_{0}{\rm D}_{A_{0}}{\rm D}_{A_{0}}+\kappa_{1}{\rm D}_{A_{0}}-{\rm D}_{A_{\bar{a}}}{\rm D}_{A_{\bar{a}}})\tilde{\Phi}+\frac{\iota}{2}((|\Phi|^{2}-1)\tilde{\Phi}+2(\Phi,\tilde{\Phi})\Phi)

(6.9)

\displaystyle-(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\tilde{\rm A}_{0}+\kappa_{1}\tilde{\rm A}_{0}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}\tilde{\rm A}_{\bar{a}})i\Phi-2i(\kappa_{0}\tilde{\rm A}_{0}{\rm D}_{0}\Phi-\tilde{\rm A}_{\bar{a}}{\rm D}_{\bar{a}}\Phi).

Neither
\ell^{0}[{\rm U}]
, see (
3.21
), nor the other components of
\ell^{1}[{\rm U}]
are affected.

Lemma
8
is the point at which the proof of Theorem
1
breaks down when
\iota\neq 0
. The lemma establishes estimates for
{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}]
, including in
L^{\infty}_{T}H^{L-1}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
. These are simply not true when
\iota\neq 0
, since
\frac{\iota}{2}(|\Phi|^{2}-1)\Phi
does not decay as
|y_{\bar{a}}|\to\infty
. However, the proof carries over with no change to show that in the
y_{\bar{a}}
-periodic setting, the needed estimate holds:

\|{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}]\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}X^{L-1,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R})}+\kappa_{0}\|\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}{\rm S}^{1}[{\rm U}^{0}]\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}X^{L-2,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{T}}^{d-2}_{R}))}\leq C(\Theta).

(6.10)

for every
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

The necessity of adding a potential to the geometric evolution equation (
1.15
) in the near-critical case arises when we revisit Lemma
9
. Noting that
{\rm U}^{0}_{\phi}=\varphi(q)
,
it is convenient to write

{\rm S}^{1}_{u}[{\rm U}^{0}]={\rm S}^{1}_{u}[{\rm U}^{0}]\Big|_{\iota=0}+\frac{\iota}{2}\begin{pmatrix}(|\varphi(q)|^{2}-1)\varphi(q)\\
0\end{pmatrix}

Thus

({\rm S}^{1}_{u}[{\rm U}^{0}],\tilde{n}_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}=({\rm S}^{1}_{u}[{\rm U}^{0}]\Big|_{\iota=0},\tilde{n}_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}+\frac{\iota}{2}(\begin{pmatrix}(|\varphi(q)|^{2}-1)\varphi(q)\\
0\end{pmatrix},\tilde{n}_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

The content of Lemma
9
is exactly that

({\rm S}^{1}_{u}[{\rm U}^{0}]\Big|_{\iota=0},\tilde{n}_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}=(\kappa_{0}\nabla_{0}\partial_{0}q+\kappa_{1}\partial_{0}q-\nabla_{\bar{a}}\partial_{\bar{a}}q,\partial_{\mu})_{g}.

And writing
V_{0}(q):=\frac{1}{8}(|\varphi(q)|^{2}-1)^{2}
,

\displaystyle\left(\begin{pmatrix}(|\varphi(q)|^{2}-1)\varphi(q)\\
0\end{pmatrix},\tilde{n}_{\mu}\right)_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

\displaystyle=((|\varphi(q)|^{2}-1)\varphi(q),{\mathcal{D}}_{\mu}\varphi(q))_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}

\displaystyle=2\partial_{\mu}V_{0}(q)\ =\ 2(\nabla_{g}V_{0}(q),\partial_{\mu})_{g}

Thus

({\rm S}^{1}_{u}[{\rm U}^{0}],\tilde{n}_{\mu})_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}=(\kappa_{0}\nabla_{0}\partial_{0}q+\kappa_{1}\partial_{0}q-\nabla_{\bar{a}}\partial_{\bar{a}}q+\iota\nabla_{g}V_{0}(q),\partial_{\mu})_{g}.

It follows that the orthogonality condition holds if and only if the modified geometric evolution equation (
1.15
) is satisfied.

The first step in the construction of the approximate solution as carried out in Section (
3.3
) is otherwise unchanged, noting that the algebraic identity in Lemma
1
holds for all values of the coupling constant
\lambda
.

Similarly, the induction argument in Section
3.4
is essentially unchanged. In the proof of Lemma
11
, the new term in

\ell^{1}[{\rm U}]
(compare (
6.9
) and (
3.23
)) leads us to change (
3.64
) to

\ell^{1}_{\phi}[{\rm U}^{0}](c^{m+1}_{\mu}N_{\mu})=[(\kappa_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}+\kappa_{1}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{0}-\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}\textsf{\reflectbox{6}}_{\bar{a}}-\frac{\iota}{2})c^{m+1}_{\mu}]n_{\mu,\phi}+\mbox{Tri}

(6.11)

with modifications to the trilinear term that do not affect its character or the estimate (
3.65
). Similarly the details of the linear operators
\Lambda^{1}_{j}
change in a way that does not fundamentally alter the estimate (
3.60
). Thus Lemma
11
, suitably modified, continues to hold. The rest of the construction of approximate solutions may be carried out exactly as in the final step of the proof of Proposition
1

Having constructed an approximate solution, we proceed to formulate the modified system

(\kappa_{0}\partial_{0}^{2}+\varepsilon\kappa_{1}\partial_{0})\tilde{U}+{\mathcal{M}}\tilde{U}+P\tilde{U}=-{\mathcal{N}}(\tilde{U})-S[U_{\varepsilon}^{ap}],.

where we later decompose
P=P_{1}+P_{2}
.
We impose the same gauge condition (
4.6
) as in the critical case.
Since

S_{\phi}(U)\Big|_{\iota\neq 0}=S_{\phi}(U)\Big|_{\iota=0}+\frac{\varepsilon^{2}\iota}{2}(|\Phi|^{2}-1)\Phi,

the new terms that arise when
\iota\neq 0
show up only in the nonlinear terms
{\mathcal{N}}
, which however continue to satisfy (
4.15
), and in the linear operator
P_{2}
, which becomes a little more complicated but continues to satisfy (
4.14
).

Finally, while the linear estimates in Theorem
7
rely strongly on the explicit form of
P_{1}
, see (
4.13
), the only properties they use of
P_{2}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
are the estimates (
4.14
) and (
4.15
), which as noted remain true when
\iota\neq 0
.

With these ingredients in place, the proof of Theorem
2
can be concluded by exactly the same argument, using the contraction mapping principle, as the proof of Theorem
1
.
∎

7.
reconnection

In this section we discuss vortex filament collisions and reconnection in
d=3

dimensions, leading up to the proof of Theorem
3
. For simplicity we will restrict our discussion to the case of
N=2
filaments, in which our main theorem provides solutions of (
1
) starting from a solution
q:I\times{\mathbb{R}}\to M_{2}
of the geometric evolution equation (
1.8
), where
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
is an interval.

Recall from Theorem
4
that the
M_{2}\cong{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
, with the bijections

\displaystyle(q_{1},q_{2})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\quad\mbox{ the polynomial }p(z;q):=z^{2}+q_{1}z+q_{2}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\quad\mbox{ the roots (repeated according to multiplicity) of $p(\cdot;q)$}

\displaystyle\longleftrightarrow\quad u(\cdot;q)=\binom{\varphi}{\mathcal{A}}\in M_{2}\mbox{ such that }|\varphi(z;q)|\approx\frac{|p(z;q)|}{\sqrt{1+|p(z;q)|^{2}}}.

The last condition says that
\varphi(\cdot;q)
vanishes at the precisely same points, and to the same order, as
p(\cdot;q)
.
It is customary to refer to the zeroes of
\varphi(\cdot;s)
,
i.e.
the roots of
p(\cdot;q)
, as the
vortex centers
for
u(\cdot;q)
.

With this in mind, given a map
q:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\cong M_{2}
and
U^{0}:{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
as appearing in the leading-order term in our approximate solution, that is,

U^{0}(y_{a},y_{3})=\begin{pmatrix}\mathfrak{u}(y_{a};q(y_{3}))\\
\chi_{q^{\prime}(y_{3})}(y_{a})\end{pmatrix},\qquad\quad\mbox{ with }\quad\mathfrak{u}(y_{a};q(y_{3}))=\binom{\varphi}{\mathcal{A}}(y_{a},q(y_{3}))

(7.1)

we define

\mbox{ the {vortex centerlines} for $U^{0}$ are }\ \big\{(z,y_{3})\in{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}:z\mbox{ is a root of }p(\cdot;q(y_{3}))\big\}

This is exactly the zero locus of the Higgs field
\varphi
of the leading-order approximate solution
U^{0}
.

More generally, if
U=(\Phi,A_{a},A_{3}):{\mathbb{R}}^{3}\to{\mathbb{C}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
is a map such that
0
is a regular value of
\Phi
,
and for every
y_{3}
,

\Phi(\cdot,y_{3})\in{\mathcal{E}}_{2},\mbox{ and $\Phi(\cdot,y_{3})$ has exactly two zeros, each of degree $1$}

(7.2)

then we define

\mbox{ the {vortex centerlines} of $U$ are the set }\{y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}:\Phi(y)=0\}.

Under our assumptions, this set consists of two curves, both graphs over the vertical
y_{3}
axis.
We will not need to define vortex centerlines for more general
U
, since the solutions we have constructed above generally satisfy the above hypotheses.

Informally, reconnection is a change in the connectivity of vortex centerlines.
We illustrate this with a couple of examples, before presenting the proof of Theorem
3
.

Example 1
.

Let
q=(q_{1},q_{2}):(0,T)\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\cong M_{2}
be a solution of (
1
) with
\kappa_{0}>0
,
with initial conditions
13
13

13

The assumption that
\kappa_{0}>0
allows us to prescribe the initial velocity in this example.

\displaystyle q|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=(q_{1},q_{2})|_{t=0}=(q^{0}_{1},q^{0}_{2})\in H^{k}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbb{C}}^{2}),

\displaystyle\partial_{y_{0}}q|_{t=0}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{y_{0}}q_{1},\partial_{y_{0}}q_{2})|_{t=0}=(q^{1}_{1},q^{1}_{2})\in H^{k-1}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathbb{C}}^{2}),

such that

\displaystyle q^{0}_{1}(y_{3})=q^{1}_{1}(y_{3})=0\mbox{ for all }y_{3},

\displaystyle q^{0}_{2}(y_{3})=ay_{3},\qquad\qquad q^{1}_{2}(y_{3})=ib\qquad\mbox{ with }a,b>0\qquad\mbox{ for $y_{3}$ near $0$.}

One can check
14
14

14

This is most easily seen in new coordinates
(P,Q):=(q_{1},q_{1}^{2}-4q_{2})
on the moduli space. Then the vortex centers corresponding to
(P,Q)
are the points
\frac{1}{2}(P\pm\sqrt{Q})
. It follows from the invariance of the Abelian Higgs action with respect to translations on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
that the geometry of
M_{2}
is invariant with respect to translations
(P,Q)\mapsto(P+v,Q)
and moreover, see
[
25
]
, that in these coordinates the metric takes the form

g=(dP_{1}^{2}+dP_{2}^{2})+f(Q)(dQ_{1}^{2}+dQ_{2}^{2})
. Using this to compute the Christoffel symbols, it follows that when
q
is written in these coordinates, the
P
and
Q
components decouple, and the
P
components solve the linear equation

(\kappa_{0}\partial_{y_{0}}^{2}+\kappa_{1}\partial_{y_{0}}-\partial_{y_{3}}^{2})P=0

with initial conditions
(P,P_{t})=(0,0)
.
This implies the claim.

that the initial conditions for
q_{1}
imply that
q_{1}(t,y_{3})=0
everywhere.
We can also solve the equation backwards in time to obtain a wave map
q:(-T,T)\times{\mathbb{R}}\to M_{2}
.
By a Taylor expansion around
t=0
, we find that
this solution satisfies

q_{1}(t,y_{3})=0,\qquad q_{2}(t,y_{3})=ay_{3}+ibt+O(|t|^{2}+|y_{3}|^{2})

and if
(y_{3},t)
is small then

\Im(q_{2}(y_{3},t))<0\mbox{ if }t<0,\qquad\qquad\Im(q_{2}(y_{3},t))>0\mbox{ if }t>0,

(7.3)

Thus
q_{2}(\cdot,t)
is qualitatively as pictured in Figure
2
below for small
t
, shown for concreteness with
a=b=1
. The plots show the curve in the modlui space for
-0.1<y_{3}<0.1
.

Figure 2
.
Curves in the moduli space for different values of
t
: On left,
t=-0.002
; center,
t=0
; right,
t=0.002
.

Since
q_{1}=0
, the roots of the
p(z;q(t,y_{3}))=z^{2}+q_{2}(t,y_{3})
are just
\pm\sqrt{-q_{2}(t,y_{3})}
. Thus each curve in the moduli space yields a pair of vortex curves in physical space, as illustrated in Figure
3
below

Figure 3
.
Vortex curves for the leading-order approximate solution
U^{0}
, see (
7.1
),corresponding to the moduli space curves shown above: On left,
t=-0.002
; center,
t=0
; right,
t=0.002
.

Note that in this example, if
\delta>0
fixed and
0<t\ll\delta
, then
q_{2}(t,-\delta)
is very near the negative real axis, and

q_{2}(t,\delta)
is very near the positive real axis. Thus, the corresponding vortex centers
\pm\sqrt{-q_{2}}
are close to the real and imaginary axes respectively. The vortex curves connect the centers at
y_{3}=-\delta
to those at
y_{3}=\delta
, and the way this connection occurs changes at
t=0
. This reflects the change of sign (
7.3
) of the imaginary part of the moduli space curve at
t=0
: if
\Im(q_{2}(t,y_{3}))<0
, then
\sqrt{-q_{2}(t,y_{3})}
lies in the first or third quadrant, and if
\Im(q_{2}(t,y_{3}))>0
, then
\sqrt{-q_{2}(t,y_{3})}
lies in the second or fourth quadrant.

The curves shown in Figure
3
are the projections onto the horizontal space of the 3d vortex centerlines as defined above. The corresponding vortex centertlines are pictured in Figure
4
below.

Informally, this change in connectivity is what is meant by “reconnection”. Although the map
q
will not be exactly as pictured, the occurence of reconnection in this example is a consequence only of (
7.3
), which holds for the
q
.

Figure 4
.
3d vortex centerlines for
U^{0}
corresponding to the moduli space curves shown above: On left,
t=-0.002
; center,
t=0
; right,
t=0.002
.

In the above example, for
|t|
small we can (arbitrarily) choose to say that
z_{1}(\cdot,t)
is the vortex centerline (that is, the branch of
\sqrt{-q_{2}(\cdot,t)}
) starting with negative real part
at
(t,-\delta)
. If
\delta
is small enough, this is well-defined for all
0<|t|\leq\delta
and
|y_{3}|<\delta
, and

\lim_{t\nearrow 0}z_{1}(t,y_{3})\neq\lim_{t\searrow 0}z_{1}(t,y_{3})\quad\mbox{ for }y_{3}>0.

(7.4)

Indeed, it is clear from Figure
3
that these limits lie on the negative and positive imaginary axes, respectively. Property (
7.4
) is characteristic of reconnection at time
t_{0}
and height
y_{3}
.

It is convenient to reformulate condition (
7.4
). For small
\delta>0
, assume that

•

I=[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]
is an interval, and

\gamma:I\to\{(t,y_{3}):|t|\leq\delta,|y_{3}|\leq\delta\}
is continuous

•

\gamma(\tau)\neq(0,0)
for
\tau\in I
, and

•

z_{1}
is a root of
p(\cdot;q(\gamma(\tau_{0})))

Then there is a unique continuous map
\zeta[\gamma,z_{1}]:I\to{\mathbb{C}}
such that

\zeta[\gamma,z_{1}](\tau_{0})=z_{1},\qquad\mbox{and }\quad\zeta[\gamma,z_{1}](\tau)\mbox{ is a root of $p(\cdot;q(\gamma(\tau)))$ for all $\tau\in I$}.

(7.5)

In fact,
\zeta[\gamma,z_{1}](\tau)
is just the continuous branch of
\sqrt{-q_{2}(\gamma(\tau))}
that starts at
z_{1}
.

Moreover, (
7.4
) is equivalent to the condition that for the closed loop
\gamma
pictured in Figure
5
, starting and ending at the point
(0,y_{3})
with
y_{3}>0
,

z_{1}=\zeta[\gamma,z_{1}](\tau_{0})\neq\zeta[\gamma,z_{1}](\tau_{1}).

(7.6)

when
\varepsilon
(indicated in the figure) is small enough.

Figure 5.
The curve
\gamma(\tau),\tau_{1}\leq\tau\leq\tau_{2}
in (
7.6
), starting at the marked spot at
\tau=\tau_{1}
and ending at the same spot at
\tau=\tau_{2}
.

Note also that if
\tilde{\gamma}:\tilde{I}\to M_{2}
is any other path that starts and ends at
\gamma(\tau_{0})
and circles the origin once in a clockwise direction,
then

\zeta[\gamma,z_{1}](\tau_{0})-\zeta[\gamma,z_{1}](\tau_{1})=\zeta[\tilde{\gamma},z_{1}](\tilde{\tau}_{0})-\zeta[\tilde{\gamma},z_{1}](\tilde{\tau}_{1})\neq 0.

Thus, the existence of a loop
\gamma
satisfying (
7.6
) may be taken as a sufficient to guarantee reconnection, regardless of whether the loop has the specific form shown in Figure
5
.

Example 2
.

Let
q=(q_{1},q_{2}):{\mathbb{R}}\times(0,T)\to{\mathbb{C}}^{2}\cong M_{2}
solve (
1.8
) and define

D:(-T,T)\times{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
by
D=q_{1}^{2}-4q_{2}
. Thus
D(t,y_{3})
is the discriminant of
p(\cdot;q(t,y_{3}))
,
and

\mbox{the roots of $p(\cdot;q(t,y_{3}))$ are }\ \frac{1}{2}\left(-q_{1}(t,y_{3})\pm\sqrt{D(t,y_{3})}\right)

Now assume the
q
there is some
(\bar{x}_{3},\bar{t})\in{\mathbb{R}}\times(0,T)
such that

D(\bar{x}_{3},\bar{t})=0,\qquad\{\partial_{t}D(\bar{x}_{3},\bar{t}),\partial_{y_{3}}D(\bar{x}_{3},\bar{t})\}\mbox{ are linearly independent over ${\mathbb{R}}$}.

(7.7)

Then by the implicit function theorem,
D
is a diffeomorphism from an open neighborhood
W
of
(\bar{x}_{3},\bar{t})\in{\mathbb{R}}\times(0,T)\times{\mathbb{R}}

onto a neighborhood of
0\in{\mathbb{C}}
. We may assume that
W
is an open ball. Let
\gamma:I\to W
be a closed loop that encircles
(\bar{x}_{3},\bar{t})
once, with
\gamma(\tau_{0})=\gamma(\tau_{1})
. For example, we can take
\gamma(\tau)=(r\cos\tau,r\sin\tau)
for
\tau\in[0,2\pi]
and
r
sufficiently small. We can also take a loop of the form shown in Figure
5
.

Fixing a root
z_{1}
of
p(\cdot;q(\gamma(\tau_{0})))
, there is a unique map
\zeta=\zeta[\gamma,z_{1}]:I\to{\mathbb{C}}
satisfying (
7.5
), that is, a continuous branch of
\tau\mapsto-q_{1}(\gamma(\tau))+\sqrt{D(\gamma(\tau)})
such that
\zeta[\gamma,\tau_{0}]=z_{1}
. Since
D(\gamma(\tau))
circles the origin exactly once, it is clear that
the chosen branch of
\sqrt{D(\gamma(\tau)}
changes sign as
\tau
increases from
\tau_{0}
to
\tau_{1}
, and hence that (
7.6
) holds.

Thus (
7.7
) is sufficient to guarantee reconnection of the vortex centerlines of the associated leading-order approximate solution
{\rm U}^{0}
.

We now present the

proof of Theorem
3
.

Let
I=[\tau_{0},\tau_{1}]
be an interval, and
\gamma:I\to{\mathbb{R}}\times(0,T_{0})
be continuous, and let

\gamma_{\varepsilon}(\tau)=\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\gamma(\tau)
. We remark that the definitions imply that
q_{\varepsilon}(\gamma_{\varepsilon}(\tau))=q(\gamma(\tau))
for all
\tau
.

Assume that
\zeta_{1,\varepsilon}
is a zero of
\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\gamma_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{0}))
. We will then write

\zeta_{\varepsilon}[\gamma_{\varepsilon};z_{1,\varepsilon}]:I\to{\mathbb{C}}
to denote a continuous map, if it exists,
such that
\zeta_{\varepsilon}[\gamma_{\varepsilon};z_{1,\varepsilon}](\tau_{0})=z_{1,\varepsilon}
and

\zeta_{\varepsilon}[\gamma_{\varepsilon};z_{1,\varepsilon}](\tau)\mbox{ is a zero of }\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\cdot;\gamma_{\varepsilon}(\tau)).

Thus,
\zeta_{\varepsilon}[\gamma_{\varepsilon};z_{1,\varepsilon}]
may be described as a lifting of
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
to the vortex centers of
U_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\gamma_{\varepsilon})
.

We will prove that for any
\delta>0
there is a continuous

\gamma:I=[\tau_{0},\tau_{0}]\to\{(x_{3},t):|x_{3}-\bar{x}_{3}|<\delta,|t-\bar{t}|<\delta\}

such that
\gamma(\tau_{0})=\gamma(\tau_{1})
, and for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon>0
and each zero
z_{1,\varepsilon}
of
\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\cdot,\gamma_{\varepsilon}(\tau_{0}))
,
there is a
unique
continuous lifting
\zeta_{\varepsilon}[\gamma_{\varepsilon};z_{1,\varepsilon}]
, and moreover

\zeta_{\varepsilon}[\gamma_{\varepsilon};z_{1,\varepsilon}](\tau_{0})\neq\zeta_{\varepsilon}[\gamma;z_{1,\varepsilon}](\tau_{1}).

(7.8)

As discussed in the examples above, this guarantees reconnection of the vortex filaments of
\Phi_{\varepsilon}
.

Our starting point is Example 2.
Given
\delta>0
, we require that the set
W
from Example
2
be contained in

\{(x_{3},t):|x_{3}-\bar{x}_{3}|<\delta,\ \ |t-\bar{t}|<\delta,t<T_{0}\}.

As in that example, we let
\gamma:I\to W
be a path that encircles
(\bar{x}_{3},\bar{t})
once, and we let

\zeta[\gamma,z_{1}]:I\to{\mathbb{C}}
be the unique continuous map satisfying (
7.5
).
We also define

r:=\frac{1}{3}\min_{\tau\in I}\sqrt{|q_{2}(\tau)|}>0,

so that for every
\tau\in I
, the distance between the two roots of
p(\cdot;q(\gamma(\tau)))
, equivalently the two zeros
of
\varphi(\cdot;q(\gamma(\tau)))
, is at least
3r
. By choosing
r
smaller if necessary and appealing to compactness and the explicit description of
\varphi(q)
provided by Theorem
4
, we can arrange that
\varphi(z;q(\gamma(\tau)))
is uniformly nondegenerate in

\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:\mbox{dist}(z,\mbox{roots of }p(\cdot,q(\gamma(\tau)))\}\leq r

in the sense that if we view
\varphi
as a map from
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, there is some positive constant
c
such that
\det D\varphi(z;q(\gamma(\tau)))\geq c
for
z
in the above set, for all
\tau\in I
.

Since we know from Theorem
1
that

\|\Phi_{\varepsilon}-\varphi(q_{\varepsilon})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{2,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{t}(\Phi_{\varepsilon}-\varphi(q_{\varepsilon}))\|_{L^{\infty}_{T_{0}/\varepsilon}W^{1,\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C\varepsilon^{2},

it is easy to see that for all sufficiently small
\varepsilon
and for every
\tau
,
\Phi_{\varepsilon}(\cdot;q(\gamma_{\varepsilon}(\tau)))
has a unique zero, say
\zeta_{\varepsilon}[\gamma_{\varepsilon};z_{1,\varepsilon}](\tau)
such that

|\zeta_{\varepsilon}[\gamma_{\varepsilon};z_{1,\varepsilon}](\tau)-\zeta[\gamma;z_{1}](\tau)|<r.

(7.9)

with
z_{1,\varepsilon}=\zeta_{\varepsilon}[\gamma_{\varepsilon};z_{1,\varepsilon}](\tau_{0})
, and that the path defined in this way is continuous.
In addition, (
7.9
) implies that

|\zeta_{\varepsilon}[\gamma_{\varepsilon};z_{1,\varepsilon}](\tau_{1})-\zeta_{\varepsilon}[\gamma_{\varepsilon};z_{1,\varepsilon}](\tau_{0})|\geq|\zeta[\gamma_{\varepsilon};z_{1}](\tau_{1})-\zeta[\gamma_{\varepsilon};z_{1}](\tau_{0})|-2r.

(7.10)

And because
\gamma(\tau_{1})=\gamma(\tau_{0})
, we see that
\zeta[\gamma;z_{1}](\tau_{0}),\zeta[\gamma;z_{1}](\tau_{1})
are just the two roots of
p(\cdot,q(\gamma(\tau_{0})))
, and hence are at least distance
3r
apart. Thus (
7.8
) follows from (
7.10
)
∎

Appendix A
Some linear estimates

A.1.
A scalar elliptic PDE

In this subsection we consider the PDE

(-\Delta_{a}+|\varphi(q)|^{2})\psi=\eta\qquad\mbox{ on }{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times(0,T).

(A.1)

As in the proof of Proposition
1
, where the results we prove here are used, we will write points in
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times(0,T)
as
y=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{d},y_{0})=(y_{a},y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})

where
y_{a}=(y_{1},y_{2})
and
y_{\bar{a}}=(y_{3},\ldots,y_{d})
.
We use the notation

\Delta_{a}=\partial_{a}\partial_{a}=\partial_{y_{1}}^{2}+\partial_{y_{2}}^{2}.

We assume that
q:{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times(0,T)\to M_{N}
is a map such that

\mbox{Image}(q)\subset K\mbox{ compact }\subset M_{N}.

(A.2)

and

\Theta_{q}:=\|\partial_{\bar{a}}q(\cdot,y_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{L}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{0}q(\cdot,y_{0})\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}H^{L}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}<\infty

(A.3)

for some
L>d/2
.
We write
\varphi(q)
to denote either

\varphi(q):{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times(0,T)\to{\mathbb{C}}\quad\mbox{ defined by }\varphi(q)(y)=\varphi(y_{a};q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))

as in (
A.1
), or
\varphi(q):{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{C}}
for a fixed
q\in M_{N}
, as
in Lemma
21
below. What we have in mind should always be clear from the context.

Note that (
A.1
) may be understood as a
d-1
-parameter family of equations on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
:

(-\Delta_{a}+|\varphi(\cdot;q(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0}))|^{2})\psi(\cdot,y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})=\eta(\cdot,y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})\qquad\mbox{on ${\mathbb{R}}^{2}$ }

for every
(y_{\bar{a}},y_{0})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d-2}\times(0,T)
. It may also be viewed as an extremely degenerate elliptic equation on
{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times(0,T)
.

We recall that the space
X^{k,j}_{\gamma}
appearing in the statement below is
defined as
H^{k}\cap W^{k-j,\infty}_{\gamma}
, see (
1.21
), (
1.22
). This space appears naturally in our construction of an approximate solution.

Proposition 6
.

Fix
j,k
such that
\frac{d}{2}<j+1\leq k\leq L
, and assume that

\eta:{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times(0,T)\to{\mathbb{R}}
satisfies

\Theta_{\eta}:=\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}X^{k,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{0}\eta\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}X^{k-1,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d-2})}<\infty

(A.4)

for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

Then (
A.1
) has a unique solution
\psi
, and there exists a constant
C
, depending on
K,\Theta_{q}
and
\gamma
, such that

\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}X^{k,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}+\kappa_{0}\|\partial_{0}\psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{T}X^{k-1,j}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})}\leq C\Theta_{\eta}.

(A.5)

We start with a 2d lemma.

Lemma 21
.

For compact
K\subset M_{N}
, given
q\in K
and
\eta\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, the equation

(-\Delta+|\varphi(q)|^{2})\psi=\eta

(A.6)

has a unique solution
\psi\in H^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, and there exists a constant
C=C(K)<\infty

such that

\|\psi\|_{H^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\leq C\|\eta\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}.

(A.7)

In addition, if

\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}:=\operatorname{ess\,sup_{y_{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}}(|\eta(y_{a})a|e^{\gamma|y_{a}|})<\infty

for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
, then

\psi\in L^{\infty}_{\gamma}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
, and

\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}_{\gamma}}\leq C\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}_{\gamma}},\qquad C=C(K,\gamma).

(A.8)

We sketch the rather standard proof for the reader’s convenience:

Proof.

Existence of a unique weak solution
\psi\in H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
follows directly from Lemma
18
, via standard arguments using the Riesz representation theorem. It also follows from Lemma
18
that

\|\psi\|_{H^{1}}\leq C\|\eta\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}.

The (
A.7
) follows by standard elliptic regularity.

Finally, assume that
\eta\in W^{k,\infty}_{\gamma}
for some
\gamma\in(0,1)
. Then there exists some
A>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
such that

|\eta(y_{a})|\leq Ae^{-\gamma|y_{a}|}\qquad\mbox{ for all }y_{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}.

(A.9)

The embedding
H^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})\hookrightarrow C_{0}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})
implies that
\psi(y_{a})\to 0
as
|y_{a}|\to\infty
,with

\|\psi\|_{L^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\leq C\|\psi\|_{H^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\leq C\|\eta\|_{L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{2})}\overset{\eqref{eta.decay}}{\leq}CA

(A.10)

for
C=C(K,\gamma)
.
Exponential decay of
\psi

|\psi(y_{a})|\leq C(K,\gamma)Ae^{-\gamma|y_{a}|}\qquad\mbox{ for all }y_{a}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}.

(A.11)

is then proved by a comparison principle argument using the auxiliary function

s(y_{a})=C(K,\gamma)Ae^{-\gamma|y_{a}|}

which, one can check, for suitable choices of
C(K,\gamma)
and
R(K,\gamma)
satisfies

(-\Delta_{a}+|\varphi(q)|^{2})(s\pm u)(y_{a})>0\qquad\mbox{ for }|y_{a}|\geq R(K,\gamma).

This uses assumption (
A.9
) as well as the exponential decay of
(1-|\varphi(q)|)
, see (
2.8
). A detailed presentation of this argument can be found in
[
9
]
, Proposition 29. Then (
A.11
) is a consequence of this fact and (
